τ d \tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}} -tilting theory for linear Nakayama algebras
Endre S. Rundsveen ∗, Laertis Vaso

Abstract
. Support τ \tau -tilting pairs, functorially finite torsion classes and 2 2 -term silting complexes are three much studied concepts in the representation theory of finite-dimensional algebras, which moreover turn out to be connected via work of Adachi, Iyama and Reiten. We investigate their higher-dimensional analogues via τ d \tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}} -rigid pairs, d d -torsion classes and ( d + 1 ) (d+1) -term silting complexes as well as the connections between these three concepts. Our work is done in the setting of truncated linear Nakayama algebras Λ ⁡ ( n , l ) = 𝐤 ​ 𝔸 n / Rad ​ ( 𝐤 ​ 𝔸 n ) l \Lambda(n,l)=\mathbf{k}\mathbb{A}_{n}/\mathrm{Rad}(\mathbf{k}\mathbb{A}_{n})^{l} admitting a d d -cluster tilting module. More specifically, we classify τ d \tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}} -rigid pairs ( M , P ) (M,P) of Λ ⁡ ( n , l ) \Lambda(n,l) with | M | + | P | = n \lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=n via an explicit combinatorial description and show that they can be characterized by a certain maximality condition as well as by giving rise to a ( d + 1 ) (d+1) -term silting complex in K b ​ ( proj ​ Λ ​ ( n , l ) ) \mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda(n,l)) . We also describe all d d -torsion classes of Λ ⁡ ( n , l ) \Lambda(n,l) . Finally, we compare our results to the classical case d = 1 d=1 and investigate mutation with a special emphasis on the case where d = gl . dim . ( Λ ) d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda) .



[name=definitions, columns=2, title=List of definitions]
[name=symbols, columns=2, title=List of symbols, options=-s index_style.ist]

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
16G20,16S90,18E10,18G99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Historical background and motivation

Tilting theory came into being through a series of papers in the mid to late 1900’s
[
20
,
16
,
12
,
18
,
22
]
; for details see the survey
[
6
]
. It soon found itself safely in the toolboxes of both representation theorists in particular and algebraists in general. Concurrently and intertwined with the birth of tilting theory, Auslander–Reiten theory was also developed
[
11
,
13
,
14
]
, providing yet another tool in the study of finite-dimensional algebras. This article lies in the intersection of certain generalizations of these two theories that we now describe.

Tilting theory.

Recall that a
partial tilting
module
T
is a rigid module with projective dimension 1 or less, and if in addition
\lvert T\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
, then
T
is called a
tilting module
. Bongartz proved that any partial tilting module can be completed to a tilting module
[
17
]
. Later it was shown that for an
almost complete
tilting module
T
(i.e. a partial tilting module with
\lvert T\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert-1
), there are at most two ways of completing it
[
35
,
37
]
. The interest in the number of completions arose from the fact that two completions of the same almost complete tilting module relate to each other through a natural short exact sequence.

If
\Lambda
is hereditary, then there are exactly two ways to complete an almost complete tilting module
T
if and only if
T
is sincere
[
24
]
. To remove the assumption on
T
being sincere, the notion of
support tilting
modules was introduced
[
36
,
26
]
. A support tilting module
T
is a partial tilting module which is tilting over
\m\Lambda/\langle e\rangle
for some idempotent
e\in\Lambda
. Over hereditary algebras this was a sufficient generalization, i.e. almost complete support tilting modules of hereditary algebras can be completed in two different ways.

\tau
-tilting theory.

In 2014, Adachi, Iyama and Reiten
[
2
]
proposed a broader generalization of tilting modules to remove the assumption on the algebra being hereditary. They introduced the notion of
\tau
-rigid
pairs defined as a module
M\in\m\Lambda
and a projective module
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau M\right)=0
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,M\right)=0
where
\tau
is the Auslander–Reiten translation. They next defined a
\tau
-tilting
module
M
to be a
\tau
-rigid module such that
\lvert M\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
, and a
support
\tau
-tilting pair

(M,P)
to be a
\tau
-rigid pair such that
M
is
\tau
-tilting in
\m\Lambda/\langle e\rangle
where
P=e\Lambda
. This generalization was shown to be broad enough for the initial motivation, as well as preserving a close relationship with functorially finite torsion classes which tilting modules had already enjoyed.

Auslander–Reiten theory.

Auslander–Reiten theory, developed by Maurice Auslander and Idun Reiten in the 1970s, is central in the representation theory of finite-dimensional algebras. The theory focuses on the study of almost split sequences and Auslander–Reiten quivers, which encode indecomposable modules and irreducible morphisms between them. An important role is played by the
Auslander–Reiten
translations
\tau
and
\tau^{-}
which act as bijections between the sets of indecomposable non-injective and indecomposable non-projective modules over an algebra.

Higher-dimensional homological algebra.

The generalization of Auslander–Reiten theory takes place in the world of higher-dimensional homological algebra introduced by Iyama
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
. A central role in this theory is played by certain subcategories
\mathcal{C}
of
\m\Lambda
called
d
-cluster tilting
. In such a subcategory one finds a
d
-dimensional version of Auslander–Reiten theory. This leads to a natural generalization of
\tau
-rigidity in
\mathcal{C}
given by replacing
\tau
with the higher Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
.

Motivation.

Many notions from classical representation theory and homological algebra have been lifted and studied in the setting of higher-dimensional homological algebra; a relevant example for us is the notion of
d
-torsion classes introduced by Jørgensen
[
32
,
7
,
10
]
. In such generalizations usually the role of the module category
\m\Lambda
is played by a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
. However, this approach is not fruitful if one tries to generalize support
\tau
-tilting modules in this setting. Indeed, if
\Lambda
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in\Lambda
is an idempotent, then, in general,
\Lambda/\langle e\rangle
does not admit a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory.

Nevertheless, inspired by the maximality properties of
\tau
-tilting modules, several authors have suggested possible generalizations of support
\tau
-tilting modules in higher-dimensional homological algebra
[
30
,
34
,
39
,
33
]
. In a similar spirit, there is work by several authors studying how the relationship between support
\tau
-tilting pairs, functorially finite torsion classes and
2
-silting complexes generalizes in the higher setting
[
10
,
33
,
9
]
.

The above facts can be seen as a motivation for this paper. How do the different types of higher
\tau
-tilting theories found in the literature look like? Is it possible to infer a type of maximality for pairs which have the same amount of indecomposable summands as
\Lambda
, as in the classical case? Inspired by Adachi’s classification of
\tau
-tilting modules of Nakayama algebras
[
1
]
, we chose to investigate these questions over Nakayama algebras.

1.2.
This paper

As mentioned we chose the realm of Nakayama algebras as base camp for our reconnaissance. However, when exploring new land one tries to tackle bite sized hurdles. We focus on acyclic Nakayama algebras bounded by homogeneous relations for two reasons. Firstly, there exists an explicit classification of Nakayama algebras bounded by homogeneous relations which admit
d
-cluster tilting subcategories
[
38
,
19
]
while such a classification is lacking for general Nakayama algebras. Second, acyclic ones were chosen since in this case the
d
-cluster tilting subcategory is unique, in contrast to the cyclic case. The algebras we are working with are therefore bounded quiver algebras of the form

\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}/R^{l}
where
\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
is the quiver given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}\colon\qquad n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n-1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

and
R
is the arrow ideal of
\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
. We denote
\Lambda(n,l)\coloneqq\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}/R^{l}
. The triples
(n,l,d)
for which
\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
can be found in Theorem
2.6
, and the description of
\mathcal{C}
is given in Section
2.4
.

Summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs

We say that a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
is
summand-maximal
if for any other
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(N,Q)
we have
\lvert N\rvert+\lvert Q\rvert\leq\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
. This is the main maximality property of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs which we study. Over an algebra
\Lambda(n,l)
which admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory, we can describe all summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in an explicit combinatorial way encoded in the notion of
well-configured pairs
, see Definition
3.28
. This allows us to prove our first main result:

Theorem A

(Theorem
3.33
)
.

Assume
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. If
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
, then the following are equivalent

(a)

(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair,

(b)

(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
, and

(c)

(M,P)
is well-configured (Definition
3.28
).

Notice that part (b) of Theorem
A
provides an easy way of checking if a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair is summand-maximal, while part (c) provides an easy way of constructing summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

d
-torsion classes

Next we focus on the study of
d
-torsion classes of
\Lambda(n,l)
when there exists a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
. Once again we provide an explicit combinatorial method of constructing
d
-torsion classes in this case through paths in the graphs
G_{1}
(eq.
4.2
) when
l>2
and
G_{2}
(eq.
4.5
) when
l=2
. It turns out that our method constructs all
d
-torsion classes which leads to the following result.

Theorem B

(Theorem
4.19
and Theorem
4.21
)
.

(a)

If
l>2
, then there exists a bijection between the set of paths
\chi
in
G_{1}
of length
p-1
starting at an odd vertex and the set of
d
-torsion classes
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

(b)

If
l=2
, then there exists a bijection between the set of paths
\chi
in
G_{2}
of length
p-1
and the set of
d
-torsion classes
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

The case
l=2
is in fact a very degenerative version of the case
l>2
, but we distinguish between the two cases for technical reasons. The above bijection, the details of which are given in Sections
4.3.1
and
4.3.2
, gives an easily implemented algorithm to construct
d
-torsion classes, and a relatively more cumbersome but still manageable way to check that a given subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
is a
d
-torsion class.

(d+1)
-term silting complexes

Our last main result gives a characterization of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in terms of
(d+1)
-term silting complexes.

Theorem C

(Theorem
5.22
)
.

Assume
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
.
Then
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair if and only if
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\coloneqq P[d]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
is a silting complex in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
.

Comparing the classical and the higher case

At the very end of the paper we take a step back and look at how properties of support
\tau
-tilting modules can be traced to different generalizations. In addition we compare the suggested frameworks in
[
30
]
,
[
39
]
,
[
33
]
and our paper. These observations and comparisons can be roughly summarized in the following diagram

]

]

]

]

where the zigzag arrows trace different generalizations and the arrows marked with an asterix hold for
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
admitting a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. We also present some observations regarding mutation of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs and the lattice of
d
-torsion classes and investigate in more details the special case where
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda(n,l))
.

Another direction of generalization that we haven’t considered in this paper is given through
[
21
]
and
[
40
]
. Rather than working in a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\m\Lambda
, this generalization takes place in the
m
-extended module category

m~-~\m\Lambda
, which is a subcategory of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\m\Lambda)
given by complexes with non-zero homology only in degrees
i
with
i\in[-(m-1),0]
.

1.3.
Structure

The paper consists of six sections. Section 2 sets the stage for the rest of the text by introducing some fundamental notation, recalling a few results and proving some preliminary observations. In Section 3 we further build our toolbox and prove Theorem
A
. In Section 4 we recall some results and notions about
d
-torsion classes before proving Theorem
B
. In Section 5 we recall results about
(d+1)
-silting complexes before proving Theorem
C
. Sections 4 and 5 are independent of each other. Section 6 depends on all preceding sections and acts both as a summary and as a collection of observations. In there we present an overview of our results and explore how they do or do not generalize relevant results from the case
d=1
. Furthermore we present examples and results regarding mutation of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs with special emphasis put on the case
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda(n,l))
. In Section
A
we include an illustration that showcases the most widely used notation in this article. We also include an index of definitions and symbols used in this paper.

We have made a website that we hope can serve as a supplemental tool for the reader during their exploration of the paper. The site can be found at
https://endresr.github.io/Higher_Tau_Nakayama/
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we introduce some notation and recall all the background facts that we need.

2.1.
Notation and conventions

We start with some basic conventions that hold throughout the whole article.

All subcategories in this article will be assumed to be full, additive and closed under isomorphisms, unless otherwise specified.

For a set
A
we denote by
\lvert A\rvert
the cardinality of
A
. If
B
is another set, then we denote by
A\sqcup B
the
disjoint union
of
A
and
B
, that is when we write
X=A\sqcup B
, we mean that
X=A\cup B
and
A\cap B=\varnothing
. For integers
a
and
b
we denote by
[a,b]
the
interval
of all integers between
a
and
b
, that is
[a,b]=\{z\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a\leq z\leq b\}
. The
length
of
[a,b]
is defined to be its cardinality.

Throughout this article we fix a field
\mathbf{k}
. We denote by
n
,
l
and
d
three positive integers with
d\geq 2
, unless stated otherwise. By an algebra we mean a finite-dimensional associative unital algebra over
\mathbf{k}
. If
\Lambda
is an algebra, we denote by
\m\Lambda
the category of finitely-generated right
\Lambda
-modules. We denote by
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}({\Lambda})
respectively
\overline{\mathrm{mod}}(\Lambda)
, the
projectively stable category
respectively
injectively stable category
. We denote by
\tau
and
\tau^{-}
the
Auslander–Reiten translations
of
\Lambda
and we denote by
\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
the
global dimension
of
\Lambda
.

For a module
M\in\m\Lambda
we denote by
\Omega(M)
the
syzygy
of
M
,
that is the kernel of a projective cover of
M
, and by
\Omega^{-}(M)
the
cosyzygy
of
M
, that is the cokernel of an injective envelope of
M
. We denote by
\lvert M\rvert
the number of nonisomorphic indecomposable direct summands of
M
; we usually use the same letter as we used for the module in small font for that, so for example
m=\lvert M\rvert
. We denote by
\ell(M)
the
Loewy length
of
M
. We write
\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
for the
additive closure
of
M
in
\m\Lambda
, that is the smallest subcategory of
\m\Lambda
closed under taking direct sums of direct summands of
M
; we usually use the same letter as we used for the module in script font for that, so for example
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
.

A
quiver

Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
consists of a set
Q_{0}
of
vertices
, a set
Q_{1}
of
arrows
and
source
and
target
maps
s,t:Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
. All quivers in this article are
finite
, that is the sets
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
are finite sets.

If
\Lambda=\mathbf{k}Q/I
is a bound-quiver algebra and
i\in Q_{0}
is a vertex, then we denote by
S(i)
the simple module corresponding to the vertex
i
. We denote by
P(i)
the indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-module with top
S(i)
and by
I(i)
the indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-module with socle
S(i)
. For a subset
J\subseteq Q_{0}
we denote the direct sums

S(J)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in J}S(i),\;\;P(J)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in J}P(i),\;\;I(J)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in J}I(i).

For more details on the representation theory of finite-dimensional algebras, on Auslander–Reiten theory and on representations of quivers with relations we refer to the textbooks
[
15
]
and
[
8
]
.

2.2.
d
-cluster tilting subcategories and
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs

We continue by recalling some central notions of higher-dimensional homological algebra.

For a subcategory
\mathcal{C}
of an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
and an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we call a morphism
f\colon A\to C
a
left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
A
if
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
-\circ f\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(C,-\right)|_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(A,-\right)|_{\mathcal{C}}
is an epimorphism. Further, if every object
A\in\mathcal{A}
admit a left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation, we call
\mathcal{C}

covariantly finite in
\mathcal{A}
. The notions of
right
\mathcal{C}
-approximations
and
contravariantly finite
subcategories are defined dually. A
functorially finite
subcategory is a subcategory which is both covariantly and contravariantly finite.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Following
[
28
]
we recall the concept of a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory.

Definition 2.1
.

A functorially finite subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
is called a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
if

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=\{M\in\m\Lambda\mid\mathrm{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{i}(M,\mathcal{C})=0\text{ for $1\leq i\leq d-1$}\}

\displaystyle=\{M\in\m\Lambda\mid\mathrm{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{i}(\mathcal{C},M)=0\text{ for $1\leq i\leq d-1$}\}.

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{add}\left(C\right)
for some module
C\in\m\Lambda
, then
C
is called a
d
-cluster tilting module
.

For more details on higher-dimensional Auslander–Reiten theory we refer to the survey
[
28
]
.

Notice that any subcategory with an additive generator is functorially finite. Notice also that a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory always contains the projective and the injective
\Lambda
-modules. We define the
d
-Auslander–Reiten translations
as in
[
28
]
by
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}=\tau\Omega^{d-1}
and
\tau_{d}^{-}=\tau^{-}\Omega^{-(d-1)}
. They are crucial in the study of
d
-cluster tilting subcategories.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
28
, Theorem 2.8 and 2.9]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\m\Lambda
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
the corresponding subcategories of
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}({\Lambda})
and
\overline{\mathrm{mod}}(\Lambda)
. Then
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
and
\tau_{d}^{-}
induce mutually quasi-inverse equivalences

\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}\colon\underline{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau_{d}^{-}\colon\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\underline{\mathcal{C}}.

The following definition introduces the main objects of interest in this article. Parts (a) and (b) in it were first introduced in
[
30
]
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\m\Lambda
. Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\m\Lambda
be a projective module. Then

(a)

M
is called
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid
if
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0
.

(b)

The pair
(M,P)
is called a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
if
M
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,M\right)=0
.

(c)

The pair
(M,P)
is called a
summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
, if it is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and for any other
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(N,Q)
we have
\lvert N\rvert+\lvert Q\rvert\leq\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
.

Note that in particular a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
has at least
\lvert\Lambda\rvert
summands, since
(\Lambda,0)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid.

2.3.
Nakayama algebras

We now introduce the main class of algebras which we study in this article.

If
Q
is a quiver, we denote by
R=R_{Q}
the ideal of the path algebra
\mathbf{k}Q
generated by all arrows of
Q
. We denote by
\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
the quiver

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}\colon\qquad n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n-1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

A bound quiver algebra
\Lambda
of the form
\Lambda=\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}/I
is called a
linear Nakayama algebra
and if
I=R^{l}
for some
l\geq 2
, then
\Lambda
is called
homogeneous
.

We denote the bound quiver algebra
\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}/R^{l}
by
\Lambda(n,l)
. The category
\m\Lambda(n,l)
is well-understood. We proceed by recalling a description of the category
\m\Lambda(n,l)
by describing the indecomposable modules and the morphisms between them. For the rest of this section we set
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
.

Indecomposable
\Lambda
-modules are defined uniquely by their support which in turn is given by an interval
[a,b]\subseteq[1,n]
with
b-a\leq l-1
. Thus, we denote by
M(a,b)
the indecomposable
\Lambda
-module with support
[a,b]
. As a representation of
\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
bound by
R^{l}
, this is given by setting
K
in the vertices
i\in Q_{0}
with
a\leq i\leq b
, and by setting all the morphisms corresponding to arrows in
\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
to be the identity if possible and zero otherwise. In particular, we can compute the length of
M(a,b)
as

\ell(M(a,b))=b-a+1.

If
b<a
, or
b<1
or
n<a
then we set
M(a,b)=0
. Morphisms in
\m\Lambda
are described by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
1
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
M(a,b)
and
M(c,d)
be indecomposable
\Lambda
-modules. The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(a,b),M(c,d)\right)\neq 0
.

(b)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(a,b),M(c,d)\right)=\mathbf{k}
.

(c)

b\in[c,d]
and
c\in[a,b]
.

Moreover, if
d-c\leq b-a
, then the following is also equivalent:

(d)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(b),M(c,d)\right)\neq 0
.

In particular, we have that if
M,N\in\m\Lambda
are indecomposable, then there exists a non-zero morphism from
M
to
N
if and only if the top of
M
is a composition factor of
N
and the socle of
N
is a composition factor of
M
. The following lemma classifies indecomposable projective, injective and projective-injective
\Lambda
-modules.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
38
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

Let
k\in[1,n]
be a vertex of
\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{n}}
.

(a)

For the indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-module
P(k)
we have that

P(k)=\begin{cases}M(1,k),&\text{if }1\leq k\leq l,\\
M(k-l+1,k),&\text{if }l+1\leq k\leq n.\end{cases}

(b)

For the indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-module
I(k)
we have that

I(k)=\begin{cases}M(k,k+l-1),&\text{if }1\leq k\leq n-l+1,\\
M(k,n),&\text{if }n-l+2\leq k\leq n.\end{cases}

(c)

An indecomposable
\Lambda
-module
M(a,b)
is both projective and injective if and only if
b-a=l-1
.

2.4.
d
-cluster tilting subcategories for
\Lambda(n,l)

Our motivation for studying the homogeneous Nakayama algebras
\Lambda(n,l)
is that not only their representation theory is well-understood, but also that they provide a non-trivial class of examples where one can apply higher-dimensional Auslander–Reiten theory. In this paragraph we recall these examples through the classification of
d
-cluster tilting subcategories of
\m\Lambda(n,l)
. The following theorem is the main result.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
38
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
. There exists a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
if and only if there exists
p\geq 1
such that

(2.1)

n=(p-1)\left(\frac{d-1}{2}l+1\right)+\frac{l}{2}

and either

(i)

l=2
, or

(ii)

l>2
and
d
and
p
are even

holds. The
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
is then given by

(2.2)

\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{add}\left(\bigoplus_{r=0}^{p-1}\tau_{d}^{-r}(\Lambda)\right).

Remark 2.7
.

If an algebra
A
admits a
d
-cluster tilting module for
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(A)
, then
A
is called
d
-representation-finite
. This case is of special interest in higher-dimensional homological algebra. From
[
38
, Theorem 3]
we have that
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory for
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
if and only if
d=2\tfrac{n-1}{l}
. Using Theorem
2.6
it follows that this is the case if and only if
p=2
.

For the rest of the section we fix integers
n
,
l
and
d
such that there exists a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
, where
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
. The indecomposable modules lying in the subcategory
\mathcal{C}
are crucial in our investigation. As it can be inferred from Theorem
2.6
, they are of two kinds: they are either projective-injective, or they are of the form
\tau_{d}^{-r}(P)
for some indecomposable projective non-injective module
P\in\m\Lambda
. In fact, the modules of the second kind can be grouped with respect to the power
r
. To describe how this works, we need to recall how to apply the functors
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
and
\tau_{d}^{-}
to indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
38
, Lemma 4.8]
)
.

Let
M(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}
be an indecomposable module.

(a)

If
M(a,b)
is nonprojective, then

\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M(a,b))=M(b-\tfrac{d}{2}l,a-\tfrac{d-2}{2}l-2)

and the length of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M(a,b))
satisfies

\ell(\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M(a,b)))=l-\ell(M(a,b)).

(b)

If
M(a,b)
is noninjective, then

\tau_{d}^{-}(M(a,b))=M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l)

and the length of
\tau_{d}^{-}(M(a,b))
satisfies

\ell(\tau_{d}^{-}(M(a,b)))=l-\ell(M(a,b)).

Using (
2.2
) and Lemma
2.8
it is easy to give a description of all modules in the
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. First, by Lemma
2.5
, we have that

P([l,n])=\bigoplus_{i\in[l,n]}P(i)

is the direct sum of all indecomposable projective-injective
\Lambda
-modules. We denote by
\mathcal{PI}
the subcategory
\mathrm{add}\left(P([l,n])\right)
of
\m\Lambda
. Notice that by (
2.2
) we have that
\mathcal{PI}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. Next, for
1\leq i\leq p
we define

(2.3)

s_{i}=\begin{cases}(i-1)\frac{d-1}{2}l+i,&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
(i-1)(\frac{d-1}{2}l+1)+\frac{l}{2},&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

In particular, we immediately have that
s_{1}=1
and, using (
2.1
), we also obtain that
s_{p}=n
. A direct computation using Lemma
2.8
gives that a simple module
S
is in
\mathcal{C}
if and only if
S\cong S(s_{i})
for some
1\leq i\leq p
. Thus the set
\{S(s_{1}),S(s_{2}),\ldots,S(s_{p})\}
is a complete and irredundant collection of representatives of isomorphism classes of simple
\Lambda
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, immediately from the definition of
s_{i}
, we can show the following equalities which we use throughout

(2.4)

s_{i+1}-s_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{d}{2}l,&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
\frac{d-2}{2}l+2,&\mbox{if $i$ is even,}\end{cases}\text{ and }s_{i+2}-s_{i}=(d-1)l+2.

We have seen that the sequence of integers
s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}
can be used to describe the simple
\Lambda
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
, but it can also be used to describe the rest of the indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
, excluding the projective-injective modules which as we have seen are easy to describe. Indeed, we define

(2.5)

D_{i}=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\limits_{x=1}^{l-1}M(s_{i},s_{i}+x-1),&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
\bigoplus\limits_{x=1}^{l-1}M(s_{i}-x+1,s_{i}),&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

We also set
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathrm{add}\left(D_{i}\right)
and we call it
the
i
-th diagonal
; we will soon motivate the name by an example. Notice that by Lemma
2.5
it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is the category of projective non-injective
\Lambda
-modules and that
\mathcal{D}_{p}
is the category of injective non-projective
\Lambda
-modules. Another direct computation using Lemma
2.8
yields that
\tau_{d}^{-}(\mathcal{D}_{i})=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq p-1
. Using (
2.2
), we can conclude that

(2.6)

\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{add}\left(\left(\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}D_{i}\right)\oplus P([1,n])\right).

The following example is indicative of the general picture.

Example 2.9
.

The algebra
\Lambda(9,3)=\mathbf{k}\overset{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{A}}_{9}}/R^{3}
admits a
2
-cluster subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Below we have drawn the Auslander–Reiten quiver of this algebra, where the indecomposable modules are given by their composition series. The indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
are the boxed vertices in the Auslander–Reiten quiver.

The relative position of the vertices carries all the essential information of the associated indecomposables. Hence in the following we omit the arrows and use circles for indecomposable modules and filled circles for modules in
d
-cluster tilting subcategories. With this convention, the above picture simplifies to

where we have also drawn the action of
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
using dashed arrows. The projective-injective indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
correspond to the top row of filled circles. The other eight indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
form four diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
,
\mathcal{D}_{3}
,
\mathcal{D}_{4}
, read from left to right. For example, we have that
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathrm{add}\left(S(1)\oplus P(2)\right)
and
\mathcal{D}_{4}=\mathrm{add}\left(S(9)\oplus I(8)\right)
.

Let
1\leq x\leq l-1
. For later convenience, we denote by
D_{i}^{\uparrow}(x)
the direct sum of all indecomposable direct summands of
D_{i}
which have length larger than or equal to
x
. Similarly, we denote by
D_{i}^{\downarrow}(x)
the direct sum of all indecomposable summands of
D_{i}
which have length smaller than or equal to
x
, and we also denote
D_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)=0
. We also set
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(x)=\mathrm{add}\left(D_{i}^{\uparrow}(x)\right)
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(x)=\mathrm{add}\left(D_{i}^{\downarrow}(x)\right)
.

Remark 2.10
.

(a)

Let
1\leq i\leq p
. If
i
is odd, then all indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
have the same socle
S(s_{i})
. Similarly, if
i
is even, then all indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
have the same top
S(s_{i})
.

(b)

For
2\leq i\leq p
we have
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(t))=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}^{\downarrow}(l-t)
and
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(t))=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}^{\uparrow}(l-t)
.

If
M
is a module in
\mathcal{C}
, then computing
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)
is all that is needed for checking if
M
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid. On the other hand, if
M
is in
\mathcal{C}
, then we have seen that each of its indecomposable summands either is projective-injective or lies in some diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Hence it becomes important to compute homomorphisms between indecomposable modules lying in the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. First we notice that when indecomposable modules lie in the same diagonal, then the only thing that determines the existence of a morphism between them is the length of the modules.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
1\leq i\leq p
and
M,N\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
be indecomposable.

(a)

If
i
is even, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,N\right)\neq 0
if and only if
\ell(N)\leq\ell(M)
,

(b)

If
i
is odd, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,N\right)\neq 0
if and only if
\ell(M)\leq\ell(N)
.

Proof.

Follows immediately by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Things are generally simpler when indecomposable modules lie in different diagonals. In this case, there are no nonzero morphisms between them, with one notable exception which is recorded in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq p
with
i\neq j
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{j}\right)\neq 0
.

(b)

d=2
and
l>3
and
i
is odd and
j=i+1
.

Proof.

Assume first that (b) holds and we show (a). Since
l>3
we have

s_{i}<s_{i}+2\leq s_{i}+l-2<s_{i}+l,

where
s_{i}
is defined in (
2.3
). By Lemma
2.4
we obtain that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-2),M(s_{i}+2,s_{i}+l)\right)\neq 0.

Clearly the indecomposable module
M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-2)
is in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and it is enough to show that
M(s_{i}+2,s_{i}+l)\in\mathcal{D}_{j}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. Since
i
is odd and
d=2
and using (
2.4
) we have
s_{i}=s_{i+1}-l
. Hence
M(s_{i}+2,s_{i}+l)=M(s_{i+1}-l+2,s_{i+1})\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
, as required.

Assume now that (a) holds and we show (b). By (a) we have that there exist indecomposable modules
M(a,b)\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
M(c,d)\in\mathcal{D}_{j}
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(a,b),M(c,d)\right)\neq 0.

By (
2.5
) we have that
b=s_{i}+x-1
and
c=s_{j}-y+1
for some
x,y\in[1,l-1]
. By Lemma
2.4
we have that

(2.7)

a\leq c\leq b\leq d.

Since
\Lambda
is representation-directed and since diagonals do not intersect we have that
j\geq i
, and by the assumption
i\neq j
we obtain that
j\geq i+1
.

We first claim that if
1\leq m\leq j
holds for some
m
odd, then
c\geq s_{m}
. Indeed, if
j=m
then
j
is odd and so
c=s_{j}=s_{m}
. On the other hand, if
j\geq m+1
, then
s_{j}\geq s_{m+1}
and using (
2.4
) we obtain

c=s_{j}-y+1\geq s_{m+1}-y+1=\frac{d}{2}l+s_{m}-y+1\geq l+s_{m}-(l-1)+1>s_{m}.

Assume now towards a contradiction that
i
is even. Then
b=s_{i}
and
i+1
is odd. Since
j\geq i+1
, the claim gives that
c\geq s_{i+1}
. Then (
2.7
) gives
s_{i+1}\leq c\leq b=s_{i}
which is a contradiction. Hence
i
is odd.

Assume now towards a contradiction that
j\geq i+2
. Since
i+2
is odd, the claim gives that
c\geq s_{i+2}
. Then (
2.7
) gives
s_{i+2}\leq c\leq b=s_{i}
which is a contradiction. Hence
j=i+1
.

Since
i
is odd and
j=i+1
, we obtain using (
2.4
) that
c=s_{i+1}-y+1=s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l-y+1
. Then, using (
2.7
) we have

s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l-y+1\leq s_{i}+x-1

which we can rewrite as
\tfrac{d}{2}l\leq x+y-2
. Since
x,y\in[1,l-1]
, we obtain that
\tfrac{d}{2}l\leq 2l-4
. Rearranging this once more we obtain that

8\leq(4-d)l.

If
l=2
, then this inequality gives
0\leq-2d
which is impossible. If
l=3
, then this inequality gives
4\geq 3d
which is again impossible since
d\geq 2
. Hence
l>3
. Then
d
must be even by Theorem
2.6
. But if
d\geq 4
then the above inequality is again not satisfied. Hence
d=2
.
∎

3.
Summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs for
\Lambda(n,l)

Aim.

In this section we fix an algebra
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
that satisfies the conditions of Theorem
2.6
, so that it admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Our aim is to investigate the structure of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs for
\Lambda
. Before we proceed, let us outline the basic steps of our strategy.

Describing
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

A basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
can be described using the indecomposable summands of
M
and
P
. By definition, all of these indecomposable summands lie in
\mathcal{C}
. On the other hand, using (
2.6
), we have that we can separate the indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{C}
in two different collections: the indecomposable projectives and the modules in the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{p}
. Each indecomposable projectives can be described uniquely using a number from
1,\ldots,n
. Each indecomposable module in the diagonals can also be described uniquely by specifying in which diagonal it lies and its length. Thus to describe a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
we need a subset
R
of
[1,n]
corresponding to the indecomposable projective summands of
M
, a subset
B
of
[1,n]
corresponding to the indecomposable (neccessarily projective) summands of
P
and then a collection of indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{p}
corresponding to the non-projective summands of
M
.

We first introduce some notation to be able to describe our
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs using this point of view. The first part in our strategy is to answer the following question: if we have a collection of combinatorial data as described in the previous paragraph, under what additional conditions does it define a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair? In this way we characterize basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in a purely combinatorial way. This is the aim of Section
3.1
.

Local restrictions on summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

The next part of our strategy is to observe that the combinatorial data described above has some important local properties. More specifically, assume that a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
is supported in some consecutive diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
. Then we define an appropriate interval
\Xi\subseteq[1,n]
which lies “close” to the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
. We may then count the amount of indecomposable summands of
(M,P)
that lie in the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
and add to that number the amount of indecomposable projective summands of
(M,P)
that are of the form
P(k)
for some
k\in\Xi
. It turns out that this number of indecomposable summands of
(M,P)
“in the neighbourhood of”
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
cannot exceed
\lvert\Xi\rvert
. In fact, with a few crucial exceptions, it cannot even exceed
\lvert\Xi\rvert-1
. These observations are made precise in Section
3.2
.

The main aim of Section
3.3
is to study how this local constraint relates to summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. It turns out that the local gap described above cannot be patched by adding indecomposable summands outside of this local area. Together with some more obvious observations, we see that this property forces summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs to be supported on at most two consecutive diagonals (again, with one exception). Then we only need to check what kind of configurations are possible when one restricts to at most two consecutive diagonals. We provide a list that describes such configurations in Definition
3.16
.

Gluing the local configurations to classify summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

With that done, the last part of the strategy in classifying summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs is to find how the local admissible configurations can be glued together so that the end result is not only a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair but is also summand-maximal. This is the aim of Section
3.4
. This leads immediately to the notion of well-configured pairs, introduced in Definition
3.28
, which completely characterizes summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in
\m\Lambda
in an explicit combinatorial way.

Another important result of this characterization is that summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs are also characterized by a simple counting argument: the number of indecomposable summands of
M\oplus P
is equal to
\lvert\Lambda\rvert=n
if and only if
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. As this condition is significantly easier to check, we highlight this in examples even before showing its equivalence to the property of being a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

The following section is quite heavy on notation. We have tried to give many examples and use conventions that make it easier to remember them, but the interested reader is advised to make use of the index and the table at the end of the article to get helpful reminders.

A special case.

As it can be inferred from the explanation of our strategy, there are a few exceptions that need to be dealt separately. The following remark encapsulates the different cases and the essential reason for their differences.

Remark 3.1
.

If
d>2
or
l\leq 3
, then Lemma
2.12
gives that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{j}\right)=0
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq p
. However, if
d=2
and
l>3
, then this property does not hold. This makes the study of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs for
d=2
and
l>3
require some more attention. This explains the need for considering this special case more closely in the sequel.

3.1.
Basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

The main aim of this section is to give a combinatorial characterization of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in
\m\Lambda
. To be able to do this, we introduce some notation that lets us describe pairs
(M,P)
with
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P
projective.

Definition 3.2
.

A
\mathcal{C}
-pair
is a pair
(M,P)
where
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
are modules such that
M\oplus P
is basic.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Using the description of
\mathcal{C}
in (
2.6
), we may assume that indecomposable summands of
M
and
P
are indecomposable summands of
P([1,n])\oplus\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}D_{i}
. Then we may write

M=M_{\text{pr}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i}

where
M_{\text{pr}}\in\mathrm{add}\left(P[1,n]\right)
and where for
i\in[2,p]
we let
M_{i}
be the direct sum of indecomposable summands of
M
in the diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. In particular we have that indecomposable summands of
\bigoplus\limits_{i=2}^{p}M_{i}
are never projective. For
i\not\in[2,p]
we set
M_{i}=0
. We denote
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
,
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\mathrm{add}\left(M_{i}\right)
,
m_{i}=\lvert M_{i}\rvert
and
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}\left(P\right)
. Moreover, we have
M_{\text{pr}}\in\mathrm{add}\left(P[1,n]\right)
and
P\in\mathrm{add}\left(P[1,n]\right)
and so there exist unique sets
R,B\subseteq[1,n]
such that
M_{\text{pr}}=P(R)
and
P=P(B)
. We call
(R,B)
the
non-diagonal component
of
M
. In pictures and examples we use Red colour and dashed lines when denoting an indecomposable module which is a summand of
M
and we use Blue colour and bold lines when denoting an indecomposable projective module which is a summand of
P(B)
. Notice finally that since
M\oplus P
is basic, we obtain that
R\cap B=\varnothing
, which we use freely in the sequel.

Clearly, if
(M,P)
is a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, it is also a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Given a
\mathcal{C}
-pair
(M,P)
, our next aim is to give necessary and sufficient conditions on the data
R
,
B
,
M_{2},\ldots,M_{p}
for
(M,P)
to be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. The following is the first step towards that direction.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),P(R)\right)=0
.

(b)

For every
x\in B
and
y\in R
such that
1\leq x<y\leq n
, there exists
z\in[1,n]
with
x<z<\cdots<z+l-2<y
such that
(R\cup B)\cap[z,z+l-2]=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Assume first that (b) holds but (a) fails and we reach a contradiction. By (a) there exist
x\in B
and
y\in R
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(x),P(y)\right)\neq 0
. In particular, if
P(x)=M(a,x)
and
P(y)=M(b,y)
for some
a
and
b
, then
a\leq b\leq x\leq y
by Lemma
2.4
. Since
R\cap B=\varnothing
, we have
x<y
. By part (b) we conclude that
y-b\geq y-x\geq l
, which contradicts
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
.

Assume now that (a) holds and we show that (b) also holds. Let
y\in R
. If there exists
x<y
, without loss of generality, we may assume that
x
is maximal such that
x<y
and
x\in B
. Then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(x),P(x+i)\right)\neq 0
for
1\leq i\leq l-1
by Lemma
2.4
. Since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),P(R)\right)=0
, we have that
x+i\not\in R
for
1\leq i\leq l-1
. Since
x<y
and
y\in R
, we conclude that
x+l-1<y
. By maximality of
x
, we have that
(x+i)\not\in B
for
1\leq i\leq l-1
. The claim follows by setting
z=x+1
.
∎

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair and let
i\in[2,p]
. Assume that
M_{i}\neq 0
. Recall that
M_{i}
is the direct sum of all indecomposable summands of
M
in the diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Since the diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
has exactly one indecomposable module of each length
1,\ldots,l-1
(see also (
2.5
)) we obtain that there exists a unique set
X_{i}\subseteq[1,l-1]
such that

(3.1)

M_{i}=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\limits_{x\in X_{i}}M(s_{i},s_{i}+x-1),&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
\bigoplus\limits_{x\in X_{i}}M(s_{i}-x+1,s_{i}),&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

In particular, an indecomposable summand of
D_{i}
is a direct summand of
M_{i}
if and only if it is of length
x\in X_{i}
. We set

(3.2)

l_{i}=\begin{cases}\min{X_{i}},&\mbox{if $M_{i}\neq 0$,}\\
l,&\mbox{if $M_{i}=0$}\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad n_{i}=\begin{cases}\max{X_{i}},&\mbox{if $M_{i}\neq 0$,}\\
0,&\mbox{if $M_{i}=0$.}\end{cases}

The definition of
l_{i}
and
n_{i}
in the case
M_{i}=0
may seem a bit arbitrary but it is motivated by the fact that it allows us to state many results without assuming that
M_{i}\neq 0
. The following observation follows immediately by the definitions.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair and let
i\in[2,p]
. Then
1\leq l_{i}\leq l
and
0\leq n_{i}\leq l-1
. Moreover,
m_{i}\neq 0
if and only if
m_{i}\leq n_{i}-l_{i}+1
and
1\leq l_{i}\leq n_{i}\leq l-1
both hold.

The next lemma is our second step towards the main result of this paragraph.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair and
i\in[2,p]
.

(a1)

If
i
is odd, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i-1},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i})\right)=0
if and only if
n_{i-1}+n_{i}\leq l-1
.

(a2)

If
i
is odd, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i+1})\right)=0
if and only if
l+1\leq l_{i}+l_{i+1}
.

(b1)

If
i
is even, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i+1})\right)=0
if and only if
n_{i}+n_{i+1}\leq l-1
.

(b2)

If
i
is even, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i-1},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i})\right)=0
if and only if
l+1\leq l_{i-1}+l_{i}
.

(c1)

If
i
is odd and
d=2
, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i+2})\right)=0
if and only if
n_{i}\leq l_{i+2}+1
.

(c2)

If
i
is odd and
d=2
, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i-2},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i})\right)=0
if and only if
n_{i-2}\leq l_{i}+1
.

Proof.

(a1)

If
M_{i-1}=0
respectively
M_{i}=0
, then
n_{i-1}=0
respectively
n_{i}=0
. Since both
n_{i}\leq l-1
and
n_{i-1}\leq l-1
hold by Lemma
3.4
, the claim follows.

Now assume that
M_{i-1}\neq 0
and
M_{i}\neq 0
. We have that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i-1},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i})\right)=0

if and only if

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(L,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0\text{ for all indecomposable }L\in\mathcal{M}_{i-1}\text{ and }N\in\mathcal{M}_{i}.

By Lemma
2.11
(a), and since
i-1
is even, this is equivalent to

\ell(\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N))>\ell(L)\text{ for all indecomposable }L\in\mathcal{M}_{i-1}\text{ and }N\in\mathcal{M}_{i}.

Using
\ell(\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N))=l-\ell(N)
from Lemma
2.8
(a), we obtain that this is equivalent to

\ell(L)+\ell(N)\leq l-1\text{ for all indecomposable }L\in\mathcal{M}_{i-1}\text{ and }N\in\mathcal{M}_{i}.

Let
L^{\prime}=M(s_{i-1}-n_{i-1}+1,s_{i-1})\in\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
and
N^{\prime}=M(s_{i},s_{i}+n_{i}-1)\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Since
\ell(L)\leq\ell(L^{\prime})=n_{i-1}
for all indecomposable
L\in\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
and
\ell(N)\leq\ell(N^{\prime})=n_{i}
for all indecomposable
N\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
, the above is equivalent to
n_{i-1}+n_{i}\leq l-1
, as required.

(a2)

Similar to part (a1).

(b1)

Follows immediately by applying part (a1) to
i+1
.

(b2)

Follows immediately by applying part (a2) to
i-1
.

(c1)

If
M_{i}=0
respectively
M_{i+2}=0
then
n_{i}=0
respectively
l_{i+2}=l
. Since both
n_{i}\leq l-1
and
1\leq l_{i+2}
hold by Lemma
3.4
, the claim follows.

Now assume that
M_{i}\neq 0
and
M_{i+2}\neq 0
. Then an indecomposable module in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is of the form
L_{x}=M(s_{i},s_{i}+x-1)
and an indecomposable module in
\mathcal{M}_{i+2}
is of the form
N_{y}=M(s_{i+2},s_{i+2}+y-1)
. We compute using Lemma
2.8
(a) that

\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N_{y})=M(s_{i+2}+y-1-l,s_{i+2}-2)\overset{(\ref{eq:difference of consecutive simples})}{=}M(s_{i}+y+1,s_{i}+l).

We have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M_{i},\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i+2})\right)=0
if and only if

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(L,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0\text{ for all indecomposable }L\in\mathcal{M}_{i}\text{ and }N\in\mathcal{M}_{i+2}.

By the above computation, this is equivalent to

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(s_{i},s_{i}+x-1),M(s_{i}+y+1,s_{i}+l)\right)=0\text{ for all $x$ and $y$ such that }L_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}\text{ and }N_{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{i+2}.

By Lemma
2.4
we obtain that this is equivalent to
s_{i}+x-1\not\in[s_{i}+y+1,s_{i}+l]
or
s_{i}+y+1\not\in[s_{i},s_{i}+x-1]
. Since
x\leq l-1
and
1\leq y
, both of the above cases are equivalent to

x\leq y+1\text{ for all $x$ and $y$ such that }L_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}\text{ and }N_{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{i+2}.

Since
L_{n_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
N_{l_{i+2}}\in\mathcal{M}_{i+2}
and also
x\leq n_{i}
and
l_{i+2}\leq y
for all
x
and
y
such that
L_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
N_{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{i+2}
, the above is equivalent to
n_{i}\leq l_{i+2}+1
, as required.

(c2)

Follows immediately by applying part (c1) to
i-2
. ∎

Before giving the third and last step towards the main result, we need to introduce some more notation. Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. For
i\in[2,p]
we define

(3.3)

B_{i}^{\times}\coloneqq\begin{cases}[s_{i},s_{i}+n_{i}-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i}-n_{i}+1,s_{i}],&\mbox{if $i$ is even,}\end{cases}\text{ and }R_{i}^{\times}\coloneqq\begin{cases}[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i-1}],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1},s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i})-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

Note that by definition we have that
\lvert R_{i}^{\times}\rvert=l-l_{i}
and
\lvert B_{i}^{\times}\rvert=n_{i}
; we use these two equalities freely throughout. The aim of the intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
respectively
B_{i}^{\times}
is to encode indecomposable projective modules which can not be in
R
respectively
B
, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
and
i\in[2,p]
.

(a)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),M_{i}\right)=0
if and only if
B\cap B_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
.

(b)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(R),\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M_{i})\right)=0
if and only if
R\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

(a)

If
M_{i}=0
, then
B_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
and the claim follows trivially. Assume that
M_{i}\neq 0
. Let
M(a,b)\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
be the indecomposable summand of
M_{i}
of largest length. Then
[a,b]=B_{i}^{\times}
by the definitions involved, that is (
3.1
) and (
3.2
). Then we have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),M_{i}\right)=0
if and only if
B\cap[a,b]=\varnothing
if and only if
B\cap B_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
, as required.

(b)

Similar to part (a) by considering the module
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M(c,d))
where
M(c,d)
is the indecomposable summand of
M_{i}
of smallest length. ∎

With this in mind we can give our main result for this paragraph.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Then
(M,P)
is a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair if and only if the following conditions all hold.

(a)

For every
x\in B
and
y\in R
such that
1\leq x<y\leq n
, there exists
z\in[1,n]
with
x<z<\cdots<z+l-2<y
such that
(R\cup B)\cap[z,z+l-2]=\varnothing
.

(b)

For all
i\in[2,p]
we have that

(b1)

if
i
is odd, then
n_{i-1}+n_{i}\leq l-1
and
l+1\leq l_{i}+l_{i+1}
hold. If moreover
d=2
, then also
n_{i}\leq l_{i+2}+1
and
n_{i-2}\leq l_{i}+1
hold,

(b2)

if
i
is even, then
n_{i}+n_{i+1}\leq l-1
and
l+1\leq l_{i-1}+l_{i}
hold, and

(b3)

R\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
and
B\cap B_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

The
\mathcal{C}
-pair
(M,P)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid if and only if

\begin{cases}\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),P(R)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(B),M\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(R),\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0\end{cases}

all hold. The first of these equalities is equivalent to condition (a) by Lemma
3.3
. The second equality is equivalent to the conditions (b1) and (b2) by Lemma
3.5
, keeping in mind Lemma
2.12
. The third and fourth equalities are equivalent to condition (b3) by Lemma
3.6
.
∎

We finish this paragraph with some observations about basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs and some examples. First notice that if we have some
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
, and we know that some
k\in[1,n]
is both in
R_{i}^{\times}
and
B_{j}^{\times}
for some
i,j
, then we have that
k
is not in
R\cup B
by Proposition
3.7
(b3). In particular
P(k)
is not a direct summand of
(M,P)
. This motivates the first observtation.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
i\in[2,p]
be odd. If
M_{i}\neq 0
, then
R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}
and
B_{i-1}^{\times}\subsetneq R_{i}^{\times}
.

Proof.

We only show the strict inclusion
R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}
as the proof for
B_{i-1}^{\times}\subsetneq R_{i}^{\times}
is similar. By (
3.3
) we have that the interval
B_{i}^{\times}
is equal to
[s_{i},s_{i}+n_{i}-1]
. Since
M_{i}\neq 0
, we have that
n_{i}>0
and son in particular
B_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i}+n_{i}-1]\neq\varnothing
. If
M_{i+1}=0
, then
R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
and so
R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}
holds. Assume that
M_{i+1}\neq 0
. Then (
3.3
) gives
R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i}+(l-l_{i+1})-1]
and using Proposition
3.7
(b1) and
l_{i}\leq n_{i}
we have

s_{i}+(l-l_{i+1})-1\leq s_{i}+(l_{i}-1)-1<s_{i}+n_{i}-1,

which shows that
R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}
.
∎

The second observation is related to the maximal number
k
such that
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
can all be nonzero. As we have mentioned in the introduction to this section, this is a crucial factor when investigating summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. At this point we only deal with two special cases.

Remark 3.9
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Let
i\in[2,p]
. Assume that
M_{i}\neq 0
and that
M_{i+1}\neq 0
. Then in the following cases we can show that
M_{i+2}=0
.

(a)

Assume first that
i
is odd and
d=2
. Assume to a contradiction that
M_{i+2}\neq 0
. Then the number
m_{i+2}=\lvert M_{i+2}\rvert
is positive. Then we have

\displaystyle m_{i+2}

\displaystyle\leq n_{i+2}-l_{i+2}+1

(Lemma
3.4
)

\displaystyle\leq(l-1-n_{i+1})+(1-n_{i})+1

(Proposition
3.7
(b1))

\displaystyle=l+1-n_{i+1}-n_{i}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leq l_{i}+l_{i+1}-n_{i+1}-n_{i}

(Proposition
3.7
(b1))

\displaystyle=(l_{i}-n_{i})+(l_{i+1}-n_{i+1})

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leq 0+0=0,

\displaystyle\text{(Lemma \ref{lem:immediate use of mi li ni})},

contradicting
m_{i+2}>0
. We conclude that
M_{i+2}=0
.

(b)

Now assume that
l=3
. Let
i
be even. Then by Proposition
3.7
(b2) we have that
n_{i}+n_{i+1}\leq l-1=2
and so we deduce that
n_{i}=l_{i}=1=n_{i+1}=l_{i+1}
. On the other hand, since
i+1
is odd, by Proposition
3.7
(b1) we obtain that

l+1\leq l_{i+1}+l_{i+2}=1+l_{i+2}\leq 1+l

from which we obtain that
l_{i+2}=l
and so
M_{i+2}=0
. If
i
is odd then a similar argument works.

Now we illustrate the notation we have introduced in the following example.

Example 3.10
.

Let us construct a basic
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
for
\Lambda(23,4)
. The algebra
\Lambda(23,4)
together with the
4
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
is illustrated via the following picture:

We see that we have
4
diagonals and so
p=4
. Moreover, we see that the simple modules in
\mathcal{C}
correspond to
s_{1}=1
,
s_{2}=9
,
s_{3}=15
,
s_{4}=23
.

For each of the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{2}
,
\mathcal{D}_{3}
and
\mathcal{D}_{4}
we need to choose a module
M_{2}
,
M_{3}
and
M_{4}
. We start by choosing, say,
M(s_{3},s_{3}+1)=M(15,16)
to be a summand of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}}
. This module has length
2
, and so
l_{3}\leq 2\leq n_{3}
where
l_{3}
respectively
n_{3}
are the smallest and largest lengths of modules in
M_{3}
. By Proposition
3.7
(b1) we obtain that
n_{2}\leq 1
and
l_{4}\geq 3
. We encircle
M(15,16)
with a red dashed line and put red crosses to the modules which we cannot choose because of the restrictions
n_{2}\leq 1
and
l_{4}\geq 3
.

We can also observe that certain indecomposable projectives are excluded from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}
respectively
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
, namely those whose top appears in the composition series of
M(15,16)
respectively
\tau_{4}(M(15,16))=M(8,9)
. These are precisely the modules that are encoded by
B_{3}^{\times}
and
R_{3}^{\times}
.

No further restrictions exist at this point and we may set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{2}}=M(8,8)
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{4}}=M(21,23)
. Then Proposition
3.7
(b2) gives
n_{3}\leq 2\leq l_{3}
. We also introduce new restrictions encoded by
B_{2}^{\times}
,
B_{4}^{\times}
,
R_{2}^{\times}
and
R_{4}^{\times}
:

As it can be seen from the above picture, we have now exhausted the amount of non-projective summands that we can include in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
, and are only left with including projective summands in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}
. We may for example include every indecomposable projective without a red cross in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}

which gives
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert=20<23=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. We may instead include every indecomposable projective without a blue cross in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}

which also gives
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert=20<23=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. Finally, we could include some indecomposable projective modules in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
and some indecomposable projective modules in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}
, for example

where we get more summands, but still shy of
\lvert\Lambda\rvert
:
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert=22<23=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. There are, of course, many different choices of indecomposable projective modules to include in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}
, but, as it is shown in Proposition
3.14
, given that we have that
d>2
and that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{i}}\neq 0
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{i+1}}\neq 0
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{i+2}}\neq 0
for some
i\in[2,p]
, any such choice gives
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert<\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. With this in mind, we encourage the reader to play around with this algebra and try to find a basic
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair with 23 summands, before venturing on.

Remark 3.11
.

Let
A
be any finite-dimensional algebra. If
d=1
, then there exists an analogue of
Bongartz completion

[
17
]
. Namely, if
(M,P)
is a basic
\tau
-rigid pair for
A
, then there exists a basic
\tau
-rigid pair
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
with
\lvert M^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert P^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert A\rvert
such that
M
is a direct summand of
M^{\prime}
and
P
is a direct summand of
P^{\prime}
, see
[
2
, Theorem 0.2]
. Example
3.10
shows that the naive generalization of this result for
d>1
fails.

3.2.
Basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs supported in consecutive diagonals

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. As we have seen, the indecomposable summands of
M
are either projective or they belong to
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for some
i\in[2,p]
. It turns out that the existence of a sequence
M_{i},M_{i+1},\ldots,M_{i+k}
where
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
j\in[0,k]
, can impose big restrictions on the rest of the modules appearing in
(M,P)
. The first such restriction is the following.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and let
i\in[2,p]
. Assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Then
\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq l-1
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
3.7
(b1) and (b2) we can show the inequalities

\sum_{j=0}^{k}n_{i+j}\leq\begin{cases}\left(\frac{k+3}{2}\right)(l-1),&\mbox{if $i$ is odd and $k$ is odd,}\\
\left(\frac{k+1}{2}\right)(l-1),&\mbox{if $i$ is even and $k$ is odd,}\\
\left(\frac{k+2}{2}\right)(l-1),&\mbox{if $k$ is even,}\end{cases}

and

\sum_{j=0}^{k}l_{i+j}\geq\begin{cases}\left(\frac{k+1}{2}\right)(l+1),&\mbox{if $i$
is odd and $k$ is odd,}\\
\left(\frac{k-1}{2}\right)(l+1)+2,&\mbox{if $i$ is even and $k$ is odd,}\\
\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)(l+1)+1,&\mbox{if $k$ is even.}\end{cases}

Let us demonstrate by showing the first inequality. We thus assume that
i
and
k
are both odd. Then using Proposition
3.7
(b1) and (b2) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}n_{i+j}

\displaystyle=n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(n_{i+2j-1}+n_{i+2j})+n_{i+k}\leq(l-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(l-1)+(l-1)=\left(\frac{k+3}{2}\right)(l-1).

Now using the above inequalities a direct computation shows that independently of the parity of
i
and
k
we have

\sum_{j=0}^{k}n_{i+j}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}l_{i+j}\leq l-k-2.

Using Lemma
3.4
and the above we compute

\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k}(n_{i+j}-l_{i+j}+1)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(n_{i+j}-l_{i+j})+\sum_{j=0}^{k}1\leq(l-k-2)+(k+1)=l-1,

as required.
∎

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Let
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Then these modules
M_{i+j}
define intervals
R_{i+j}^{\times}
and
B_{i+j}^{\times}
which affect which indecomposable projective modules can appear in
M
and
P
, as we have seen. We are interested in the largest possible subinterval
\Xi
of
[1,n]
that contains all of these intervals
R_{i+j}^{\times}
and
B_{i+j}^{\times}
, as this encodes which indecomposable projective modules are allowed or not allowed to be summands of
M
and
P
, given the sequence
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
. Thus we define

(3.4)

\Xi(i,i+k)=\begin{cases}[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i+k}],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd and $k$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i+k}+n_{i+k}-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd and $k$ is even,}\\
[s_{i-1},s_{i+k}+n_{i+k}-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is even and $k$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1},s_{i+k}],&\mbox{if $i$ is even and $k$ is even.}\end{cases}

That is
\Xi(i,i+k)
, is the interval starting at the start of
R_{i}^{\times}
and ending at the end of
B_{i+k}^{\times}
. We simply write
\Xi_{i}
instead of
\Xi(i,i)
. In particular we have

\Xi_{i}=\begin{cases}[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i}+n_{i}-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1},s_{i}],&\mbox{if $i$ is even,}\end{cases}

and a straightforward induction shows that
\Xi(i,i+k)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}\Xi_{i+j}
. We also define
Z_{i}=\Xi_{i}\setminus(R_{i}^{\times}\cup B_{i}^{\times})
, that is

(3.5)

Z_{i}=\begin{cases}[s_{i-1}+1,s_{i}-1],&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i}),s_{i}-n_{i}],&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

Example 3.13
.

In any of the
\tau_{4}
-rigid pairs
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
that appear in Example
3.10
, we have

\begin{split}\Xi_{2}&=[s_{1},s_{2}]=[1,9],\\
\Xi_{3}&=[s_{2}-(l-l_{3})+1,s_{3}+n_{3}-1]=[8,16],\\
\Xi_{4}&=[s_{3},s_{4}]=[15,23],\\
\Xi(2,4)&=[s_{1},s_{4}]=[1,23]=\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3}\cup\Xi_{4}.\end{split}

The main result of this paragraph counts the difference between the size of the set
\Xi(i,i+k)
and the sum of the number of indecomposable summands of the modules
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
together with the number of indecomposable projective modules indexed in
\Xi(i,i+k)
. It shows that in most cases
\Xi(i,i+k)
has strictly larger size. As we will see later, this is an obstruction to having a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Set
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
.

(a)

If
d=2
and
i
is odd, then
k\leq 1
.

(b)

If
d=2
and
i
is even, then
k\leq 2
.

(c)

If
d>2
and
k\geq 2
, then

(3.6)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}<\lvert\Xi\rvert.

Proof.

Part (a) is Remark
3.9
(a). Part (b) follows immediately from part (a). It remains to show part (c). Thus we assume that
d>2
and
k\geq 2
. Then we have that
M_{i}\neq 0
,
M_{i+1}\neq 0
and
M_{i+2}\neq 0
. By Lemma
3.12
we obtain that
\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq l-1
, that is the number of nonisomorphic indecomposable summands appearing in all of
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
is at most equal to
l-1
. Hence it is enough to show that
\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert\leq\Xi-l
. Therefore, it is enough to show that there exists a subset
X\subseteq\Xi
such that

(i)

(R\cup B)\cap X=\varnothing
, and

(ii)

\lvert X\rvert\geq l
.

We consider the cases
i
odd and
i
even separately and in each case we construct such a set
X
.

Assume first that
i
is odd. Set
X=R_{i+1}^{\times}\cup B_{i+1}^{\times}
. Clearly
X\subseteq\Xi
. By Lemma
3.8
we have that

R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}\text{ and }B_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq R_{i+2}^{\times}.

By Proposition
3.7
(b3) we have that
B\cap B_{j}^{\times}=\varnothing
and
R\cap R_{j}^{\times}=\varnothing
for all
j\in[2,p]
. We conclude that

(R\cup B)\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing\text{ and }(R\cup B)\cap B_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing,

and so condition (i) holds. Moreover, by definition of the intervals
R_{i+1}^{\times}
and
B_{i+1}^{\times}
we have that
R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
. Using Lemma
3.4
we obtain

\lvert X\rvert=\lvert R_{i+1}^{\times}\rvert+\lvert B_{i+1}^{\times}\rvert=l-l_{i+1}+n_{i+1}=l+(n_{i+1}-l_{i+1})\geq l,

and so condition (ii) holds too. This shows the claim when
i
is odd.

Assume now that
i
is even. Let
X_{1}=Z_{i+1}
. Using the definition of
Z_{i+1}
in (
3.5
) and since
i
is even, we obtain

(3.7)

\lvert X_{1}\rvert=s_{i+1}-1-(s_{i}+1)+1=\frac{d-2}{2}l+1\geq l+1,

where we used (
2.4
) and
d>2
. Hence
X_{1}
satisfies condition (ii). We may thus assume that
X_{1}
does not satisfy condition (i), otherwise there is nothing to prove. We then obtain that
(R\cup B)\cap Z_{i+1}\neq\varnothing
. We consider the subcases
R\cap Z_{i+1}\neq\varnothing
and
B\cap Z_{i+1}\neq\varnothing
separately.

Subcase 1
: Assume that
R\cap Z_{i+1}\neq\varnothing
. Then there exists
y\in R\cap Z_{i+1}
. Now let
X_{2}=R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times}\subseteq\Xi
. By definition of the intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
and
R_{i+1}^{\times}
we have that
R_{i}^{\times}\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
. Using Lemma
3.4
and Proposition
3.7
(b2) we obtain

\lvert X_{2}\rvert=\lvert R_{i}^{\times}\rvert+\lvert R_{i+1}^{\times}\rvert=(l-l_{i})+(l-l_{i+1})\geq 2l-(n_{i}+n_{i+1})\geq 2l-(l-1)=l+1.

Hence
X_{2}
also satisfies condition (ii). Again we may assume that
X_{2}
does not satisfy condition (i). Since by Proposition
3.7
(b3) we have that
R\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
and
R\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
, the negation of condition (i) for
X_{2}
gives that
B\cap(R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times})\neq\varnothing
. Thus there exists
x\in B\cap(R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times})
. By the definition of the intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
,
R_{i+1}^{\times}
and
Z_{i+1}
we have that

x\leq\max{(R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times})}=s_{i}<s_{i}+1=\min{Z_{i+1}}\leq y.

Since
x<y
and
x\in B
and
y\in R
all hold, by Proposition
3.7
(a) we conclude that there exists
x<z<\cdots<z+l-2<y
such that for
Z=[z,z+l-2]
we have
(R\cup B)\cap Z=\varnothing
. Set
X=Z\cup R_{i+2}^{\times}
. By Lemma
3.8
we have that
R_{i+2}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i+1}^{\times}
. By Proposition
3.7
(b3) we conclude that
R_{i+2}^{\times}\cap(R\cup B)=\varnothing
. Hence
X
satisfies condition (i). Moreover, we have that
Z\cap R_{i+2}^{\times}=\varnothing
since
\max{Z}<y\leq\max{Z_{i+1}}<\min{R_{i+2}^{\times}}
. Then

\lvert X\rvert=\lvert[z,z+l-2]\rvert+\lvert R_{i+2}^{\times}\rvert=l-1+\lvert R_{i+2}^{\times}\rvert\geq l,

and so
X
satisfies condition (ii) as well.

Subcase 2
: Assume that
B\cap Z_{i+1}\neq\varnothing
. Then there exists
x^{\prime}\in B\cap Z_{i+1}
. An argument similar to the previous subcase shows that
X_{3}=B_{i+2}^{\times}\cup B_{i+1}^{\times}
satisfies condition (ii) and thus we may assume that
R\cap(B_{i+1}^{\times}\cup B_{i+2}^{\times})\neq\varnothing
. Then there exists
y^{\prime}\in R\cap(B_{i+1}^{\times}\cup B_{i+2}^{\times})
. Applying Proposition
3.7
(a) to
x^{\prime}<y^{\prime}
similarly as above gives a set
Z^{\prime}
with
(R\cup B)\cap Z^{\prime}=\varnothing
and
\lvert Z^{\prime}\rvert=l-1
. Setting
X^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\cup B_{i}^{\times}
one can then show similarly that
X^{\prime}
satisfies conditions (i) and (ii).
∎

3.3.
Admissible configurations

We now want to describe summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs for
\Lambda
. Thus let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Assume that we have a sequence
M_{i},M_{i+1},\ldots,M_{i+k}
such that
M_{i+j}
is nonzero for all
0\leq j\leq k
. If
d=2
, then Proposition
3.14
(a) and (b) bound
k
by
1
if
i
is odd and by
2
if
i
is even. If
d>2
, then we show later that Proposition
3.14
(c) implies that
k\leq 1
. This indicates that
M
can intersect a very small number of consecutive diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
, namely at most
2
if
d>2
and at most
3
in the very special case when
d=2
and
i
is even. This is a first reduction of our problem of classifying summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs, as we now need to only study configurations satisfying

\displaystyle M_{i-1}=0,\;\;M_{i}\neq 0,\;\;\text{and }M_{i+1}=0,\text{ or}

\displaystyle M_{i-1}=0,\;\;M_{i}\neq 0,\;\;M_{i+1}\neq 0,\;\;\text{and }M_{i+2}=0,\text{ or}

\displaystyle M_{i-1}=0,\;\;M_{i}\neq 0,\;\;M_{i+1}\neq 0,\;\;M_{i+2}\neq 0,\;\;M_{i+3}=0,\;\;d=2,\;\;\text{and }\text{$i$ is even.}

We can find certain restrictions on how these modules look like and these restrictions are encoded in Table
1
in an illustrative way and in Definition
3.16
in a formal way.

In addition to the modules on the diagonals, we can find a few more necessary conditions that need to be satisfied in such local configurations if
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. The crucial part of these conditions pertain to the interval
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
and impose restrictions on which indecomposable projectives
P(\xi)
with
\xi\in\Xi
can be direct summands of
M
and of
P
. Since
P(R)
is the direct sum of all indecomposable projective summands of
M
and
P(B)=P
, we can reformulate these conditions using the intersections
R\cap\Xi
and
B\cap\Xi
. The following definition is motivated by the need to describe these conditions.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
I\subseteq[1,n]
. We say that
I
is

•

support
if
R\cap I=\varnothing
and
B\cap I=I\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=2}^{p}B_{i}^{\times}\right)
,

•

rigid
if
B\cap I=\varnothing
and
R\cap I=I\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=2}^{p}R_{i}^{\times}\right)
,

•

support to rigid at
x\in I
if
B\cap I=\{y\in I\mid y\leq x\}\neq\varnothing
and
R\cap I=\{y\in I\mid x+l\leq y\}\neq\varnothing
,

•

rigid to support at
x\in I
if
R\cap I=\{y\in I\mid y\leq x\}\neq\varnothing
and
B\cap I=\{y\in I\mid x+1\leq y\}\neq\varnothing
.

Note that in Definition
3.15
the properties of the interval
I
are defined with respect to the given
\mathcal{C}
-pair, which is suppressed in the naming. Note also that an empty interval is both rigid and support, and that is the only interval with this property. With this in mind we can now describe all configurations that are possible for a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, as we prove later.

(I) -
i
odd

(I) -
i
even

(II) -
i
odd

(II) -
i
even

(III) -
i
odd

(III) -
i
even

(IV)

m_{i}+m_{i+1}<l-1

(V)

m_{i}+m_{i+1}<l-1

(VI)

m_{i}+m_{i+1}=l-1

(VII)

m_{i}+m_{i+1}=l-1

(VIII)

\begin{array}[]{c}l_{i+1}=l-l_{i+2}+1\\
n_{i+1}=l-n_{i}-1\\
m_{i}+m_{i+1}+m_{i+2}=l-1\end{array}

Table 1.
Types of admissible configurations

Definition 3.16
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Set
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
. We say that
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is an
admissible configuration
if one of the following holds.

(I)

k=0
,
l_{i}=1
,
n_{i}<l-1
and
\Xi
is support.

(II)

k=0
,
l_{i}>1
,
n_{i}=l-1
and
\Xi
is rigid.

(III)

k=0
,
l_{i}=1
,
n_{i}=l-1
and
\Xi
is either support, rigid, or support to rigid at some
x\in\Xi\setminus B_{i}^{\times}
.

(IV)

k=1
,
i
is odd,
n_{i}=n_{i+1}=l-1
,
m_{i}+m_{i+1}<l-1
, and
\Xi
is rigid.

(V)

k=1
,
i
is even,
l_{i}=l_{i+1}=1
,
m_{i}+m_{i+1}<l-1
, and
\Xi
is support.

(VI)

k=1
,
i
is odd,
n_{i}=n_{i+1}=l-1
,
m_{i}+m_{i+1}=l-1
and
\Xi
is rigid or support to rigid at
x\in[s_{i}-(l-m_{i+1}),s_{i}-1]
.

(VII)

k=1
,
i
is even,
l_{i}=l_{i+1}=1
,
m_{i}+m_{i+1}=l-1
, and
\Xi
is support or support to rigid at
x\in[s_{i}-(l-1),s_{i}-m_{i}]
.

(VIII)

k=2
,
d=2
,
i
is even,
l_{i}=1
,
l_{i+1}=l-l_{i+2}+1
,
n_{i+1}=l-n_{i}-1
,
n_{i+2}=l-1
and
\Xi
is support to rigid at
x\in[s_{i+1}-l_{i+2},s_{i}-n_{i}]
.

In each case (I)—(VIII) we call the corresponding roman numeral the
type
of the admissible configuration. We say that an admissible configuration is
full
if
m_{i+j}=n_{i+j}-l_{i+j}+1
holds for
0\leq j\leq k
.

Before we give some examples, we collect some useful observations about admissible configurations.

Remark 3.17
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
and let
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
be an admissible configuration. Let
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
.

(a)

If
l=2
then
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is necessarily of type (III).

(b)

An admissible configuration has the property of being “locally”
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid. Indeed, assume that
M_{i-1}=0
and
M_{i+k+1}=0
. Set
R^{\prime}=R\cap\Xi
and
B^{\prime}=B\cap\Xi
. Let
i^{\prime}\in[i,i+k]
. It is straightforward to check that independently of the type of the admissible configuration, conditions (a) and (b3) in Proposition
3.7
hold with
R^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
in place of
R
and
B
and conditions (b1) and (b2) in Proposition
3.7
hold with
i^{\prime}
in place of
i
.

(c)

Assume that the admissible configuration is full. We claim that

(3.8)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i}=\lvert\Xi\rvert.

Indeed, we have that
\Xi
is either support, rigid or support to rigid. Assume
\Xi
is support. Then
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is of one of the types (I), (III), (V) or (VII). In all of these cases
l_{i+t}=1
for
0\leq t\leq k
and so
m_{i+t}=n_{i+t}
. Additionally, in cases (V) and (VII) it is easy to see that
B_{i}^{\times}\cap B_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
. Hence, since
\Xi
is support, we compute

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{t=0}^{k}\lvert B_{i+t}^{\times}\rvert=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{t=0}^{q}n_{i+t}=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{t=0}^{q}m_{i+t},

which shows (
3.8
). If
\Xi
is rigid, then the proof is similar. If
\Xi
is support to rigid, then
\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert=\lvert\Xi\rvert-(l-1)
and
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is of one of the types (III), (VI), (VII) or (VIII). We claim that in all of these types we have
\sum_{t=0}^{k}m_{i+t}=l-1
, and so (
3.8
) holds again. Indeed, it is only needed to show that
\sum_{t=0}^{k}m_{i+t}=l-1
holds in type (VIII), as in the other cases it holds by definition. In that case we have
k=2
and

\sum_{t=0}^{2}m_{i+t}=\sum_{t=0}^{2}(n_{i+t}-l_{i+t}+1)=(n_{i}-1+1)+(l-n_{i}-1-(l-l_{i+2}+1)+1)+(l-1-l_{i+2}+1)=l-1,

as required.

(d)

Assume that the type of the configuration is (III). If
\Xi
is support, then
\Xi
is the disjoint union of two consecutive intervals
B\cap\Xi
and
B_{i}^{\times}
. If
\Xi
is rigid, then
\Xi
is the disjoint union of two consecutive intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
and
R\cap\Xi
. Furthermore, both intervals
B_{i}^{\times}
and
R_{i}^{\times}
have length
l-1
.

Example 3.18
.

Let us assume that we have a
\tau_{4}
-rigid module
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
of
\Lambda(23,4)
which we can see encircled in the following picture:

In the above picture we have also marked the sets
R_{2}^{\times}\cup R_{3}^{\times}
with red crosses, the set
B_{2}^{\times}\cup B_{3}^{\times}
with blue crosses and
\Xi(2,3)
. Assume that we want to find a projective module
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P
such that
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
is a
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (VII). Then there are several choices for
\Xi(2,3)
. For example,
\Xi(2,3)
can be support:

or,
\Xi(2,3)
can be support to rigid at
7\in[s_{2}-(l-1),s_{2}-m_{2}]=[6,7]
:

In each of these case we can construct
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
such that
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert=23
. In the first case this is done by letting
[1,23]\setminus\Xi(2,3)=[16,23]
be support, and in the other case by letting
[16,23]
be either rigid, rigid to support, or support.

Example 3.19
.

Now, let us look at the slightly special case of
d=2
. We consider the algebra
\Lambda(13,5)
which admit a
2
-cluster tilting subcategory, and the
\tau_{2}
-rigid module
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
given by the following encircled summands:

Now, if we want to expand
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{4}})
into an admissible configuration, it has to be of type (VIII). Hence
\Xi(2,4)=[1,13]
needs to be support to rigid at some
x\in[s_{3}-4,s_{2}-1]=[4,5]=\mathcal{I}
. For example, the following expansion is a full admissible configuration of type (VIII)

After counting, we see that
\lvert{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}\rvert+\lvert{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P}\rvert=13=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Set
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
. As we have mentioned, we are now interested in studying
(M,P)
locally in the “neighbourhood” of the sequence
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
. A way to define this neighbourhood is to think of all the indecomposable
\Lambda
-modules which are directly affected from
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
with respect to being or not direct summands of
(M,P)
. As we have seen, the interval
\Xi
is the largest subinterval of
[1,n]
which contains all of
R_{i+j}^{\times}
and
B_{i+j}^{\times}
for
0\leq j\leq k
and thus is a good candidate for such a neighbourhood. Thus we can count how many
\xi\in\Xi
belong to
R
, how many
\xi\in\Xi
belong to
B
and also count the indecomposable summands of each
M_{i+j}
which we have agreed to denote by
m_{i+j}
. Thus the number of indecomposable summands of
(M,P)
in this neighbourhood of
M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k}
is

(3.9)

N\coloneqq(R\cup B)\cap\Xi+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}.

We have seen in Proposition
3.14
that if
d>2
and
k\geq 2
, then
N(i,i+k)
cannot be greater than
\lvert\Xi\rvert-1
. The main aim of this paragraph is to show that the greatest possible value for
N
is
\lvert\Xi\rvert
, and moreover that this value is attained if and only if
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration.

We should mention that at this point it is not clear why maximizing each of these numbers
N
does maximize the total number of indecomposable summands of
(M,P)
