which is our aim. But the fact that the maximal possible value attained by
N
is
\lvert\Xi\rvert
will be enough to show that indeed such a maximization has to happen. The details for this point belong to the next section.

In counting the number
N
from above, we may notice that the intersection
(R\cup B)\cap\Xi
is closely related to the intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
and
B_{i}^{\times}
. Hence we first investigate how the intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
and
R_{i+1}^{\times}
interact when
M_{i}\neq 0
and
M_{i+1}\neq 0
(and similarly for
B_{i}^{\times}
and
B_{i+1}^{\times}
).

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
2\leq i\leq i+1\leq p
be such that
M_{i}\neq 0
and
M_{i+1}\neq 0
.

(a)

If
i
is even and
d=2
, then
R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i-1},s_{i}]
. Otherwise, we have
R_{i}^{\times}\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
.

(b)

If
i
is odd and
d=2
, then
B_{i}^{\times}\cup B_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i+1}]
. Otherwise, we have
B_{i}^{\times}\cap B_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

We only show part (a) as part (b) is similar. If
i
is odd, then by (
3.3
) we have that
R_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i-1}]
and
R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i}+(l-l_{i})-1]
and so
R_{i}^{\times}\cap R_{i+1=\varnothing}^{\times}
since
s_{i-1}<s_{i}
.

Assume that
i
is even. By (
3.3
) we have that
R_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i-1},s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i})-1]
and
R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i}-(l-l_{i+1})+1,s_{i}]
. If
d>2
, then using
s_{i}-s_{i-1}=\tfrac{d}{2}l
by (
2.4
) and
1\leq l_{i}
by Lemma
3.4
, we obtain that

s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i})-1=s_{i}-\frac{d}{2}l+(l-l_{i})-1<s_{i}-l+(l-l_{i})-1=s_{i}-l_{i}-1\leq s_{i}-2,

and so
R_{i}^{\times}\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
. If
d=2
, then it is enough to show that

s_{i-1}\leq s_{i}-(l-l_{i+1})+1\leq\left(s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i})-1\right)+1\leq s_{i}.

Equivalently, and using
s_{i}-s_{i-1}=l
by (
2.4
), we need to show that

0\leq l_{i+1}+1\leq l-l_{i}\leq l.

Thus only the middle inequality needs to be shown. But this inequality is equivalent to
l_{i}+l_{i+1}\leq l-1
, which holds since
l_{i}+l_{i+1}\leq n_{i}+n_{i+1}\leq l-1
by Proposition
3.7
(b2).
∎

In the definition of admissible configurations we see that the interval
\Xi
is either support, rigid, or support to rigid. Assume that
\Xi
is rigid; similar considerations hold when
\Xi
is support. The interval
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
contains all of the intervals
R_{i}^{\times},\ldots,R_{i+k}^{\times}
and exactly those as long as intervals of the form
R_{j}^{\times}
are concerned. Therefore,
\Xi
being rigid implies that
(R\cup B)\cap\Xi
is equal to
\Xi
from which we have removed all of the intervals
R_{i}^{\times},\ldots,R_{i+k}^{\times}
. Then the number
N
in (
3.9
) is equal to

\lvert\Xi\rvert-\lvert\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}R_{i+j}^{\times}\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}.

Thus to find the maximal value of
N
we need to consider the difference between the last two terms above. The following technical lemma deals with this case.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
k\in\{0,1\}
be such that
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Set
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
,
R^{\times}(i,i+k)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}R_{i+j}^{\times}
, and
B^{\times}(i,i+k)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}B_{i+j}^{\times}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert\geq\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}
. Moreover, if
k=1
and
i
is even, then the inequality is strict.

(b)

\lvert B^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert\geq\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}
. Moreover, if
k=1
and
i
is odd, then the inequality is strict.

(c)

Let
X=R^{\times}(i,i+k)
or
X=B^{\times}(i,i+k)
. If

(3.10)

(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=\Xi\setminus X\text{ and }\lvert X\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}

hold, then
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration.

Proof.

(a)

If
k=1
and
i
is even and
d=2
, then using Lemma
3.20
(a) and Proposition
3.7
(b2) we have

\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+1)\rvert=s_{i}-s_{i-1}=l>n_{i}+n_{i+1}\geq m_{i}+m_{i+1},

as required. Otherwise
R_{i}^{\times}\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}=\varnothing
holds by Lemma
3.20
(a) and so

\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\lvert R_{i+j}^{\times}\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(l-l_{i+j})\geq\sum_{j=0}^{k}(n_{i+j}+1-l_{i+j})=\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j},

as required. Moreover, if
k=1
and
i
is even, then we have
l\geq n_{i}+n_{i+1}+1
by Proposition
3.7
(b2). Using this we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert R_{i}^{\times}\rvert+\lvert R_{i+1}^{\times}\rvert=l-l_{i}+l-l_{i+1}\geq 2(n_{i}+n_{i+1}+1)-l_{i}-l_{i+1}

\displaystyle=m_{i}+m_{i+1}+n_{i}+n_{i+1}>m_{i}+m_{i+1},

and so the inequality is strict.

(b)

Similar to part (a).

(c)

We only show the claim when
X=R^{\times}(i,i+k)
as the proof when
X=B^{\times}(i,i+k)
is similar. By definition of the intervals we have that
s_{i+k}\in B_{i+k}^{\times}
and
s_{i+k}\not\in R^{\times}(i,i+k)
, see (
3.3
). By the first part of (
3.10
) we have

s_{i+k}\in\Xi\setminus R^{\times}(i,i+k)=(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=(R\cap\Xi)\cup(B\cap\Xi).

Since
s_{i+k}\in B_{i+k}^{\times}
and
B\cap B_{i+k}^{\times}=\varnothing
, we conclude that
s_{i+k}\in R\cap\Xi
. In particular, we have that
s_{i+k}\in R\cap B_{i+k}^{\times}
. Set
y=s_{i+k}
.

We claim that
B\cap(\Xi\setminus X)=\varnothing
. Indeed, assume to a contradiction that there exists
x\in B\cap(\Xi\setminus X)
. In particular
x\not\in B_{i+k}^{\times}
and
x\not\in R_{i}^{\times}
. Recall that
\Xi
is the interval starting at
R_{i}^{\times}
and ending at
B_{i+k}^{\times}
. Since
y\in B_{i+k}^{\times}
, we obtain that
x<y
. Then by Proposition
3.7
(a) there exists an interval
Z=[z,z+l-2]
with
x<z<z+l-2<y
such that
(R\cup B)\cap Z=\varnothing
. It follows that

(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=(R\cup B)\cap(\Xi\setminus Z)\subseteq\Xi\setminus Z.

By the first part of (
3.10
) we conclude that
\Xi\setminus X\subseteq\Xi\setminus Z
or equivalently that
Z\subseteq X=R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+k}^{\times}
. Since
x<z
and
x\not\in R_{i}^{\times}
, we have that
R_{i}^{\times}
and
Z
are disjoint (as both intervals
R_{i}^{\times}
and
Z
lie inside
\Xi
, and
\Xi
starts at
R_{i}^{\times}
). Hence
k=1
and
Z\subseteq R_{i+1}^{\times}
. Then the second part of (
3.10
) together with part (a) of this lemma imply that
i
is odd. Hence Lemma
3.8
implies that

Z\subseteq R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}

and so
l-1=\lvert Z\rvert<\lvert B_{i}^{\times}\rvert=n_{i}\leq l-1
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, our claim that
B\cap(\Xi\setminus X)=\varnothing
holds.

Now notice that by the first part of
(
3.10
) we have

\varnothing=B\cap(\Xi\setminus X)=B\cap((R\cup B)\cap\Xi)=(B\cap(R\cup B))\cap\Xi=B\cap\Xi.

Hence
B\cap\Xi=\varnothing
and so the first part of (
3.10
) gives that
\Xi
is rigid. By the second part of (
3.10
) we have that
\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}
holds. By part (a) of this lemma we obtain that
k=0
or
i
is even. In any case, Lemma
3.20
gives that
\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+k)\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\lvert R_{i+j}^{\times}\rvert
. Hence we obtain that

\sum_{j=0}^{k}(l-l_{i+j})=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\lvert R_{i+j}^{\times}\rvert=\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k}(n_{i+j}-l_{i+j}+1).

Since
n_{i+j}\leq l-1
for
0\leq j\leq k
, we conclude that the last inequality must be an equality and that
n_{i+j}=l-1
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Therefore, if
k=0
, then
(M_{i})
is a full admissible configuration of type (II) or (III), while if
k=1
, then we have seen that
i
is odd and so
(M_{i},M_{i+1})
is a full admissible configuration of type (IV) or (VI). ∎

We can now show the main result for this paragraph.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
2\leq i\leq i+k\leq p
and assume that
M_{i+j}\neq 0
for
0\leq j\leq k
. Set
\Xi=\Xi(i,i+k)
. Then

(3.11)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert.

Moreover equality holds if and only if
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration.

Proof.

If
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration, then equality holds in (
3.11
) by Remark
3.17
(c). We now show the other direction.

If
d>2
and
k\geq 2
then (
3.11
) holds by Proposition
3.14
(c). Hence we may assume that
d=2
or
k\leq 1
. We consider the two cases separately.

Case
k\leq 1
. In this case we work similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.14
. That is, it is enough to show that there exists a subset
X\subseteq\Xi
such that

(i)

(R\cup B)\cap X=\varnothing
,

(ii)

\lvert X\rvert\geq\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}
, and

(iii)

if (
3.10
) holds, then
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration.

Indeed, conditions (i) and (ii) give

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap(\Xi\setminus X)\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert-\lvert X\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{i+j}\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert,

which shows (
3.11
). Moreover, if equality holds in (
3.11
), then the above line shows that (
3.10
) holds and so
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration by condition (iii).

Notice that by Lemma
3.21
conditions (ii) and (iii) hold for
R^{\times}(i,i+k)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}R_{i+j}^{\times}
and for
B^{\times}(i,i+k)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B_{i+j}^{\times}
. Hence we may assume that condition (i) fails for these two sets, otherwise the claim follows. Thus we assume that
(R\cup B)\cap R^{\times}(i,i+k)\neq\varnothing
, from which we obtain that
B\cap R^{\times}(i,i+k)\neq\varnothing
, and we assume that
(R\cup B)\cap B^{\times}(i,i+k)\neq\varnothing
, from which we obtain that
R\cap B^{\times}(i,i+k)\neq\varnothing
. Let
x\in B\cap R^{\times}(i,i+k)
and
y\in R\cap B^{\times}(i,i+k)
.

We claim that
x<y
. Recall that
\Xi
is the interval starting at
R_{i}^{\times}
and ending at
B_{i+k}^{\times}
. By assumption, we have that
x\in R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+k}^{\times}
. Moreover,
y\in R
and so
y\not\in R_{i}^{\times}
. Hence if
x\in R_{i}^{\times}
, since
\Xi
starts at
R_{i}^{\times}
, we have that
x<y
. Similarly, if
y\in B_{i+k}^{\times}
, then we again obtain
x<y
. Hence it is enough to show that if
k=1
and
x\in R_{i+1}^{\times}
and
y\in B_{i}^{\times}
, then we reach a contradiction. If
i
is odd, then
R_{i+1}^{\times}\subsetneq B_{i}^{\times}
by Lemma
3.8
, contradicting
x\in B\cap R_{i+1}^{\times}
. Hence
i
is even. But then
i+1
is odd and Lemma
3.8
gives
B_{i}^{\times}\subsetneq R_{i+1}^{\times}
, contradicting
y\in R\cap B_{i}^{\times}
. This shows that
x<y
.

Since
x<y
, by Proposition
3.7
(a) there exists an interval
Z=[z,z+l-2]
with
x<z<z+l-2<y
such that
(R\cup B)\cap Z=\varnothing
. Hence the interval
Z\subseteq\Xi
satisfies condition (i). Moreover, since
\lvert Z\rvert=l-1
, it also satisfies condition (ii) by Lemma
3.12
. Hence it remains to show that
Z
satisfies condition (iii). Assume thus that (
3.10
) holds for
Z
and we need to show that
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration. Let
x^{\prime}=\min(R_{i}^{\times})
and note that

x^{\prime}=\min(R_{i}^{\times})=\min(\Xi)\leq x<z=\min(Z),

and so
x^{\prime}\not\in Z
. By the first part of (
3.10
) we then have

x^{\prime}\in\Xi\setminus Z=(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=(R\cap\Xi)\cup(B\cap\Xi).

Since
R\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing
and
x^{\prime}\in R_{i}^{\times}
, we conclude that
x^{\prime}\in B\cap\Xi
. We now claim that
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}\cap R=\varnothing
. Assume to a contradiction that there exists
\xi\in\Xi
with
\xi<z
and
\xi\in R
. Then
x^{\prime}=\min(\Xi)\leq z
and since
x^{\prime}\in B
and
\xi\in R
, we have
x^{\prime}<\xi
. Hence by Proposition
3.7
(a) there exists an interval
Z^{\prime}
of length
l-1
between
x^{\prime}
and
\xi
such that
(R\cup B)\cap Z^{\prime}=\varnothing
. In particular,
Z^{\prime}\cap Z=\varnothing
since
\xi<z=\min(Z)
. But then by the first part of (
3.10
) we have

Z^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\setminus Z\subseteq\Xi\setminus Z=(R\cup B)\cap\Xi.

Hence
Z^{\prime}\subseteq R\cup B
and together with
(R\cup B)\cap Z^{\prime}=\varnothing
we obtain
Z^{\prime}=\varnothing
, contradicting
\lvert Z^{\prime}\rvert=l-1
. We conclude that indeed
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}\cap R=\varnothing
. Then

\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}\setminus Z\subseteq\Xi\setminus Z=(R\cap\Xi)\cup(B\cap\Xi)

implies that
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}\subseteq B\cap\Xi
. A similar argument shows that
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi>z+l-2\}\subseteq R\cap\Xi
. Then we have

\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}\sqcup\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi>z+l-2\}=\Xi\setminus Z=(R\cap\Xi)\sqcup(B\cap\Xi),

and together with the aforementioned inclusions, we conclude that
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi<z\}=B\cap\Xi
and
\{\xi\in\Xi\mid\xi>z+l-2\}=R\cap\Xi
. Hence
\Xi
is support to rigid at
z-1
. At this point we consider the subcases
k=0
and
k=1
separately.

Subcase
k=0
. If
k=0
, then the second condition of (
3.10
) gives
l-1=\lvert Z\rvert=m_{i}
, from which we obtain that
n_{i}=l-1
,
l_{i}=1
and
m_{i}=n_{i}-l_{i}+1
. Hence in this case
(M_{i})
is a full admissible configuration of type (III).

Subcase
k=1
. Now assume that
k=1
. Then the first part of (
3.10
) gives

(R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times})\cap(\Xi\setminus Z)=(R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times})\cap(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=\varnothing.

Hence
R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times}\subseteq Z=[z,z+l-2]
. Therefore, if
R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times}=[\alpha,\beta]
, then

(3.12)

z-1\leq\alpha-1\text{ and }\beta+1\leq z+l-1.

Also the second part of (
3.10
) gives
l-1=\lvert Z\rvert=m_{i}+m_{i+1}
. We consider the cases
i
odd and
i
even separately.

Assume that
i
is odd. Then using Lemma
3.4
and Proposition
3.7
(b1) we have

\displaystyle l-1

\displaystyle=m_{i}+m_{i+1}\leq(n_{i}-l_{i}+1)+(n_{i+1}-l_{i+1}+1)=n_{i}+n_{i+1}+2-(l_{i}+l_{i+1})

\displaystyle\leq n_{i}+n_{i+1}+2-(l+1)\leq(l-1)+(l-1)+2-(l+1)=l-1.

We conclude that all inequalities above must be equalities and so
m_{i+1}=n_{i+1}-l_{i+1}+1
and
n_{i+1}=l-1
. On the other hand, using (
3.3
) we compute that
[\alpha,\beta]=R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i}+(l-l_{i+1})-1]
. Hence in this case (
3.12
) becomes

z-1\leq s_{i}-1\text{ and }s_{i}+(l-l_{i+1})-1+1\leq z+l-1.

Equivalently, and using
m_{i+1}=n_{i+1}-l_{i+1}+1
and
n_{i+1}=l-1
, we obtain

z-1\leq s_{i}-1\text{ and }s_{i}-(l-m_{i+1})\leq z-1.

Hence
z-1\in[s_{i}-(l-m_{i+1}),s_{i}-1]
, which shows that
(M_{i},M_{i+1})
is a full admissible configuration of type (VI).

Assume that
i
is even. Then using Lemma
3.4
and Proposition
3.7
(b2) we have

l-1=m_{i}+m_{i+1}\leq n_{i}+n_{i+1}+2-(l_{i}+l_{i+1})\leq(l-1)+2-(l_{i}+l_{i+1})\leq(l-1)+2-(1+1)=l-1.

We conclude that all inequalities above must be equalities and so
m_{i}=n_{i}-l_{1}+1
and
l_{i}=1
. On the other hand, using (
3.3
) we compute that
[\alpha,\beta]=R_{i+1}^{\times}\cap B_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i}-n_{i}+1,s_{i}]
. Hence in this case (
3.12
) becomes

z-1\leq s_{i}-n_{i}+1-1\text{ and }s_{i}+1\leq z+l-1.

Equivalently, and using
m_{i}=n_{i}-l_{i}+1
and
l_{i}=1
, we obtain

z-1\leq s_{i}-m_{i}\text{ and }s_{i}-(l-1)\leq z-1.

Hence
z-1\in[s_{i}-(l-1),s_{i}-m_{i}]
, which shows that
(M_{i},M_{i+1})
is an admissible configuration of type (VII).

Case
d=2
. By Proposition
3.14
(a) and (b) we obtain that
k\leq 2
. Since the case
k\leq 1
has already been dealt with, we may assume that
k=2
. Then Proposition
3.14
(a) implies that
i
is even. Hence we have that
d=2
and
i
is even and
k=2
. Using (
3.3
) and (
2.4
) we may immediately compute that

R_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i}-l,s_{i}-2]\text{ and }R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i}-(l-l_{i+1})+1,s_{i}].

We claim that

R^{\times}(i,i+1)=R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times}=[s_{i}-l,s_{i}].

Indeed, it is enough to show that

s_{i}-(l-l_{i+1})+1\leq s_{i}-1,

or equivalently that
1+l_{i+1}\leq l-1
. Notice that since since
m_{i}>0
, we have that
n_{i}\geq 1
and so Proposition
3.7
(b2) gives
n_{i+1}\leq l-2
. In particular
l_{i+1}\leq n_{i+1}\leq l-2
which shows the required inequality
1+l_{i+1}\leq l-1
. This shows that
R^{\times}(i,i+1)=[s_{i}-l,s_{i}]
. Similarly we may show that
B^{\times}(i+1,i+2)=[s_{i+2}-l,s_{i+2}]
.

Assume now that
B\cap R^{\times}(i,i+1)=\varnothing
. Then, since
\lvert R^{\times}(i,i+1)\rvert=l+1
, and using Lemma
3.12
, we obtain that

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{2}m_{i+j}\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert-(l+1)+(l-1)=\lvert\Xi\rvert-2<\lvert\Xi\rvert,

which shows (
3.11
). Hence we may assume that there exists
x\in B\cap R^{\times}(i,i+1)
. A similar argument shows that we may assume that there exists
y\in R\cap B^{\times}(i+1,i+2)
. By our computations we have that
x\in[s_{i}-l,s_{i}]
and
y\in[s_{i+2}-l,s_{i+2}]
and so
x<y
by (
2.4
). Hence by Proposition
3.7
(a) there exists an interval
Z=[z,z+l-2]
with
x<z<z+l-2<y
such that
(R\cup B)\cap Z=\varnothing
. Then using Lemma
3.12
again we have

(3.13)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{j=0}^{2}m_{i+j}\leq\lvert\Xi\setminus Z\rvert+(l-1)=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\lvert Z\rvert+(l-1)=\lvert\Xi\rvert,

and so (
3.11
) holds again. Moreover, in the case of equality in (
3.11
), we obtain that all inequalities in (
3.13
) are equalities. Hence
m_{i+j}=n_{i+j}-l_{i+j}+1
for
j\in\{0,1,2\}
and also
(R\cup B)\cap\Xi=\Xi\setminus Z
. Then, as in the previous case, we can show that
z-1\in[s_{i+1}-l_{i+2},s_{i}-n_{i}]
. Therefore in this case
(M_{i},M_{i+1},M_{i+2})
is a full admissible configuration of type (VIII).
∎

3.4.
Well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pairs

Admissible configurations play an important role in the characterization of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid modules. Indeed, they restrict the local behaviour of a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
. The other restriction comes from what happens between two admissible configurations. To explain this other restriction, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. The
diagonal component of
M
is defined to be the set
T=T(M)=\{i\in[2,p]\mid M_{i}\neq 0\}
. By ordering the elements of
T
there is a unique way to write
T
as a union of disjoint intervals
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
:

T=T_{1}\sqcup T_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup T_{k}

such that
\max(T_{j})<\min(T_{j+1})-1
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
. We call
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
the
diagonal partition of
M
.

In other words, the diagonal partition of a
\mathcal{C}
-pair
(M,P)
encodes which sequences of consecutive diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i+k}
are intersected by
M
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with diagonal partition
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
. For
1\leq j\leq k
we write
T_{j}=[t_{j,1},t_{j,2}]
and we set
\Xi(T_{j})\coloneqq\Xi(t_{j,1},t_{j,2})
. For
0\leq j\leq k
we define

(3.14)

\Theta_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}[1,\min(\Xi(T_{1}))-1],&\mbox{if $j=0$,}\\
[\max(\Xi(T_{j}))+1,\min(\Xi(T_{j+1}))-1],&\mbox{if $1\leq j\leq k-1$,}\\
[\max(\Xi(T_{j}))+1,n],&\mbox{if $j=k$.}\end{cases}

The interval
\Xi(T_{j})
plays the same role as the interval
\Xi(i,i+k)
which we saw in the previous paragraph. The interval
\Theta_{j}
is exactly the interval between the intervals
\Xi(T_{j})
and
\Xi(T_{j+1})
as the following examples demonstrate and as we show soon.

Example 3.24
.

(a)

The diagonal partition of the
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair in Example
3.10
is
T_{1}=[2,4]
with
\Xi(T_{1})=[1,23]
and
\Theta_{0}=\varnothing=\Theta_{1}
.

(b)

The diagonal partition of the
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair in Example
3.18
is
T_{1}=[2,3]
with
\Xi(T_{1})=[1,15]
,
\Theta_{0}=\varnothing
and
\Theta_{1}=[16,23]
.

Example 3.25
.

Let
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair of
\Lambda(n,l)
.

(a)

If
d=4,\ n=37,\ l=4
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
is given by

then the diagonal partition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
is
(T_{1},T_{2})=([3,3],[5,6])
. In addition, we have

\Xi(T_{1})=[7,16],\ \Xi(T_{2})=[23,37],\text{ and }\Theta_{0}=[1,6],\ \Theta_{1}=[17,22],\ \Theta_{2}=\varnothing.

(b)

If
d=2,\ n=34,\ l=5
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
is given by

then the diagonal partition is
(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})=([2,4],[6,7],[9,9])
. In addition, we see that

\Xi(T_{1})=[1,13],\ \Xi(T_{2})=[15,24],\ \Xi(T_{3})=[25,32],

and

\Theta_{0}=\varnothing,\ \Theta_{1}=[14,14],\ \Theta_{2}=\varnothing,\ \Theta_{3}=[33,34].

If
j=0
or
j=k
then, as the above examples show, it is possible that
\Theta_{j}
is empty. Moreover, in the above examples we see that
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})=\varnothing
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
. This always holds for
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs, and even for
\mathcal{C}
-pairs there is only one possible case where this property does not hold as recorded in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with diagonal partition
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
.

(a)

Let
1\leq j\leq k-1
and set
T_{j}=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]
and
T_{j+1}=[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]
. Then the following are equivalent

(i)

\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})\neq\varnothing
.

(ii)

d=2
and
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{2}+2
and
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}
are both odd and
n_{\alpha_{2}}>l_{\alpha_{2}+2}+1
all hold.

(b)

Assume that
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})=\varnothing
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
. Then the sequence

(3.15)

\Theta_{0},\Xi(T_{1}),\Theta_{1},\Xi(T_{2}),\ldots,\Theta_{k-1},\Xi(T_{k}),\Theta_{k}

is a sequence of consecutive intervals which forms a partition of
[1,n]
.

Proof.

(a)

From the definition of a diagonal partition we have that
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{2}+q
for some
q\geq 2
. From (
3.4
) and using Lemma
3.4
we see that

\max(\Xi(T_{j}))\leq\begin{cases}s_{\alpha_{2}}+l-2,&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ is odd,}\\
s_{\alpha_{2}},&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ is even,}\end{cases}

and

\min(\Xi(T_{j+1}))\geq\begin{cases}s_{\beta_{1}-1}-l+2,&\text{ if }\beta_{1}\text{ is odd,}\\
s_{\beta_{1}-1},&\text{ if }\beta_{1}\text{ is even.}\end{cases}

On the other hand, (
3.4
) also gives that
\min(\Xi(T_{j}))\leq s_{\alpha_{1}-1}
. Together with
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}=\beta_{1}-q\leq\beta_{1}-2
, we obtain

\min(\Xi(T_{j}))\leq s_{\alpha_{1}-1}\leq s_{\alpha_{2}-1}\overset{(\ref{eq:difference of consecutive simples})}{=}s_{\alpha_{2}+1}-(d-1)l+2\leq s_{\beta_{1}-1}-l+2\leq\min(\Xi(T_{j}+1)).

We conclude that
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})=\varnothing
if and only if

(3.16)

\max(\Xi(T_{j}))<\min(\Xi(T_{j+1})),

holds. From (
2.4
) we obtain the following computation

s_{\beta_{1}-1}-s_{\alpha_{2}}=\begin{cases}\frac{q-1}{2}[(d-1)l+2],&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ is odd and }\beta_{1}\text{ is even,}\\
\frac{q-1}{2}[(d-1)l+2],&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ is even and }\beta_{1}\text{ is odd,}\\
\frac{q-2}{2}[(d-1)l+2]+\frac{d}{2}l,&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ and }\beta_{1}\text{ are both odd},\\
\frac{q-2}{2}[(d-1)l+2]+\frac{d-2}{2}l+2,&\text{ if }\alpha_{2}\text{ and }\beta_{1}\text{ are both even}.\end{cases}

Notice that if one of
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}
is even and the other is odd, then it follows that
q\geq 3
. Using this and the above inequalities and equalities it is straightforward to verify that (
3.16
) holds, except for when
d=2
and
q=2
and
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}
are both odd.

Now assume that
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})\neq\varnothing
. Then this is equivalent to
\max(\Xi(T_{j}))\geq\min(\Xi(T_{j+1}))
. Therefore (
3.16
) does not hold and so we may assume that
d=2
and
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{2}+2
and
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}
are both odd. Using (
3.4
) we have that

s_{\alpha_{2}}+n_{\alpha_{2}}-1=\max(\Xi(T_{j}))\geq\min(\Xi(T_{j+1}))=s_{\beta_{1}-1}-(l-l_{\beta_{1}})+1,

or, equivalently, using
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{2}+2
, that

n_{\alpha_{2}}\geq s_{\alpha_{2}+1}-s_{\alpha_{2}}-l+l_{\alpha+2}+2.

Since
s_{\alpha_{2}+1}-s_{\alpha_{2}}=l
, this is equivalent to
n_{\alpha_{2}}>l_{\alpha_{2}+2}+1
, as required.

(b)

Follows immediately by part (a) and the definition of
\Theta_{j}
.∎

Remark 3.27
.

Notice that if
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, then condition (ii) in Lemma
3.26
(a) never occurs by Proposition
3.7
(b1). Hence for
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs, the condition in Lemma
3.26
(b) is always satisfied.

We are now ready to give the definition of a well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pair, which serves as the combinatorial description of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. First let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with diagonal partition
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
. Assume that for each
j\in[1,k]
the sequence
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is an admissible configuration. When this sequence is an admissible configuration of type (III), we have that
T_{j}=\{t_{j}\}
and so
t_{j,1}=t_{j,2}=t_{j}
, and also that
M_{t_{j}}=D_{t_{j}}
. This extreme case plays a special role which is recorded in the following definition.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Let
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be the diagonal partition of
(M,P)
. We say that
(M,P)
is
well-configured
if any of the following conditions hold:

(a)

T=\varnothing
,
R=[1,x]
and
B=[x+1,n]
for some
0\leq x\leq n
, or

(b)

T\neq\varnothing
and the following hold:

(i)

for
1\leq j\leq k
we have that
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration,

(ii)

for
1\leq j\leq k-1
we have that
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{j+1})=\varnothing
,

(iii)

for
0\leq j\leq k
we have that
\Theta_{j}
is either rigid, support, or rigid to support,

(iv)

for
1\leq j\leq k
we have that if
\Xi(T_{j})
is rigid, then
\Theta_{j-1}
is rigid or
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (III), and

(v)

for
1\leq j\leq k
we have that if
\Xi(T_{j})
is support, then
\Theta_{j}
is support or
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (III).

There is admittedly a lot of data in Definition
3.28
, but almost all of it is collected in the much more compact Table
2
. The only exception is condition (ii) in Definition
3.28
(b) which happens only under the extremely specific circumstances described in Lemma
3.26
(a)(ii) and needs to be checked separately. Instead of giving an explicit but cumbersome general algorithm of how to use that table, we illustrate with three representative examples.
