Table 2.
In the table we show the possible direct neighbouring admissible types of a well-configured pair as non-blacked out cells. The rows are the possible type of
T_{i}
and the columns of
T_{i+1}
. For
T_{i}
of type VII and
T_{i+1}
of type VI also the form of
\Xi(T_{\bullet})
is of importance, hence they are specified. The internal cells contain a label denoting the form of
\Theta_{i}
. The labels are given by: ”R” – Rigid, ”S” – Support, ”SR” – Support to Rigid, ”A” – Any of Rigid, Support and Rigid to Support.

Example 3.29
.

(a)

Let
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
be a
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair of
\Lambda(37,4)
. Assume that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
is as in Example
3.25
(a), that is

Assume that
\Xi(T_{1})
and
\Xi(T_{2})
are such that
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (I) and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{5}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{6}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (IV). Table
2
indicates that this is impossible for a well-configured pair as a full admissible configuration of type (IV) can not come immediately after a full admissible configuration of type (I). We conclude that
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
is not well-configured.

(b)

Let us construct a well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pair of
\Lambda(37,4)
by slightly adjusting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}
from the previous example. There are many ways to achieve this. For example, we may add a summand to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{6}}
and change the configuration
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{5}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{6}})
to be a full admissible configuration of type (VI). This gives the following picture:

Consulting Table
2
we see all the possibilities for the sets
R
and
B
. In particular, since we have an admissible configuration of type (I) followed by an admissible configuration of type (VI), we see that
\Xi(T_{2})
needs to be support to rigid and that
\Theta_{1}
has to be rigid. Moreover, since
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}})
is an admissible configuration of type (I), we have that
\Xi(T_{1})
needs to be support. To find the choices for
\Theta_{0}
we may consult the column of Table
2
corresponding to the type of the first admissible configuration we have. As long as the option “A” appears there,
\Theta_{0}
can be anything among rigid, rigid to support at any point in
\Theta_{0}
, and support; otherwise it has to be rigid. In our case we see that we may pick
\Theta_{0}
to be rigid to support at, say,
4\in\Theta_{0}=[1,6]
. Then we obtain the following well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pair:

(c)

Let
d=2
and let us now construct a well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-rigid pair of
\Lambda(34,5)
. In this case we have that
p=10
. We may pick any
i\in[2,10]
for our first admissible configuration to start. Say we pick
i=2
. Then, we may pick any admissible configuration type from the first column of Table
2
. Say we pick an admissible configuration of type (VIII). This covers three diagonals as can be seen in Definition
3.16
and so defines
T_{1}=[2,4]
and also
l_{2}=1
,
l_{3}=6-l_{4}
,
n_{3}=4-n_{2}
,
n_{4}=4
. We may choose, say
n_{2}=1
,
n_{3}=3
,
l_{3}=2
,
l_{4}=4
which defines
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{4}})
.

Next, we must have that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{5}}=0
and so we may pick
i\in[6,10]
for our second admissible configuration to occur, or stop here. We may pick
i=6
and our second admissible configuration to be of type (VII) with, say
l_{6}=l_{7}=1
,
n_{6}=1
and
n_{7}=3
. This defines
T_{2}=[6,7]
and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{6}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{7}})
. Then for the next diagonal we must have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{8}}=0
and so we may choose
i\in[9,10]
for the next admissible configuration to occur, or again stop here.

Say we pick
i=9
. Table
2
reveals that we may pick the next admissible configuration to be of type (III). However, since we are in the case
d=2
, we have to be slightly more careful. Condition (ii) in Definition
3.28
(b) is the only condition not encoded in Table
2
and we have to check that it is not violated. This can be done easily via Lemma
3.26
(a). An admissible configuration of type (III) gives
T_{3}=[9,9]
,
l_{9}=1
and
n_{9}=4
. Then we observe that
n_{7}=3>2=l_{9}+1
, which implies by Lemma
3.26
that
\Xi(T_{2})\cap\Xi(T_{3})\neq\varnothing
. This means that this choice does not lead to a well-configured pair and so we may not pick an admissible configuration of type (III) at this point. We may instead choose the last configuration to be of type (II). Consulting Table
2
as in the previous example reveals the possible nature for the intervals
\Xi(T_{1})
,
\Theta_{1}
,
\Xi(T_{2})
,
\Theta_{2}
,
\Xi(T_{3})
and
\Theta_{3}
. A possible well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pair obtained this way is the following:

In the above examples all well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pairs are maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs with
n=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
summands. Our main aim is to prove that well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pairs are characterized by this property and, furthermore, this property characterizes summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. We start by showing that well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pairs are always
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
(M,P)
be a well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Then
(M,P)
is a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

Proof.

We need to check that the conditions in Proposition
3.7
hold. Let
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be the diagonal partition of
(M,P)
. If
T=\varnothing
then we are in case (a) of Definition
3.28
and clearly
(M,P)=(P(R),P(B))
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid.

Assume now that
T\neq\varnothing
. Then for
1\leq j\leq k
we have that
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration by condition (i) in Definition
3.28
(b). Let
i\in[2,p]
. If
i\in T_{j}
for some
j
, then by Remark
3.17
(b) we have that conditions (b1) and (b2) of Proposition
3.7
hold. If
i\not\in T_{j}
, then
M_{i}=0
and conditions (b1) and (b2) of Proposition
3.7
hold trivially. Hence conditions (b1) and (b2) of Proposition
3.7
hold for all
i\in[2,p]
.

For
0\leq q\leq 2k
we set
X_{q}=\Theta_{\frac{q}{2}}
for
q
even and
X_{q}=\Xi(T_{\frac{q+1}{2}})
for
q
odd. We then set
R_{q}=R\cap X_{q}
and
B_{q}=B\cap X_{q}
. By condition (ii) in Definition
3.28
(b) and Lemma
3.26
we have that
R
is the disjoint union of the sets
R_{0},R_{1},\ldots,R_{2k}
and similarly for
B_{q}
. We first claim that condition (b3) of Proposition
3.7
hold with
R_{q}
respectively
B_{q}
in place of
R
respectively
B
. Indeed, if
q
is even, then this follows by Remark
3.17
(b), while if
q
is odd, this follows since
\Theta_{j}
intersects no interval of the form
B_{i}^{\times}
or
R_{i}^{\times}
by Lemma
3.26
(b). This shows that condition (b3) in Proposition
3.7
holds.

Notice that all of the intervals
X_{q}
are either rigid, support, support to rigid, or rigid to support. It remains to show that condition (a) of Proposition
3.7
holds. Thus let
x\in B
and
y\in R
such that
1\leq x<y\leq n
and we need to find an interval of length
l-1
between them that intersects neither
R
nor
B
. Then
x\in B_{q}
and
y\in R_{q^{\prime}}
for some
q\leq q^{\prime}
. If there exists an interval
X_{q^{\prime\prime}}
with
q\leq q^{\prime\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
and such that
X_{q^{\prime\prime}}
is support to rigid, then we are done. Hence we may assume that for all
q^{\prime\prime}
with
q\leq q^{\prime\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
we have that
X_{q^{\prime\prime}}
is not support to rigid. Since
X_{q}\cap B\neq\varnothing
, we conclude that
X_{q}
is support or rigid to support. Similarly we obtain that
X_{q^{\prime}}
is rigid or rigid to support. Since
x<y
with
x\in B
and
y\in R
, we conclude that
X_{q}\neq X_{q^{\prime}}
. It follows that there exists
r
such that
q\leq r<r+1\leq q^{\prime}
and such that
X_{r}
is support or rigid to support, while
X_{r+1}
is rigid or rigid to support. Assume that
r
is odd. Then
X_{r}=\Xi(T_{\frac{r+1}{2}})
is support, since no interval
\Xi(T_{j})
can be rigid to support by part (v) of Definition
3.28
(b) and the definition of admissible configurations. Similarly we obtain that
X_{r+1}=\Theta_{\frac{r+1}{2}}
is rigid. Hence by condition (v) in Definition
3.28
we obtain that the full admissible configuration corresponding to
T_{\frac{r+1}{2}}
is of type (III). By Remark
3.17
(d) we have that
\Xi(T_{\frac{r+1}{2}})
is the disjoint union of two consecutive intervals of the form
B_{r}
and
B_{i}^{\times}
, with the last one having length
l-1
. Since
X_{r}
is support, it follows that
B_{i}^{\times}\cap R=\varnothing
. This shows the claim and so condition (a) of Proposition
3.7
holds if
r
is odd; the case where
r
is even is similar.
∎

The following statement establishes a crucial bound on the number of indecomposable summands for a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

Proposition 3.31
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with non-diagonal component
(R,B)
. Then

(3.17)

\lvert R\rvert+\lvert B\rvert+\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}\leq n.

Moreover, if equality holds in (
3.17
), then
(M,P)
is well-configured.

Proof.

Let
T
be the diagonal component of
M
. If
T=\varnothing
, then

\lvert R\rvert+\lvert B\rvert+\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}=\lvert R\cup B\rvert=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap[1,n]\rvert\leq n.

Moreover, equality holds only if
(R\cup B)\cap[1,n]=[1,n]
. It is easy to see that this implies that case (a) of Definition
3.28
must be satisfied and so
(M,P)
is well-configured.

Assume that
T\neq\varnothing
. Let
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be the diagonal partition of
(M,P)
. By Remark
3.27
we can apply Lemma
3.26
(b). In particular, condition (ii) of Definition
3.28
(b) holds. Moreover, we have that for
i\neq j
we have
\Xi(T_{i})\cap\Xi(T_{j})=\varnothing
and
\Theta_{i}\cap\Theta_{j}=\varnothing
. We then set

\Xi=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\Xi(T_{j})\text{ and }\Theta=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{k}\Theta_{j}.

By Lemma
3.26
(b) we also have that
[1,n]=\Xi\sqcup\Theta
. Using Proposition
3.22
we have that

\displaystyle\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi(T_{j})\rvert\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\lvert\Xi(T_{j})\rvert-\sum_{i\in T_{j}}m_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert\Xi(T_{j})\rvert-\sum_{i=2,m_{i}\neq 0}^{p}m_{i}=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i},

where if equality holds, then
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration for all
j\in[1,k]
. Hence we have

(3.18)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i},

and equality holds only if condition (i) of Definition
3.28
(b) holds. Using this and
[1,n]=\Xi\sqcup\Theta
, we have

(3.19)

\lvert R\rvert+\lvert B\rvert=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap[1,n]\rvert=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Theta\rvert\leq\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}+\lvert\Theta\rvert=n-\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i},

which shows (
3.17
). Moreover, equality in (
3.19
) holds only if, in addition to equality in (
3.18
), we have that

(3.20)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Theta\rvert=\lvert\Theta\rvert.

Hence it is enough to show that if (
3.20
) holds, then conditions (iii), (iv) and (v) of Definition
3.28
are satisfied. Let
0\leq j\leq k
and we first claim that there exists no interval
I\subseteq\Theta_{j}
which is support to rigid. Indeed, applying Proposition
3.7
(a) to such an interval
I
gives the existence of a subset
Z\subsetneq\Theta_{j-1}
of length
l-1
with
(R\cup B)\cap Z=\varnothing
, which contradicts (
3.20
). Since (
3.20
) holds and
\Theta_{j}
contains no interval which is support to rigid, it follows that
\Theta_{j}
is rigid, support or support to rigid. This shows condition (iii) of Definition
3.28
. Let us now only show that condition (iv) of Definition
3.28
is satisfied, as the proof for condition (v) is similar.

Assume then that (
3.18
) holds, that
\Xi(T_{j})
is rigid and that
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration of any type other than (III) and we show that
\Theta_{j-1}
is rigid. Assume to a contradiction that
\Theta_{j-1}
is not rigid. In particular,
\Theta_{j-1}
is non-empty since the empty interval is rigid. Since we have shown that condition (iii) of Definition
3.28
holds, it follows that
\Theta_{j-1}
is either support or rigid to support. In any case, we have that
\max(\Theta_{j-1})\in B
. Now consider the interval
\Xi(T_{j})
. By construction, it starts at the interval
R_{t_{j,1}}^{\times}=[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]
. Since
\Xi(T_{j})
is rigid, we have that
\rho_{2}+1\in R
. By Lemma
3.26
(b) we have that
\max(\Theta_{j-1})=\rho_{1}-1
. Since
\max(\Theta_{j-1})\in B
, we conclude that
\rho_{1}-1\in B
. Then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P(\rho_{1}-1),P(\rho_{2}+1)\right)=0
, and so we conclude that
\rho_{2}+1-(\rho_{1}-1)\geq l
. Then

l-l_{t_{j,1}}=\lvert R_{t_{j,1}}^{\times}\rvert=\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}+1\geq l-1,

and so
l_{t_{j,1}}\leq 1
. Since
1\leq l_{t_{j,1}}
holds by Lemma
3.4
, we have that
(M_{t_{j,1}},\ldots,M_{t_{j,2}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (I), (V), (VII) or (VIII) (since we have assumed it cannot be of type (III)). But in none of these cases is the interval
\Xi(T_{j})
rigid. Hence we reach a contradiction and we conclude that
\Theta_{j-1}
is rigid as required.
∎

Corollary 3.32
.

Let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Then
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert\leq\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
3.31
we have

\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert P(R)\rvert+\left\lvert\left(\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i}\right)\right\rvert+\lvert P(B)\rvert=\lvert R\rvert+\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}+\lvert B\rvert\leq n=\lvert\Lambda\rvert.\qed

We can now formulate our main result for this section.

Theorem 3.33
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

(b)

(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

(c)

(M,P)
is well-configured.

Proof.

(a) implies (b): If (a) holds, then by the definition of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs we have that
\lvert\Lambda\rvert\leq\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
. By Corollary
3.32
we obtain that
\lvert\Lambda\rvert=\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
.

(b) implies (a): Let
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
be any
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Then by Corollary
3.32
we have that
\lvert M^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert P^{\prime}\rvert\leq\lvert\Lambda\rvert=\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
. Since
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
was an arbitrary
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, it follows that
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair.

(b) implies (c): If (b) holds, then we have equality in (
3.17
). It follows that
(M,P)
is well-configured by Proposition
3.31
.

(c) implies (b): Assume now that
(M,P)
is well-configured. Then
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair by Proposition
3.30
. It remains to show that
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. Let
T
be the diagonal component of
M
. If
T=\varnothing
, then by Definition
3.28
(a) we have
(M,P)=(P(R),P(B))
and
\lvert R\rvert+\lvert B\rvert=n=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
, as required.

Assume now that
T\neq 0
and let
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be the diagonal partition of
(M,P)
. Then conditions (i)—(v) of Definition
3.28
(b) are satisfied. Let

\Xi=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\Xi(T_{j})\text{ and }\Theta=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{k}\Theta_{j}.

Then condition (ii) of Definition
3.28
(b) allows us to apply Lemma
3.26
(b),
so that
[1,n]=\Xi\sqcup\Theta
. By the definition of the intervals
\Xi
and
\Theta
, we have that
\Theta_{j}\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\varnothing=\Theta_{j}\cap B_{i}^{\times}
for any
i\in[2,p]
. Hence condition (iii) of Definition
3.28
(b) gives that

(3.21)

(R\cup B)\cap\Theta=\Theta.

Now let
1\leq j\leq k
. By Remark
3.17
(c) we have that

(3.22)

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi(T_{j})\rvert+\sum_{i\in T_{j}}m_{i}=\lvert\Xi(T_{j})\rvert.

Using (
3.22
) we have

\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi(T_{j})\rvert=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\lvert\Xi(T_{j})\rvert-\sum_{i\in T_{j}}m_{i}\right)=\lvert\Xi\rvert-\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}.

Using this and (
3.21
) we have

\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap[1,n]\rvert+\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}=\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Theta\rvert+\lvert(R\cup B)\cap\Xi\rvert+\sum_{i=2}^{p}m_{i}=\lvert\Theta\rvert+\lvert\Xi\rvert=n,

which shows that (b) holds.
∎

3.4.1.
The case
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)

We finish this section by applying Theorem
3.33
to count the summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in the special case when
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
.

Remark 3.34
.

(a)

Assume that
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda(n,l))
and that
\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Then
p=2
by Remark
2.7
, and consequently the diagonal partition of a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
is either empty or consists only of
T_{1}=\{2\}
. In the first case we have that
[1,n]=\Phi_{0}
is either rigid, support or rigid to support, providing
2+(n-1)
possible pairs. In the second case we have three possible subcases:

(1)

(M_{2})
is full admissible of type (I) with
\Xi_{2}=[1,n]
rigid,

(2)

(M_{2})
is full admissible of type (II) with
\Xi_{2}=[1,n]
support, and

(3)

(M_{2})
is full admissible of type (III) with
\Xi_{2}=[1,n]
rigid, support or support to rigid at some
x\in[1,n-l]
.

Each of (1) and (2) provides
l-2
different pairs, and (3) provides
2+(n-l)
pairs. In total we then have
2n+l-1
different summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs of
\Lambda(n,l)
when
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
.

(b)

To illustrate the exponential rate of growth in the number of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs, let us note that through a bit more work in the same spirit as in part (a) we can calculate that for
p=4
and either
(d>2
and
l>2)
or
(d=2
and
l=3)
there exists exactly

\frac{1}{27}\left(-19l^{3}+18l^{2}n+144l^{2}-6ln^{2}-36ln-126l+2n^{3}+36n^{2}+72n-135\right)

summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

4.
d
-torsion classes

Aim.

In this section we once again fix an algebra
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
that satisfies the conditions of Theorem
2.6
and so admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Our aim now is to classify
d
-torsion classes of
\Lambda
. These are subcategories
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
of
\Lambda
which serve as a higher analogue of torsion classes and are defined by certain homological properties.

Local restrictions on
d
-torsion classes.

Our basic strategy is in principle similar to our strategy for summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. That is, we first find some local conditions that must be satisfied for a subcategory
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
to be a
d
-torsion class, and then we find how these local conditions must be joined together. Before we proceed, let us give a brief overview of this strategy.

To describe the local condition, assume that we know that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is a
d
-torsion class and let us consider the subcategory
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}
consisting of all modules in
\mathcal{U}
which lie in the diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. First we can notice that
\mathcal{U}_{i}
must have a specific form:
\mathcal{U}_{i}
must be one of
0
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(x)
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(y)
or
\mathcal{D}_{i}
(with some additional restrictions depending on the parity of
i
). Additionally, knowing the type of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
places restrictions on the type of
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
as well. Finally, the information of the type of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
restricts which indecomposable projective-injective modules with top between
s_{i}+a
and
s_{i+1}+b
can be included in
\mathcal{U}
(the
a
and
b
can be made precise and depend only on the parity of
i
).

Using the local restrictions to construct
d
-torsion classes.

Given this local behaviour, we can construct a
d
-torsion class by choosing a possible
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, then a suitable
\mathcal{U}_{2}
and a compatible collection of indecomposable projective-injective modules in-between the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, then a suitable
\mathcal{U}_{3}
(which depends only on
\mathcal{U}_{2}
) and so on. We then collect all of this information about which type of diagonal may follow which one in the graph (
4.2
), where the labels of the arrows inform us which choices we have for the indecomposable projective-injective modules in between two diagonals. Thus, paths in the graph (
4.2
) with
p-1
arrows (and so going throug
p
vertices, which correspond to
p
diagonals) which start in a suitable orientation for
\mathcal{U}_{1}
give
d
-torsion classes, and we prove that each
d
-torsion class may be constructed in this way.

A special case.

Along the way there are again a few special considerations when
d=2
. Furthermore, the case
l=2
is quite degenerate as all of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(x)
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(y)
are the same and since the parity of
i
does not matter and so it is dealt with in a separate small section.

Although again there is some notation in this section, it is much lighter than Section
3
. Still the reader is reminded to make use of the index and the pictures at the end of the article in case it is needed.

4.1.
Preliminaries

Let
d\geq 1
be a positive integer. We start by recalling some notions related to
d
-torsion classes from
[
10
]
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category. Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism in
\mathcal{A}
. Then
f
is called
left minimal
if any endomorphism
g
of
B
satisfying
g\circ f=f
is an isomorphism. A morphism
g:B\to C
in
\mathcal{A}
is called a
weak cokernel of
f
if
g\circ f=0
and for any
h:B\to D
with
h\circ f=0
, there exists a morphism
j:C\to D
such that
j\circ g=h
; if moreover the morphism
j
is unique, then
g:B\to C
is called a
cokernel
and
C
is unique up to an isomorphism. We say that a morphism
g:B\to C
is a
weak cokernel in
\mathcal{A}
if there exists another morphism
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
g
is a weak cokernel of
f
. A
d
-cokernel
of a morphism
f_{0}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
is a sequence of morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d+1}\mathrlap{,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

such that
f_{i}
is a weak cokernel of
f_{i-1}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
and
f_{d}
is a cokernel of
f_{d-1}
. The notions of
right minimal
,
weak kernel
,
kernel
and
d
-kernel
are defined dually. A sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

is called a
d
-extension
if
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
is a
d
-cokernel of
f_{0}
and
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d-1})
is a
d
-kernel of
f_{d}
. Such a
d
-extension is said to be
equivalent
to a
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\mathrlap{,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d}}

if there exists a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d}}

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra and let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory for some
d\geq 1
. Then the notion of equivalence of
d
-extensions in
\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence relation by
[
31
, Proposition 4.10]
. Generalizing the classical notion of torsion classes to higher homological algebra, Jørgensen introduced the following notion.

Definition 4.1
.

[
32
, Definition 1.1]

We say that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is a
d
-torsion class
if for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
there exists a
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-1}}

such that
U\in\mathcal{U}
and, for every
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, the induced sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{d}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is exact.

Note that a
d
-torsion class is closed under direct sums and summands by
[
32
, Lemma 2.7(iii)]
.

Recently, an equivalent characterization of
d
-torsion classes was given in
[
10
]
. We introduce some more notions necessary to recall that result.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a subcategory. We say that
\mathcal{U}
is
closed under
d
-quotients
if for any morphism
f:M\to U
with
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists a
d
-cokernel

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

of
f
, where
U_{1},\ldots,U_{d}\in\mathcal{U}
. We recall the following results from
[
10
]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

[
10
, Construction 2.9]

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. Let
c:Y\to M
be the cokernel of
f
in
\m\Lambda
. Let
g:M\to C
be a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
M
. Then
g\circ c
is a left minimal weak cokernel of
f
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Assume that
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
\pi_{X}:X\to X/\mathrm{Soc}(X)
be the canonical epimorphism and let
g_{0}:X/\mathrm{Soc}(X)\to C
be the minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
X/\mathrm{Soc}(X)
. Then
g=g_{0}\circ\pi_{X}
is a left minimal weak cokernel in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi:P\to\mathrm{Soc}(X)
be the projective cover of
\mathrm{Soc}(X)
and
\iota:\mathrm{Soc}(X)\to X
be the canonical inclusion. Then
\pi_{X}:X\to X/\mathrm{Soc}(X)
is the cokernel of
\iota\circ\pi
, and so the claim follows by Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

[
10
, Lemma 3.14]

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a subcategory which is closed under
d
-quotients. Let
g:M\to N
be a left minimal weak cokernel in
\mathcal{C}
. If
M\in\mathcal{U}
, then
N\in\mathcal{U}
.

We say that
\mathcal{U}
is
closed under
d
-extensions
if for any
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

in
\mathcal{C}
with
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
there exists an equivalent
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{d}}

where
U_{1},\ldots,U_{d}\in\mathcal{U}
. With this, we can now recall the following from
[
10
]
.

Theorem 4.5
.

[
10
, Theorem 1.1]

A subcategory
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is a
d
-torsion class if and only if it is closed under
d
-quotients,
d
-extensions and direct summands.

In fact, we want to use a slightly modified but equivalent description of
d
-torsion classes. A
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

in
\mathcal{C}
is called
minimal
if
f_{i}\in\mathrm{Rad}_{\m\Lambda}{(C_{i},C_{i+1})}
for
i=1,\ldots,d-1
, where

\mathrm{Rad}_{\m\Lambda}{(M,N)}=\{f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,N\right)\mid 1_{X}-g\circ f\text{ is invertible for any $g\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,M\right)$}\}

defines the
Jacobson radical
of
\m\Lambda
. We say that
\mathcal{U}
is
closed under minimal
d
-extensions
if for any minimal
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

in
\mathcal{C}
with
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
, we have that
C_{1},\ldots,C_{d}\in\mathcal{U}
. We then have the following.

Proposition 4.6
.

\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is a
d
-torsion class if and only if it is closed under
d
-quotients, minimal
d
-extensions and direct summands.

Proof.

By
[
25
, Proposition 2.4]
we have that every
d
-extension in
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to a minimal
d
-extension and, moreover, that this minimal
d
-extension appears as a direct summand in any
d
-extension in its equivalence class. The claim follows.
∎

For further details about
d
-torsion classes we refer to
[
32
]
and
[
10
]
.

For the rest of this section we fix a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
which admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
for some integer
d\geq 2
. Our aim is to classify the
d
-torsion classes of
\Lambda
. As
d
-torsion classes are closed under direct sums and summands, for the rest of this section all subcategories are closed under direct sums. In practice, this means that we describe subcategories using the indecomposable modules lying in them.

We find the classification of
d
-torsion classes to be somewhat less complicated in comparison to our previous classification of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pairs. Moreover, many of the computations are of a similar flavor to the ones already performed in this article, and so we leave some details to the reader.

We may notice that any nonzero endomorphism of an indecomposable module in
\m\Lambda
is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.4
. It follows that any nonzero morphism
f:M\to N
with
M
and
N
indecomposable is left minimal. We use this fact throughout.

4.2.
Weak cokernels in
\mathcal{C}

In this small section we compute some weak cokernels in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
M=M(a,b)\in\m\Lambda
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
be indecomposable. Assume that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,C\right)\neq 0
. Then one of the following holds.

(a)

C
is isomorphic to one of the indecomposable injective modules
I(a+j)
for
0\leq j\leq b-a
.

(b)

C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
for some odd
i
and
s_{i}-l+1\leq a\leq s_{i}\leq b\leq s_{i}+l-1
.

(c)

C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
for some even
i
and
s_{i}-l+1\leq a\leq b\leq s_{i}
.

Proof.

This follows by a direct computation using the description of
\mathcal{C}
(see for example (
2.6
)).
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
M=M(a,b)\in\m\Lambda
be indecomposable and assume that
M(a,b)\not\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
I=M(a,a+l-1)
be the injective envelope of
M
and
\iota:M\to I
be the canonical inclusion. Let
i\in[1,p]
be minimal such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\mathcal{D}_{i}\right)\neq 0
, if such
i
exists, and
i=0
otherwise. Then exactly one of the following hold.

(a)

i=0
. Then
\iota:M\to I
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation.

(b)

i\neq 0
is odd. Then
N=M(s_{i},b)
is in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
\iota\oplus\pi_{N}:M\to I\oplus N
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation, where
\pi_{N}:M\to N
is the canonical projection.

(c)

i\neq 0
is even and
N=M(a,s_{i})
is in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Then the canonical inclusion
\iota_{N}:M\to N
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation.

(d)

i\neq 0
is even and
M(a,s_{i})
is not in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Then
\iota:M\to I
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation.

Proof.

This is a direct computation using Lemma
4.7
and the description of
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

(a)

Let
2\leq i\leq p
be even and
M=M(a,s_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,s_{i}-a+1\}
and

N=M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1+j).

Then there exists in
\mathcal{C}
a minimal
d
-extension of the form

0
M
I(a)\oplus M(a+j,s_{i})
I(a+j)
I(a+l)
I(a+l+j)
\dots
I(a+\tfrac{d-4}{2}l+j)
I(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+j)
I(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+j)\oplus M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)
N
0
f_{0}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
f_{d-3}
f_{d-2}
f_{d-1}
f_{d}

In particular, if
j=1
, then
\tau_{d}^{-}(M)=M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l)
and so this gives a minimal
d
-extension between
M
and
\tau_{d}^{-}(M)
.

(b)

Let
1\leq i\leq p-1
be odd and
M=M(s_{i},b)\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,l-1+s_{i}-b\}
and

N={M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+1+j,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)}.

Then there exists in
\mathcal{C}
a minimal
d
-extension of the form

0
M
M(s_{i},b+j)
I(b+1)
I(b+j+1)
I(b+l+1)
\dots
I(a+\tfrac{d-4}{2}l+1)
I(a+\tfrac{d-4}{2}l+j+1)
M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+1,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)
N
0
f_{0}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
f_{d-3}
f_{d-2}
f_{d-1}
f_{d}

In particular, if
j=1
, then
\tau_{d}^{-}(M)=M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)
and so this gives a minimal
d
-extension between
M
and
\tau_{d}^{-}(M)
.

Proof.

We only show part (a) as part (b) can be shown similarly.
Notice that all the modules appearing in the sequence in (a) belong to
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed, the injective modules clearly belong to
\mathcal{C}
, the modules
M
and
M(a+j,s_{i})
belong to
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, while the modules
N
and
M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)=M(s_{i+1},a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)
belong to
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. Consider the following sequence of morphisms

0
M=M(a,s_{i},
)
I(a)
M(a+j,s_{i})
M(a+j,a+l-1)
I(a+j)
M(a+l,a+l+j-1)
I(a+l)
M(a+l+j,a+2l-1)
I(a+l+j)
\cdots
\cdots
I(a+\tfrac{d-4}{2}l+j)
M(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l,a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+j-1)
I(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l)
M(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+j,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)
M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,a+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)
I(a+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+j)
N
0
-\iota_{0}
\pi_{0}
f_{1}^{1}
f_{1}^{2}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
f_{d-3}
f_{d-2}
f_{d-1}^{1}
f_{d-1}^{2}
-\pi_{d}
\iota_{d}

where each morphism not labeled by
f
is a canonical (up to the noted signs) inclusion or projection, and the morphisms labeled by
f
are the compositions of these morphisms. Set
f_{0}=(-\iota_{0})\oplus\pi_{0}
, set
f_{1}=f_{1}^{1}\oplus f_{1}^{2}
, set
f_{d-1}=f_{d-1}^{1}\oplus f_{d-1}^{2}
and set
f_{d}=(-\pi_{d})\oplus\iota_{d}
. Using Lemma
4.8
it is easy to check that for
1\leq i\leq d
, each
f_{i}
decomposes as the composition of the cokernel of
f_{i-1}
with a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of this cokernel. By Lemma
4.2
we have that
(f_{1},\dots,f_{d})
is a
d
-cokernel of
f_{0}
. Dually it can be shown that
(f_{1},\dots,f_{d-1})
is a
d
-kernel of
f_{d}
. This shows that the given sequence is a
d
-extension. Moreover, there are no nonzero morphism in the given sequence going the opposite direction, which shows that each
f_{i}
is a radical morphism. This shows that this
d
-extension is minimal.
∎

Example 4.10
.

Let
d=4
and
l=4
. The following picture give examples of minimal
d
-extensions between consecutive diagonals in this case which can be obtained via Lemma
4.9
.

The modules appearing in the minimal
d
-extensions are given as yellow-encircled points and the morphisms are obtained by composing the morphisms in the pictures.

4.3.
Classification of
d
-torsion classes

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be an (additive and full) subcategory. Let
1\leq i\leq p
. We set
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}
. We further set
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq[1,n-(l-1)]
to be the index set of the projective-injective indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{U}
, that is, for
q\in[1,n-l+1]
we have that
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}
if and only if
q\in Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. Then we have that

\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{add}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{p},I(Q_{\mathcal{U}})\right).

Hence a subcategory
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is described uniquely via the data
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{p},Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. Using the results of paragraph
4.2
, we can obtain a lot of information about the data
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{p},Q_{\mathcal{U}}
when
\mathcal{U}
is a
d
-torsion class. The following three lemmas collect all of this information.

For the convenience of the reader, we recall that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
(respectively
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
) is the additive closure of all indecomposable modules in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
with length at most (respectively at least)
h
. Recall also that if
i
is odd (respectively even), then
M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)
(respectively
M(s_{i}-h+1,s_{i})
) is the unique indecomposable module in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
of length
h
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class in
\mathcal{C}
and let
1\leq i\leq p
.

(a)

Assume that
i
is even and let
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. If
M(s_{i}-h+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
, then
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

(b)

Assume that
i
is odd and let
I(q)
be an indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-module with
s_{i}\leq q\leq s_{i+1}-(l-1)
. If
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}
, then
[q,s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
.

(c)

Assume that
i
is even and let
I(q)
be an indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-module with
s_{i}-(l-2)\leq q\leq s_{i+1}-1
. If
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}
, then
[q,s_{i+1}-1]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. If moreover
s_{i+1}-(l-1)\leq q
, then
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
where
h=l+q-s_{i+1}
.

(d)

Assume that
i
is odd and let
M(s_{i},b)\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
be indecomposable with
b>s_{i}
. If
M(s_{i},b)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
, then
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
.

Proof.

(a)

If
l=2
there is nothing to show. Otherwise let
a=s_{i}-h+1
and it is enough to show that
M(a+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. We have that
M(a+1,s_{i})=M(a,s_{i})/\mathrm{Soc}(M(a,s_{i}))
. Moreover, since
M(a+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that the minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
M(a+1,s_{i})
is the identity morphism. It follows by Corollary
4.3
that the canonical epimorphism
M(a,s_{i})\to M(a+1,s_{i})
is a left minimal weak cokernel in
\mathcal{C}
. We conclude that
M(a+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
by Lemma
4.4
.

(b)

We first show the claim for
Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. It is enough to show that
I(q+1)\in\mathcal{U}
when
q+1\leq s_{i+1}-(l-1)
. We have that
I(q)/\mathrm{Soc}(I(q))=M(q+1,q+l-1)
. Let
\pi:I(q)\to M(q+1,q+l-1)
be the canonical epimorphism. Using
q+1\leq s_{i+1}-(l-1)
it is easy to see that
M(q+1,q+l-1)
satisfies the assumptions of either (a) or (d) of Lemma
4.8
. Hence Lemma
4.8
gives that the canonical inclusion
\iota:M(q+1,q+l-1)\to I(q+1)
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
M(q+1,q+l-1)
. It follows by Corollary
4.3
that
g=\iota\circ\pi
is a left minimal weak cokernel in
\mathcal{C}
. We conclude that
I(q+1)\in\mathcal{U}
by Lemma
4.4
.

Next notice that
I(s_{i+1}-(l-1))
admits an epimorphism to any indecomposable module in
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
and so, similarly to part (a), we obtain that
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
always holds, the claim follows.

(c)

We first show the claim for
Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. If
q<s_{i+1}-(l-1)
, then the proof is similar to the previous case. Hence we may assume that

(4.1)

s_{i+1}-(l-1)\leq q<q+1\leq s_{i+1}-1.

Again we let
\pi:I(q)\to M(q+1,q+l-1)
be the canonical epimorphism of
I(q)
to
I(q)/\mathrm{Soc}(I(q))
and we let
\iota:M(q+1,q+l-1)\to I(q+1)
be the canonical inclusion. Using (
4.1
) it is easy to see that
M(q+1,q+l-1)
satisfies the assumptions of (b) of Lemma
4.8
. Let
N=M(s_{i+1},q+l-1)
and let
\pi_{N}:M(q+1,q+l-1)\to N
be the canonical projection. Then Lemma
4.8
gives that
\iota\oplus\pi_{N}:M(q+1,q+l-1)\to I(q+1)\oplus N
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
M(q+1,q+l-1)
. We conclude that
I(q+1)\in\mathcal{U}
by Lemma
4.4
.

Next notice that the above also shows that
M(s_{i+1},q^{\prime}+l-1)\in\mathcal{U}
for
q^{\prime}\in[q,s_{i+1}-1]
and so
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(l+q-s_{i+1})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
as well.

(d)

Since
b>s_{i}
, we have that
M(s_{i},b)
is not simple. Hence it is easy to see using Lemma
4.8
that
I(s_{i}+1)
is a minimal left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation of
M(s_{i},b)\to M(s_{i},b)/S(s_{i})
. Thus
I(s_{i}+1)
is a weak cokernel in
\mathcal{C}
and so
I(s_{i}+1)\in\mathcal{U}
by Lemma
4.4
. By part (b) we obtain that
[s_{i}+1,s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. It remains to show that
I(s_{i})=M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-1)\in\mathcal{U}
. Set
j=l-1+s_{i}-b
. Then by Lemma
4.9
(b) we obtain a minimal
d
-extension between
M=M(s_{i},b)
and
N=M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+1+j,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)
. By Proposition
4.6
we have that all middle terms in this minimal
d
-extension are in
\mathcal{U}
, and so we obtain that
M(s_{i},b+j)=M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-1)=I(s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}
, as required.∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class and let
1\leq i\leq p-1
.

(a)

Assume that
i
is even. Assume also that
M=M(s_{i}-h+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
and that
\tau_{d}^{-}(M)\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
and
[s_{i}-h+1,s_{i+1}-1]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. Moreover, we also have that
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(u)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
where

u=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if $d>2$ or $h\in\{l-2,l-1\}$,}\\
l-h-1,&\mbox{if $d=2$ and $h\leq l-3$.}\end{cases}

(b)

Assume that
i
is odd. Assume also that
M=M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
and that
M^{\prime}=M(s_{i+1}-h^{\prime}+1,s_{i+1})\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-h\}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
,
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
and
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

(a)

By Lemma
4.11
(a) we have that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Since
M,\tau_{d}^{-}(M)\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}
is closed under minimal
d
-extensions by Proposition
4.6
, Lemma
4.9
(a) gives that
I(s_{i}-h+1)\in\mathcal{U}
. By Lemma
4.11
(c) we conclude that
[s_{i}-h+1,s_{i+1}-1]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
.

Now let

q=\begin{cases}s_{i+1}-(l-1),&\mbox{if $d>2$ or $h\in\{l-2,l-1\}$,}\\
s_{i}-h+1,&\mbox{if $d=2$ and $h\leq l-3$.}\end{cases}

We claim that
s_{i}-h+1\leq q
. Clearly this holds in the second case. In the first case, using (
2.4
), we have that
s_{i}-h+1\leq q
if and only if

h+2+\frac{d-2}{2}l\geq l.

This inequality clearly holds if
d\geq 4
or
h=l-2
or
h=l-1
. It also holds if
d=2
since in this case
l=2
. Hence the claim is shown and
s_{i}-h+1\leq q
. Similarly we can show that
s_{i+1}-(l-1)\leq q
. Then the second part of Lemma
4.11
(c) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(l+q-s_{i+1})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Noticing that
u=l+q-s_{i+1}
completes the proof.

(b)

Set
b=s_{i}+h-1
and
j=s_{i}-b+l-h^{\prime}
. Using
1\leq h^{\prime}\leq l-h
we can show that
1\leq j\leq s_{i}-b+l-1
. By Lemma
4.9
(b) there exists a minimal
d
-extension between
M(s_{i},b)
and

M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+1+j,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)=M(s_{i+1}-h^{\prime}+1,s_{i+1})=M^{\prime}.

Since
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}
is closed under minimal
d
-extensions by Proposition
4.6
, we obtain using the minimal
d
-extension in
4.9
(b) that
M(s_{i},b+j)=M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Since
1\leq h\leq l-h^{\prime}
, we have that
s_{i}+l-h^{\prime}>s_{i}
and so Lemma
4.11
(d) gives that
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Let
M(s_{i},s_{i}+h+k-1)\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
for some
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-1-h\}
and it is enough to show that
M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1+k)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then
M^{\prime\prime}=M(b+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+1+k,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l)\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. By Lemma
4.9
(b) we have a minimal
d
-extension between
M
and
M^{\prime\prime}
and so by Proposition
4.6
we obtain that
M(s_{i},b+k)=M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1+k)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
, as required. ∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a
d
-torsion class and let
1\leq i\leq p
.

(a)

Assume
i
is even. Then
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
for some
h\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-1\}
.

(b)

Assume
i
is odd. Then
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
.

Proof.

(a)

If
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\{0\}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
. Assume now that
\mathcal{U}_{i}\neq\{0\}
. Let
M(s_{i}-h+1,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
be the indecomposable module of largest length
h
in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
. By Lemma
4.11
(a) we also have that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
and so
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\{0\}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
. Assume now that
\mathcal{U}_{i}\neq\{0\}
. Let
M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
be the indecomposable module of smallest length
h
in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
. We consider the cases
h>1
and
h=1
separately.

If
h>1
, then by Lemma
4.11
(d) we also have that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. In particular, we obtain that
\tau_{d}^{-}(M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1))\in\mathcal{U}
. Then Lemma
4.12
(b) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
and so
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
.

If
h=1
, then
M(s_{i},s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
then we are done. Now assume that there exists
h^{\prime}>1
such that
M(s_{i},s_{i}+h^{\prime}-1)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then Lemma
4.11
(d) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. In particular,
\tau_{d}^{-}(M(s_{i},s_{i}))\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Since we also have
M(s_{i},s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
, Lemma
4.12
(b) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(1)\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, and so we conclude that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(1)
. ∎

We illustrate how we can use the previous lemmas via the following example.

Example 4.14
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(23,4)
and
d=4
so that
\Lambda
admits a
4
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Let us construct the smallest
4
-torsion class
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
that contains the following encircled modules:

Applying Lemma
4.11
(b) and (c) we obtain that the following modules are also included in
\mathcal{U}
:

Next, applying Lemma
4.11
(d) and then Lemma
4.11
(c)
again we can further enlarge our collection of modules in
\mathcal{U}
as follows:

Next using the two rightmost diagonals we can apply Lemma
4.12
(b) to obtain the following collection of modules which must be in
\mathcal{U}
:

We can not get any more modules that must be included in
\mathcal{U}
using our previous results. Indeed, we can notice that the encircled part is a
4
-torsion class and thus it is equal to
\mathcal{U}
.

Our aim is to show that we can compute all
d
-torsion classes in a way similar to Example
4.14
. The cases
l=2
is a very degenerate version of the case
l>2
, but for technical reasons we study them separately.

4.3.1.
The case
l>2
.

Our first aim is to describe all possible pairs
(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})
given that
\mathcal{U}
is a
d
-torsion class. We have already done most of the work in the lemmas
4.11
,
4.12
and
4.13
, and so our work here consists mostly of collecting all of the information in a cleaner way.

Note first that the pairs depend on the parity of
i
and so we have two separate cases. Additionally, we also want to describe the intersection
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
when
i
is odd and the intersection
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
when
i
is even, consisting of all indices
q
such that
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}
. The following two lemmas contain all of this information.

Lemma 4.15
.

Assume that
l>2
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class and let
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
be odd. Then exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})\in\{(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)),(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(h)),(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)),\mid 1\leq h\leq l-2\}

and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
.

(b)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i+1}-(l-1)-(y-1),s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
.

(c)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h),\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}

and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
and that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-1\}
. We consider three cases.

Assume first that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}\neq\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
, then Lemma
4.11
(b) gives that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
, and we are in case (a). Assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. If
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
, then we set
y=0
and we are in case (b). Otherwise, set
m=\min\{Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\}
and set
y=s_{i+1}-(l-1)-(m-1)
. It easily follows that
1\leq y\leq\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2
. By Lemma
4.11
(b) we have that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[m,s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i+1}-(l-1)-(y-1),s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
and so we are in case (b) again.

Assume now that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
. Assume to a contradiction that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. Then both
M(s_{i},s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
and
M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1})\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
hold. Then Lemma
4.12
(b) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(1)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}
, which contradicts
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
since
l>2
. Hence
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
and so
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
by Lemma
4.11
(b). Therefore we are in case (a).

Finally assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h)
for some
1\leq h\leq l-1
. By Lemma
4.11
(d) we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. Since
M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-2)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
by assumption and since
\tau_{d}^{-}(M(s_{i},s_{i}+l-2))\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
, we obtain by Lemma
4.12
(b) that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
and so we are in case (c).
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Assume that
l>2
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class and let
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
be even. Then exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})\in\{(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)),(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(1)),(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)),(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(1)),(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0))\mid 1\leq h\leq l-2\}
and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
.

(b)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h))
for some
h\in\{2,\ldots,l-1\}
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-h\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-1-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
.

(c)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,\tfrac{d}{2}l\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-1-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
.

(d)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h),\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-2\}
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-h-1\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-h-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
.

(e)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
.

(f)

d=2
,
(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(u))
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-3\}
and
u\in\{2,\ldots,l-h-1\}
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-(h+u)-1\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-h-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
for some
h\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-1\}
and that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(1)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. We consider three cases.

Assume first that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}\neq\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
, then Lemma
4.11
(c) gives that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
, and we are in case (a). Assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. If
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
, set
y=0
and we are in one of the cases (b) and (c). Otherwise, set
m=\min\{Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]\}
and set
y=s_{i+1}-1-(m-1)
. Then by Lemma
4.11
(c) we have that

Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[m,s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-1-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1].

If
h^{\prime}=1
, then
s_{i}-(l-2)\leq m\leq s_{i+1}-1
gives that
1\leq y\leq\tfrac{d}{2}l
and so we are in case (c). If
h^{\prime}\geq 2
, then the second part of Lemma
4.11
(c) gives that
s_{i+1}-(l-h^{\prime})\leq m\leq s_{i+1}-1
, which gives that
1\leq y\leq l-h^{\prime}
and so we are in case (b).

Assume now that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h)
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-2\}
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}\neq\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
, then Lemma
4.11
(c) gives that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
, and we are in case (a). Assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. We claim that
h^{\prime}\leq l-h-1
. Indeed, assume to a contradiction that
h^{\prime}>l-h-1
. Then
l-h^{\prime}\leq h
and so
M(s_{i}-(l-h^{\prime}-1),s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then
\tau_{d}^{-}(M(s_{i}-(l-h^{\prime}-1),s_{i}))=M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+(h^{\prime}-1))\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is closed under minimal
d
-extensions, Lemma
4.9
(a) gives that

M(s_{i}+\frac{d-2}{2}l+2,s_{i}-(l-h^{\prime}-1)+\frac{d}{2}l-1)=M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+(h^{\prime}-1)-1)\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1},

contradicting that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
. Hence indeed
h^{\prime}\leq l-h-1
. It follows that
M(s_{i}-(h-1),s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
and

\tau_{d}^{-}(M(s_{i}-(h-1),s_{i}))=M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+(l-h-1))\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}.

Then Lemma
4.12
(a) gives that
[s_{i}-h+1,s_{i+1}-1]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}}
. Set
m=\min\{Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i}-h]\}
and
y=s_{i}-(h-1)-m
. Then
s_{i}-(l-2)\leq m\leq s_{i}-h
, which in turn gives that
0\leq y\leq l-h-1
. Using Lemma
4.11
(c), we have that

Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[m,s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-h-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1].

Now assume that
d>2
or
h=l-2
. Then Lemma
4.12
gives that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. Clearly, the same is true if
h^{\prime}=1
. Hence in this case we are in case (d). Thus assume that
d=2
and
h\leq l-3
and
h^{\prime}>1
. Set
u=h^{\prime}
. Then
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\uparrow}(u)
, which implies that
M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+u-1)\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
and
M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+u-2)\not\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. In particular, we have that
I(s_{i+1}+u-2-l+1)\not\in\mathcal{U}
since there exists an epimorphism
I(s_{i+1}+u-l-1)\to M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+u-2)
. We conclude that
s_{i+1}+u-l\leq m
. Using
m=s_{i}-(h-1)-y
and
s_{i+1}-s_{i}=2
by (
2.4
), we obtain

y=s_{i}-(h-1)-m\leq s_{i}-(h-1)-s_{i+1}-u+l=l-(h+u)-1,

showing that we are in case (f).

Finally assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
, then we have that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
by Lemma
4.11
(c) and so we are in case (a). Assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}\neq\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
. Then there exists an indecomposable module
N\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. Then
N=\tau_{d}^{-}(M)
for some
M\in\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{i}
. By Lemma
4.12
(a) we obtain that
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
and that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
and so we are in case (e).
∎

Motivated by the previous results in this section, we introduce the following directed multigraph
G=G(\mathcal{C})
for
l>2
:

(4.2)

When
d=2
, we also include the following arrows in
G
:

(4.3)

For clarity, we note that

\bullet

there are
\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+3
arrows from
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
to
\mathcal{D}_{2t}
, labelled
\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\frac{d-2}{2}l+2}
,

\bullet

for every
h\in\{2,\ldots,l-1\}
, there are
l-h+1
arrows from
\mathcal{D}_{2t}^{\downarrow}(0)
to
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}^{\uparrow}(h)
, labelled
\zeta_{h,0},\ldots,\zeta_{h,l-h}
,

\bullet

there are
\tfrac{d}{2}l+1
arrows from
\mathcal{D}_{2t}^{\downarrow}(0)
to
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}
, labelled
\kappa_{0},\ldots,\kappa_{\frac{d}{2}l}
,

\bullet

for every
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-2\}
, there are
l-h
arrows from
\mathcal{D}_{2t}^{\downarrow}(h)
to
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}
, labelled
\lambda_{h,0},\ldots,\lambda_{h,l-h-1}
,

\bullet

if
d=2
, then for every
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-3\}
and any
u\in\{2,\ldots,l-h-1\}
, there are
l-(h+u)
arrows from
\mathcal{D}_{2t}^{\downarrow}(h)
to
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}^{\uparrow}(u)
, labelled
\mu_{h,0}^{u},\ldots,\mu_{h,l-(h+u)-1}^{u}
, and

\bullet

all other arrows have multiplicity one.

We say that a vertex in
G
is
odd
if the subscript appearing in that vertex is
2t+1
and that it is
even
if the subscript appearing in that vertex is
2t
. Notice that all arrows starting at odd vertices terminate at even vertices and all arrows starting at even vertices terminate at odd vertices. Hence a directed path
\chi
in
G
of odd length starts and terminates at vertices of opposite parity.

Let
\chi=\chi_{1}\chi_{2}\cdots\chi_{p-1}
be a directed path in
G
of length
p-1
, such that
\chi
starts at an odd vertex; since
p
is even, this is equivalent to
\chi
terminating at an even vertex. We associate to
\chi
a full additive subcategory
\mathcal{U}(\chi)\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. First, let
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
and write
i=2t+1
if
i
is odd and
i=2t
if
i
is even. Then we set

\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{U}(\chi)\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\text{start of arrow $\chi_{i}$,}&\mbox{if $i$ is odd,}\\
\text{end of arrow $\chi_{i}$,}&\mbox{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

Next, we want to define
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}
. Notice that we may partition
[1,n-(l-1)]
as

\displaystyle[1,n-(l-1)]

\displaystyle=[s_{1},s_{2}-(l-1)]\sqcup[s_{2}-(l-2),s_{3}-1]\sqcup\cdots\sqcup[s_{p-1},s_{p}-(l-1)]

\displaystyle=F_{1}\sqcup F_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup F_{p-1}

We describe some conditions which describe
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap F_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
; for all other
i
we set
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap F_{i}=\varnothing
.

Conditions 4.17
.

(i)

if
i
is odd and
\chi_{i}=\beta_{y}
for some
y\in\{0,\ldots,\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i+1}-(l-1)-(y-1),s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
,

(ii)

if
i
is odd and
\chi_{i}=\iota
or
\chi_{i}=\epsilon_{h}
for some
h\in\{2,\ldots,l-1\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
,

(iii)

if
i
is even and
\chi_{i}=\zeta_{h,y}
for some
h\in\{2,\ldots,l-1\}
and some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-h\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-1-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
,

(iv)

if
i
is even and
\chi_{i}=\kappa_{y}
for some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,\tfrac{d}{2}l\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-1-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
,

(v)

if
i
is even and
\chi_{i}=\lambda_{h,y}
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-2\}
and some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-h-1\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-h-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
,

(vi)

if
i
is even and
\chi_{i}=\iota^{-}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
,

(vii)

if
d=2
,
i
is even and
\chi_{i}=\mu_{h,y}^{u}
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-3\}
and some
u\in\{2,\ldots,l-h-1\}
and some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,l-(h+u)-1\}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i}-h-(y-1),s_{i+1}-1]
.

Then we have

\mathcal{U}(\chi)=\mathrm{add}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}(\chi),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{p}(\chi),I(Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)})\right).

Example 4.18
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(37,4)
and
d=4
so that
\Lambda
admits a
4
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the path
\chi=\epsilon_{2}\zeta_{2,2}\eta\theta_{2}\delta_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
is the subcategory defined as the additive closure of the encircled modules in the following picture:

As the next theorem shows,
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
is a
4
-torsion class.

We are ready to prove our main result for this section, namely that
d
-torsion classes can be described using paths in the graph
G
.

Theorem 4.19
.

Assume that
l>2
. There exists a bijection between the set of paths
\chi
in
G
of length
p-1
starting at an odd vertex and the set of
d
-torsion classes
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{C}
. The bijection is given by
\chi\mapsto\mathcal{U}(\chi)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class in
\mathcal{C}
. Let
1\leq i\leq p
. Write
i=2t+1
if
i
is odd and
i=2t
if
i
is even. By Lemma
4.13
we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i}
appears as one of the vertices in
G
. Assume that
i\leq p-1
. Then the arrows in
G
starting at
\mathcal{U}_{i}
are in bijection with the possible configurations
(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})
as they are outlined in Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.16
; the bijection is described by the conditions on the arrows given when defining
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}
. We may thus define
\chi_{i}
to be the corresponding arrow under this bijection. By definition of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
, we then have that
\mathcal{U}(\chi)=\mathcal{U}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
is a
d
-torsion class. For this we use Definition
4.1
. Let
C\in\mathcal{C}
and we need to produce a
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-1}}

such that
U\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
and, for every
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
, the induced sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U,C_{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U,C_{d}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is exact. Since
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
is an additive category, it is enough to show the claim for
C
indecomposable. If
C\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we can clearly consider the
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Assume that
C\not\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
. We consider three complementary cases:
C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
i
is odd,
C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
i
is even and
C=I(q)
for some
q\in[1,n-(l-1)]
.

Case
C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
i
is odd. Then
C=M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. By definition of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)\in\{\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1),\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})\mid 1\leq h^{\prime}\leq l-1\}
. Since
C\not\in\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
, we conclude that either no submodule of
C
is in
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
. If no submodule of
C
is in
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
, consider the
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C\right)=0
for all
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
. If
s_{i}=1
, then this is clear. Assume that
s_{i}>1
and assume to a contradiction that there exists a nonzero morphism
f:U^{\prime}\to C
for some indecomposable module
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
. Since
i
is odd, we have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{D}_{i-1},\mathcal{D}_{i}\right)=0
by Lemma
2.12
. We conclude that
U^{\prime}
is injective and so
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
for some

. Hence

. By the construction of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we obtain that
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]\supseteq[q,s_{i+1}-1]
and that
\chi_{i-1}
is one of the arrows in cases (iii), (iv), (v), (vi) or (vii) of Conditions

. In each of these cases we obtain that
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(q+l-s_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
. But then
q\leq s_{i}+h-l
gives
q+l-s_{i}\leq h
and so
C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(q+l-s_{i})
, contradicting
