. Hence it remains to consider the case
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
. In this case, by the construction of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
, we have that
\chi_{i-1}=\theta_{h}
and
\chi_{i}=\eta
. We conclude that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
and moreover that
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
. Set
b=s_{i}
and
j=h-1
and consider the
d
-extension in Lemma
4.9
(b), which is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i},s_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)=C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-1}}

Similarly to the previous case, it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C_{s}\right)=0
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. All the modules
C_{1},\ldots,C_{d}
are either indecomposable injective modules of the form
I(q)
for
q\in[s_{i}+1,s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
or modules in
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
. Since
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i-1}-(l-1)+1,s_{i+1}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
and
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
, it follows that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C_{s}\right)=0
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, as required.

Case
C\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
i
is even. Then
C=M(s_{i}-h+1,s_{i})
for some
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
. By definition of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we have that
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in\{0,1\ldots,l-1\}
. Since
C\not\in\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
, we have that
h^{\prime}<h\leq l-1
. By the construction of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we obtain that
\chi_{i-1}
is one of the arrows
\gamma
,
\eta
or
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{l-2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i-1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i-1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}^{\downarrow}(1)
. In any case we have that
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i-1},s_{i}-(l-1)]=\varnothing
and that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}_{i-1}(\chi),C\right)=0
. It follows that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C\right)=0
and so we may again consider the
d
-extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Finally, assume that
C=I(q)
for some
q\in[1,n-(l-1)]
. Then either
q\in[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
for some odd
i
or
q\in[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
for some even
i
. We consider the two cases separately.

Assume that
q\in[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]
for some odd
i
. Then
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)\in\{\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1),\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})\mid 1\leq h^{\prime}\leq l-1\}
. If
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\uparrow}(h^{\prime})
for some
h^{\prime}\in[1,l-1]
, then by the construction of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we obtain that
\chi_{i}=\epsilon_{h^{\prime}}
and so
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}
by Condition
4.17
(ii). But then
I(q)\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
, which is a contradiction. Hence we may assume that
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(1)
. Checking the arrows in
G
going to the vertices
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
and
\mathcal{D}_{2t+1}^{\downarrow}(1)
reveals that none of them appears in Condition
4.17
, from which we obtain that
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i-1}-(l-2),s_{i}-1]=\varnothing
. We may again argue that we also have
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap[s_{i},q]=\varnothing
, since otherwise
C=I(q)\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
. All in all we obtain that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C\right)=0
which is enough.

For the last case we may assume that
q\in[s_{i}-(l-2),s_{i+1}-1]
for some even
i
. Again we may assume that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C\right)\neq 0
. By the definition of
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
and since
I(q)\not\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
, it is easy to check that this implies that
M(q,s_{i})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)
. Combined with the fact that
I(q)\not\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
we obtain that
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(1)
. It follows that
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\cap Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}=\varnothing
. Set
a=q
and
j=s_{i}-a+1
and consider the
d
-extension in Lemma
4.9
(a), which is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(q,s_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(q)=C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-1}}

All the modules
C_{1},\ldots,C_{d-2}
are indecomposable injective modules of the form
I(q^{\prime})
with
q^{\prime}\in[q+j,q+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l]=[s_{i}+1,q+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l]
. Moreover we have

C_{d-1}=I(q^{\prime\prime})\oplus M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,q+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)\text{ and }C_{d}=M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,s_{i}+\tfrac{d}{2}l),

where again
q^{\prime\prime}\in[s_{i}+1,s_{i+1}-1]
. Set
C_{d-1}^{\prime}=M(s_{i}+\tfrac{d-2}{2}l+2,q+\tfrac{d}{2}l-1)
. Since
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\cap Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}=\varnothing
and
C_{d-1}^{\prime},C_{d}\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
, we obtain that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),C_{i}\right)=0
for
1\leq i\leq d-2
and that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathcal{U}(\chi),I(q^{\prime\prime})\right)=0
too. Now let
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\chi)
be indecomposable. Then the sequence

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{d-1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{d}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{d-1}^{\prime}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(U^{\prime},C_{d}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Notice that both
C_{d-1}^{\prime}
and
C_{d}
belong to
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
and that
[s_{i},s_{i+1}-(l-1)]\cap Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}=\varnothing
. Hence if
U^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)
, then both
\mathrm{Hom}
-spaces in the above sequence become zero. It remains to consider the case
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)
or
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}(\chi)=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(1)
, we conclude that it remains to check the case
U^{\prime}=M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1})
. But then, using the fact that
C_{d-1}^{\prime},C_{d}\in\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
we obtain that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}),C_{d-1}^{\prime}\right)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}),C_{d}\right),

which shows that the sequence (
4.4
) is exact, as required.
∎

4.3.2.
The case
l=2
.

We finish this section with this case which is considerably simpler.

Lemma 4.20
.

Assume that
l=2
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
d
-torsion class and let
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
. Then exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})\in\{(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0)),(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i+1}^{\downarrow}(0))\}

and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-1]=\varnothing
.

(b)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\downarrow}(0),\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
and there exists some
y\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i+1}-y,s_{i+1}-1]
.

(c)

(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})=(\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
and
Q_{\mathcal{U}}\cap[s_{i},s_{i+1}-1]=[s_{i},s_{i+1}-1]
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proofs of Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.16
; we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

Notice in particular that the possibilities for
(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i+1})
in Lemma
4.20
do not depend on the parity of
i
, unlike the case
l>2
. Then, in a similar manner to the previous section, we may introduce the following directed multigraph
G=G(\mathcal{C})
for
l=2
.

(4.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\downarrow}(0)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Let
\chi=\chi_{1}\chi_{2}\cdots\chi_{p-1}
be a directed path in
G
of length
p-1
. Notice that since we are in the case
l=2
,
p
does not necessarily have to be even. To
\chi
we associate a full additive subcategory
\mathcal{U}(\chi)\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. First, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
we set

\mathcal{U}_{i}(\chi)=\mathcal{U}(\chi)\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}\coloneqq\text{start of arrow $\chi_{i}$}

Next, we want to define
Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}
. Notice that we may partition
[1,n-1]
as

\displaystyle[1,n-1]=[s_{1},s_{2}-1]\sqcup[s_{2},s_{3}-1]\sqcup\cdots\sqcup[s_{p-1},s_{p}-1]=F_{1}\sqcup F_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup F_{p-1}

We set

Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)}\cap F_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\varnothing,&\mbox{if $\chi_{i}=\beta$ or $\chi_{i}=\gamma$,}\\
[s_{i+1}-y,s_{i+1}-1],&\mbox{if $\chi_{i}=\delta_{y}$ for some $y\in\{0,\ldots,d\}$,}\\
F_{i},&\mbox{if $\chi_{i}=\epsilon$.}\end{cases}

Then we set

\mathcal{U}(\chi)=\mathrm{add}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}(\chi),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{p}(\chi),I(Q_{\mathcal{U}(\chi)})\right),

and have the following result.

Theorem 4.21
.

Assume that
l=2
. There exists a bijection between the set of paths
\chi
in
G
of length
p-1
starting at an odd vertex and the set of
d
-torsion classes
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{C}
. The bijection is given by
\chi\mapsto\mathcal{U}(\chi)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.19
; we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

Example 4.22
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(19,2)
and
d=3
so that
\Lambda
admits a
3
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the path
\chi=\beta\delta_{3}\beta\epsilon\delta_{2}\gamma
. A direct computation shows that
\mathcal{U}(\chi)
is the
3
-torsion class given by the encircled modules in the following picture:

4.3.3.
The case
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)

We finish this section by applying our classification of
d
-torsion classes to count their number in the special case where
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
.

Remark 4.23
.

(a)

Assume that
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda(n,l))
and that
\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Then
p=2
and
d=2\tfrac{n-1}{l}
by Remark
2.7
. If
l>2
, then by Theorem
4.19
every
d
-torsion class of
\m\Lambda
is given by a path in
G(\mathcal{C})
of length
1
starting at an odd vertex. As a path of length one is an arrow, counting the arrows in (
4.2
) starting at an odd vertex we find that there are

1+(l-2)+\left(\frac{d-2}{2}l+3\right)+1+(l-2)+1=\frac{d+2}{2}l+2=\frac{2\frac{n-1}{l}+2}{2}l+2=n+l+1

such paths. If
l=2
then by Theorem
4.21
every
d
-torsion class of
\m\Lambda
is given by an arrow in
G(\mathcal{C})
. Counting the arrows in (
4.5
) we find that there are
d+4=2\tfrac{n-1}{2}+4=n+3
such paths. All in all we conclude that in either case
\Lambda(n,l)
admits exactly
n+l+1

d
-torsion classes.

(b)

A similar calculation shows that when
p=4
and either (
d>2
and
l>2
) or (
d=2
and
l=3
) there exist exactly

\frac{1}{18}(17l^{2}+10ln+57l+2n^{2}+30n+18)

d
-torsion classes.

5.
(d+1)
-term silting complexes

Motivation and aim.

An important result in
\tau
-tilting theory establishes a bijection between basic support
\tau
-tilting pairs and basic
2
-silting complexes in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)

[
2
]
. Thus, when trying to generalize support
\tau
-tilting modules in the setting of higher-dimensional homological algebra, it is natural to consider an approach through silting theory. This is done in
[
33
]
where Martínez and Mendoza give a higher version of
\tau
-tilting modules through
(d+1)
-silting complexes. We call such modules
MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting
and we note that it is straightforward to adapt this notion to pairs
(M,P)
as well. The main aim of this section is to prove Theorem
C
, which essentially shows that such pairs are characterized by being summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

Setting

The main observations in this section are done for the algebras
\Lambda(n,l)
, however we begin our endeavour in a more general setting by recalling some results from
[
33
]
and giving some fundamental definitions. The notation introduced in this section is less extensive than in the previous ones, but as before we refer to the index at the end of the article for reminders.

5.1.
Preliminaries

Let
d\geq 1
be a positive integer. In this section we begin by letting
\Lambda
be a finite dimensional
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with
n
simples and
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
the category of bounded complexes of finitely generated projective
\Lambda
-modules. Let
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
be a complex. We define the
truncation

\sigma_{\geq k}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
and
\sigma_{\leq k}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
by

\sigma_{\geq k}\mathbf{P}^{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{P}^{i},&\mbox{if $i\geq k$,}\\
0,&\mbox{if $i<k$,}\end{cases}\qquad\text{ respectively }\qquad\sigma_{\leq k}\mathbf{P}^{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{P}^{i},&\mbox{if $i\leq k$,}\\
0,&\mbox{if $i>k$.}\end{cases}

We similarly define the
truncations

\sigma_{<k}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
and
\sigma_{>k}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
. The
thick closure
of
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
, denoted
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}\right)
, is the smallest triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
which is closed under direct summands and contains
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
. The complex
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
is called
presilting
if it admits no non-trivial maps to its positive shifts, i.e.
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet},\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}[i]\right)=0
for all
i>0
.
A presilting complex
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}
is a
silting complex
if its thick closure is the whole of
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
.
Let
\mathrm{silt}\,\Lambda
denote the collection of silting complexes. Since we have assumed that
\lvert\Lambda\rvert=n
and
\Lambda
is clearly a silting complex, we have the following well-known observation.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
4
, Corollary 2.28]
)
.

For any
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{silt}\,\Lambda
we have
\lvert\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}\rvert=n
.

For any
M\in\m\Lambda
we fix a minimal projective resolution

\cdots\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-3}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-2}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-1}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{0}(M)\rightarrow M

of
M
and obtain the complex
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
given by

\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)\colon\qquad\cdots\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-3}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-2}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{-1}(M)\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{0}(M)\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots.

We now recall the following definition from
[
33
]
.

Definition 5.2
.

[
33
, Definition 1.1]

We say that a module
M\in\m\Lambda
is
Martínez-Mendoza
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting
, abbreviated to
MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting
, if
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)\in\mathrm{silt}\,\Lambda
.

As shown in
[
33
, Theorem 5.7]
, a MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting module is sincere and has
n
non-isomorphic indecomposable summands. Moreover, in
[
33
, Theorem 3.4]
it is shown that
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
is presilting in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
if and only if
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0
and
\mathrm{Ext}_{\Lambda}^{i}({M},{M})=0
for
1\leq i\leq d-1
.

Now let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory and let
(M,P)
be a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. By the previous paragraph we conclude that
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
is presilting in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
. On the other hand, if
P\neq 0
, then
M
is not sincere and so
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
cannot be a silting complex. To have a chance at obtaining a silting complex from a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
, it is necessary to encode information of
P
in the candidate complex. We follow the strategy of the
d=1
case in
[
2
]
and simply add the projective module to the truncated complex as a stalk complex. Let us therefore fix the notation
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\coloneqq P[d]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)\in\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\,\Lambda)
for any
\mathcal{C}
-pair
(M,P)
. We are now ready to give a slight expansion of
[
33
, Thm. 3.4]
. The proof is straight-forward using
[
33
, Lem. 5.2]
, hence we omit it here.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair.
Then
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair if and only if
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}

is a
(d+1)
-presilting complex.

As a corollary, we obtain one direction of Theorem
C
.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. If
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\in\mathrm{silt}\,\Lambda
,
then
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
5.1
and
5.3
.
∎

5.2.
Thick closure of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in
\Lambda(n,l)

Our aim is to show the converse of Corollary
5.4
in the case of
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
. Hence let
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
be an algebra that satisfies the conditions of Theorem
2.6
. To attain our goal we see in light of Lemma
5.3
that we only need to show that for a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
the complex
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}
generates the triangulated category
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
, i.e. that
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)=\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
. The strategy used for this mirrors largely those of the previous sections, meaning we partition the problem into smaller problems centered around admissible configurations
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
.

To show that
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)=\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
we need to show that each indecomposable projective module appears in
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
. This is clear for indecomposable projective summands of
M
and
P
; let us call this collection of indecomposable projective modules
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. As a next step we may find all complexes in
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
that contains some of these indecomposable projective modules, together with one other indecomposable projective module. If this other indecomposable projective module appears as a stalk complex, then it is easy to see that it also belongs to
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
. This gives us a new collection of indecomposable projective modules, say
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, that necessarily includes
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and is included in
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
. We may repeat this process inductively with the hope of eventually obtaining a collection
\mathcal{P}_{k}
containing all of the indecomposable projective modules.

In paragraph
5.2.1
we show that the above strategy works. We begin by obtaining some useful complexes in
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
: these are the truncated projective resolutions of modules in
M
. We collect the terms of such projective resolutions in sets
\mathcal{P}(X)
for each indecomposable module
X
. Then we introduce the notion of reducing sets which encode the inductive procedure we describe in the previous paragraph. By this translation we see that
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)=\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
holds if a particular type of reducing set exists for
[1,n]\setminus(R\cup B)
. We go on to show that this type of reducing set can be constructed from reducing sets of
\Xi({i,i+k})\setminus(R\cup B)
for each admissible configuration
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
of a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
.

Then in paragraph
5.2.2
we are concerned with showing that for each full admissible type
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
in Definition
3.16
we can find a reducing set of
\Xi({i,i+k})\setminus(R\cup B)
. We conclude the paragraph at the end by proving Theorem
C
.

5.2.1.
Restricting silting to local conditions

Lemma 5.5

(
[
38
, Corollary 4.5]
)
.

Let M(a,b) be a non-projective indecomposable module. Then

\mathbf{P}^{-j}(M(a,b))=\begin{cases}P(a-\frac{j-1}{2}l-1),&\text{ if }j\geq 1\text{ is odd,}\\
P(b-\frac{j}{2}l),&\text{ if }j\geq 0\text{ is even.}\end{cases}

As a tool for later, we define for an indecomposable module
X=M(a,b)
the set
\mathcal{P}(X)
given by the indices of the indecomposable projective modules appearing in its truncated minimal projective resolution
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(X)
. In particular, since
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{-t}(X)
is indecomposable by Lemma
5.5
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}(X)=&\{i\in[1,n]\ |\ P(i)=\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{-t}(X)\text{ for some }t\}\\
=&[1,n]\cap\{x_{d}<\cdots<x_{1}<x_{0}\},\end{split}

where
x_{j}=b-\tfrac{j}{2}l
if
j
is even and
x_{j}=a-\tfrac{j-1}{2}l-1
if
j
is odd. Observe also that
x_{j-2}-x_{j}=l
for
0\leq j-2<j\leq d
, and that if
i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, then there exist unique
t_{i},t_{i^{\prime}}
such that
P(i)=\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{-t_{i}}(X)
and
P(i^{\prime})=\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{-t_{i^{\prime}}}(X)
, and then
i\leq i^{\prime}
is equivalent to
t_{i}\leq t_{i^{\prime}}
.

Example 5.6
.

Consider the indecomposable
\tau_{4}
-rigid module
M(s_{3},s_{3}+2)=M(15,17)
of
\Lambda(23,4)
. Then, we have

\mathcal{P}(M(15,17))=\{x_{4}=9<x_{3}=10<x_{2}=13<x_{1}=14<x_{0}=17\}

We record the following important corollary of Lemma
5.5
which arises from a couple of easy calculations.

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair with
M_{i}\neq 0
for
2\leq i\leq p
. Then

\bigcup_{X\in\mathrm{ind}M_{i}}\mathcal{P}(X)\subseteq\Xi_{i}.

Moreover, when
m_{i}=l-1
, this strengthens to an equality.

For a basic module
M\in\m\Lambda
and a subset
\Gamma
of
[1,n]
we let
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M)
be the set

\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M)\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma\ |\ X\text{ an indecomposable summand of M}\}.

This set is in the heart and center of the following arguments. In order for it to reach its full potential we need one additional notion, namely reducing sets.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite set with
k+1
elements. A subset
\Psi\subseteq\mathbb{P}(\Gamma)
of the power set of
\Gamma
is called a
reducing set of
\Gamma
if there exists an ordering
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
of the elements of
\Gamma
such that for
0\leq i\leq k
we have
\{\alpha_{i}\}\in\Psi_{i}
, where

\Psi_{i}=\begin{cases}\Psi,&\mbox{if $i=0$,}\\
\{\chi\setminus\{\alpha_{i-1}\}\mid\chi\in\Psi_{i-1}\},&\mbox{if $1\leq i\leq k$.}\end{cases}

Example 5.9
.

For any finite set
\Gamma
, the set
\{\{\beta\}\mid\beta\in\Gamma\}
is a reducing set, which we call the
trivial reducing set
.

We have some immediate properties of reducing sets which contrary to their simplicity will play a significant part in the following.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma^{1}\sqcup\Gamma^{2}
be a finite set and
\Psi=\Psi^{1}\cup\Psi^{2}
a subset of the power set of
\Gamma
. Assume that
\Psi^{1}
is a reducing set of
\Gamma^{1}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}\coloneqq\{\xi\cap\Gamma^{2}\ |\ \xi\in\Psi^{2}\}
is a reducing set of
\Gamma^{2}
, then
\Psi
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite set and
\Psi
a subset of the power set
\mathbb{P}(\Gamma)
such that

(a)

\bigcup_{\chi\in\Psi}\chi=\Gamma
,

(b)

for all
\chi\in\Psi
,
\lvert\chi\rvert\leq 2
,

(c)

there exists a unique
\alpha\in\Gamma
such that
\chi\cap\xi=\{\alpha\}
for all
\chi,\xi\in\Psi
, and
\{\alpha\}\in\Psi

Then
\Psi
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
.

We can now obtain the thickness condition for silting from the existence of a reducing set as per the following lemma.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and
\Gamma\coloneqq[1,n]\setminus(R\cup B)
. If
\Psi\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
, then
\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)=\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,(\Lambda))
.

Proof.

We know that
\mathrm{thick}\left(\Lambda\right)=\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,(\Lambda))
, hence it is enough to show that for all
i\in[1,n]
, the indecomposable projective
P(i)
lies in
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\mathrm{thick}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}\right)
. Immediately, we observe that for
i\in R\cup B
, we have
P(i)\in\mathcal{T}
, hence we only need to consider the projective modules
P(i)
for
i\in\Gamma
.

An important, albeit simple, observation we make use of is the following. Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and assume that a complex
\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}
and the stalk complexes
\mathbf{Q}^{i}
for
i\neq k
lie in
\mathcal{T}
. Then the stalk complex
\mathbf{Q}^{k}
also lies in
\mathcal{T}
. Let us justify this briefly. The triangle
\sigma_{>k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\sigma_{\leq k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\sigma_{>k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}[1]
, shows that
\sigma_{\leq k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{T}
, since both
\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}
and
\sigma_{>k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}[1]
lie in
\mathcal{T}
. Now, the triangle
\mathbf{Q}^{k}[-k]\rightarrow\sigma_{\leq k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\sigma_{<k}\mathbf{Q}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathbf{Q}^{k}[-k][1]
shows that
\mathbf{Q}^{k}\in\mathcal{T}
.

The rest of the proof is done iteratively. Since
\Psi
is a reducing set, there exists an ordering
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
of the elements of
\Gamma
as in Definition
5.8
. Then

\{\alpha_{0}\}\in\Psi=\{\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma\mid X\text{ is an indecomposable summand of $M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{p}$}\}.

Hence there exists an indecomposable summand
X_{\alpha_{0}}
of
M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{p}
such that
\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha_{0}})\cap\Gamma_{0}=\{\alpha_{0}\}
. By definition of
\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha_{0}})
there exists
t_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{t_{0}}(X_{\alpha_{0}})=P(\alpha_{0})
and
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{i}(X_{\alpha_{0}})
lies in
\mathcal{T}
for
i\neq t_{0}
(since there are no repeats of indecomposable summands in a projective resolution of
X_{\alpha_{0}}
). Since
X_{\alpha_{0}}
is a summand of
M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{p}
, the complex
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(X_{\alpha_{0}})
also lies in
\mathcal{T}
. Hence from the discussion above also
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{t_{0}}(X_{\alpha_{0}})=P(\alpha_{0})
lies in
\mathcal{T}
.

Now assume that
P(\alpha_{0}),P(\alpha_{1}),\ldots,P(\alpha_{j-1})\in\mathcal{T}
and we show that
P(\alpha_{j})\in\mathcal{T}
. As above there exists an indecomposable summand
X_{\alpha_{j}}
of
M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{p}
such that
\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha_{j}})\cap\Gamma_{j}=\{\alpha_{j}\}
. Since
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{j}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}\}
, we have that
\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha_{j}})\cap\Gamma\subseteq\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j}\}
. By induction hypothesis we have that
P(\alpha_{0}),P(\alpha_{1}),\ldots,P(\alpha_{j-1})\in\mathcal{T}
. Since
\alpha_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha_{j}})\cap\Gamma
, there exists
t_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{t_{j}}(X_{\alpha_{j}})=P(\alpha_{j})
. Moreover, as in the base case, the complexes
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(X_{\alpha_{j}})
and
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{i}(X_{\alpha_{0}})
, for
i\neq t_{j}
, lie in
\mathcal{T}
by assumption. Again we conclude that
\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{t_{j}}(X_{\alpha_{j}})=P(\alpha_{j})
lies in
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

We are now in the home stretch of this section. As sketched in the introduction of the paragraph
the strategy is to show that for a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
and
\Gamma=[1,n]\setminus(R\cup B)
, the set
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(\oplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
. To manage this, we look at the existence of a reducing set more locally.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair with diagonal partition
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
. Then the following hold.

(a)

\mathcal{P}_{\Xi(T_{j})}(M_{t_{i,1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{t_{i,2}})=\{\varnothing\}
when
i\neq j
.

(b)

Let
\Gamma=[1,n]\setminus(R\cup B)
. Let
\Gamma_{T_{j}}=\Xi(T_{j})\setminus(R\cup B)
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
if and only if for every
j\in[1,k]
the set
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{T_{j}}}(M_{t_{j,1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{t_{j,2}})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma_{T_{j}}
.

Proof.

A summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair is necessarily well-configured, hence by Definition
3.28
(b)(ii), we have
\Xi(T_{j})\cap\Xi(T_{i})=\varnothing
when
i\neq j
. Using Corollary
5.7
and the fact that
\Xi(T_{i})=\bigcup_{\alpha\in T_{i}}\Xi_{\alpha}
, we therefore obtain (a).

Recall that for a well-configured
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
with non-diagonal support
(R,B)
we have
(R\cup B)\cap\Theta=\Theta
and
\Theta\sqcup\Xi=[1,n]
where

\Xi=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\Xi(T_{j}).

Hence, (b) follows from (a) and repeated use of Lemma
5.10
.
∎

5.2.2.
Local silting through admissible configurations.

Now we study each type of admissible configuration separately and show the existence of reducing sets for each such type locally.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Assume that
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration and let
\Gamma=\Xi(i,i+k)\setminus(R\cup B)
.

(a)

If the configuration is of type (I), then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma=B_{i}^{\times}
.

(b)

If the configuration is of type (II), then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma=R_{i}^{\times}
.

(c)

If the configuration is of type (IV) or (VI) with
\Xi
rigid, then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})\cup\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma=R_{i}^{\times}\cup R_{i+1}^{\times}
.

(d)

If the configuration is of type (V) or (VII) with
\Xi
support, then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})\cup\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma=B_{i}^{\times}\cup B_{i+1}^{\times}
.

Proof.

If
d
is odd, then
l=2
by Theorem
2.6
and so all admissible configurations are of type (III). Hence we assume that
d
is even.

(a)

This part can be proved similarly to (b), hence we only consider (b) here.

(b)

The configuration is of type (II), hence
k=0
,
\Xi_{i}
is rigid and
\Gamma=\Xi_{i}\setminus(R\cup B)=R_{i}^{\times}
. Further
1<l_{i}\leq n_{i}=l-1
. From (
3.3
) we get

R_{i}^{\times}=\begin{cases}[s_{i-1}-(l-l_{i})+1,s_{i-1}],&\text{ if }i\text{ is odd,}\\
[s_{i-1},s_{i-1}+(l-l_{i})-1],&\text{ if }i\text{ is even.}\end{cases}

Let
X(h)
be an indecomposable summand of
M_{i}
with length
h\in[l_{i},l-1]
. By (
3.1
), (
2.4
) and Lemma
5.5
we obtain that

\mathcal{P}(X(h))\cap R_{i}^{\times}=\begin{cases}\{s_{i-1}-(l-h)+1\},&\text{ if }i\text{ is odd,}\\
\{s_{i-1}\},&\text{ if }i\text{ is even and }h=l_{i},\\
\{s_{i-1},s_{i-1}+(l-h)\},&\text{ if }i\text{ is even and }l_{i}+1\leq h.\end{cases}

Thus, if
i
is odd, then
\mathcal{P}_{R_{i}^{\times}}(M_{i})
is the trivial reducing set, and if
i
is even, then
\mathcal{P}_{R_{i}^{\times}}(M_{i})
is reducing by Lemma
5.11
.

(c)

This follows as in (b) using Lemma
5.10
with
\Gamma^{1}=R_{i+1}^{\times}
,
\Gamma^{2}=\Gamma\setminus\Gamma^{1}
,
\Psi^{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
and
\Psi^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
.

(d)

This follows as in (a) using Lemma
5.10
with
\Gamma^{1}=B_{i}^{\times}
,
\Gamma^{2}=\Gamma\setminus\Gamma^{1}
,
\Psi^{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
and
\Psi^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
. ∎

Example 5.15
.

Consider the following summand-maximal
\tau_{2}
-rigid pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
of
\Lambda(20,5)
. The diagonal partition is
(T_{1})=([4,4])
and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{4}})
is a full admissible configuration of type (I).

We have that
\Gamma=\Xi(T_{1})\setminus(R\cup B)=\{11,12,13\}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(11,13))

\displaystyle=\{8<10<13\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(12,13))

\displaystyle=\{8<11<13\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(13,13))

\displaystyle=\{8<12<13\}.

Hence, we have
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{4})=\{\{13\},\{11,13\},\{12,13\}\}
, which is clearly a reducing set of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
a full admissible configuration of type (III). Then
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma\coloneqq\Xi(i,i+k)\setminus(R\cup B)

Proof.

If
l=2
, then a direct calculation shows that
\Gamma=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})=\{a\}
for some
a\in[s_{i}-d,s_{i}]
and the claim follows immediately.

Assume that
l>2
. We only prove the case of
i
being odd, the even case is essentially the same. Since the admissible configuration is of type (III), we have that
k=0
, that
\Xi(i,i+k)=\Xi_{i}
and that
\Xi_{i}
is either rigid, support or support to rigid at some
x\in\Xi_{i}\setminus B_{i}^{\times}
. If

•

\Xi_{i}
is rigid, then
\Gamma=R_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i-1}-l+2,s_{i-1}]
,

•

\Xi_{i}
is support, then
\Gamma=B_{i}^{\times}=[s_{i},s_{i}+l-2]
,

•

\Xi_{i}
is support to rigid, then
\Gamma=[x+1,x+l-1]
for some
x\in\Xi_{i}\setminus B_{i}^{\times}
.

In any case, we have
\lvert\Gamma\rvert=l-1
, and from the description after Lemma
5.5
, we see that
\lvert\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma\rvert\leq 2
for all indecomposable summands
X
of
M_{i}
. Observe now that
\Xi_{i}=[s_{i-1}-l+2,s_{i}+l-2]
is equal to
\bigcup_{X\in\mathrm{ind}M_{i}}\mathcal{P}(X)
by Corollary
5.7
. Therefore
\bigcup_{\chi\in\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})}\chi=\Gamma.

Next, for
h\in\{1,\ldots,l-1\}
let
X(h)=M(s_{i},s_{i}+h-1)
so that
M_{i}=\bigoplus_{h=1}^{l-1}X(h)
. Using Lemma
5.5
we see that

\mathbf{P}^{-j}(X(h))=\begin{cases}P(s_{i}-\frac{j-1}{2}l-1),&\text{ if }j\geq 1\text{ is odd,}\\
P(s_{i}+h-1-\frac{j}{2}l),&\text{ if }j\geq 0\text{ is even.}\end{cases}

It follows that either
(\mathcal{P}(X(h))\cap\Gamma)\cap(\mathcal{P}(X(h^{\prime}))\cap\Gamma)=\varnothing
for any
h\neq h^{\prime}
, in which case
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
is the trivial reducing set of
\Gamma
, or there exists
\alpha=s_{i}-\tfrac{j-1}{2}l-1\in\Gamma
for some
j
such that
\{\alpha\}=(\mathcal{P}(X(h))\cap\Gamma)\cap(\mathcal{P}(X(h^{\prime}))\cap\Gamma)
for any
h\neq h^{\prime}
of
M_{i}
, in which case
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
by Lemma
5.11
.
∎

Example 5.17
.

Let
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
be the following summand-maximal
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair of
\Lambda(23,4)
with diagonal partition
(T_{1})=([3,3])
of type (III).

Here we got

\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M)=\{\{14,15\},\{14\},\{13,14\}\}

which is clearly a reducing set of
\Gamma=[1,23]\setminus(R\cup B)=[13,15]
.

We continue with admissible configurations of type (VI) and (VII).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and let
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
be a full admissible configuration.

(a)

If the configuration is of type (VI), then any subinterval
\Gamma
of

\mathcal{I}=[s_{i}-(l-m_{i+1})+1,s_{i}+(l-1)-1].

with length
l-1
has
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
as a reducing set.

(b)

If the configuration is of type (VII), then any subinterval
\Gamma
of

\mathcal{I}=[s_{i}-(l-1)+1,s_{i}-m_{i}+(l-1)].

with length
l-1
has
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
as a reducing set.

Proof.

We only prove (a) as the proof for (b) is similar.

The interval
\mathcal{I}
can be rewritten as
[s_{i}-m_{i},s_{i}+(l-1)-1]
, using
m_{i}+m_{i+1}=l-1
. Now, an arbitrary subinterval
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{I}
with length
l-1
takes the form
\Gamma=[s_{i}-m_{i}+\gamma,s_{i}-m_{i}+\gamma+(l-1)-1]
for some
\gamma\in[0,m_{i}]
. Let
X=M(s_{i},s_{i}+x-1)
for
x\in[l_{i},l-1]
and
Y=M(s_{i+1}-y+1,s_{i+1})
for
y\in[l_{i+1},l-1]
be indecomposable summands of
M_{i}
and
M_{i+1}
respectively. With some calculations using (
2.4
) and Lemma
5.5
, we see that

\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\mathcal{I}=\begin{cases}\{s_{i}-1,\ s_{i}+l_{i}-1\},&\text{ if }x=l_{i},\\
\{s_{i}-(l-x)-1,\ s_{i}-1,\ s_{i}+x-1\},&\text{ else,}\end{cases}

and

\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\mathcal{I}=\{s_{i},\ s_{i}+l-y\}.

It is easily verified that the union of all sets in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
is in fact
\mathcal{I}
, and hence, for any
\gamma\in[0,m_{i}]
also the union of all sets in
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
equals
\Gamma
.

Let us now first consider the case
\gamma=m_{i}
. Then
\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma=\{s_{i}+x-1\}
and
\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\Gamma=\{s_{i},s_{i}+l-y\}
. We also have that
s_{i}+l_{i}-1=s_{i}+l-l_{i+1}
, since
l_{i}+l_{i+1}=l+1
. Hence, we see that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
, by ordering the elements of
\Gamma
first by the elements appearing in the sets of
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
, then the element
s_{i}
, and then any ordering of the remaining elements in
\Gamma
, e.g.

(s_{i}+(l-1)-1,\ s_{i}+(l-2)-1,\ \ldots,\ s_{i}+l_{i}-1=s_{i}+l-l_{i+1},\ s_{i},\ s_{i}+1,\ \ldots,\ s_{i}+l-l_{i+1}-1).

We are now left with considering
0\leq\gamma\leq m_{i}-1
, which gives

\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma=\begin{cases}\{s_{i}-(l-x)-1,\ s_{i}-1\},&\text{ if }x>\gamma+l_{i},\\
\{s_{i}-1\},&\text{ if }x=\gamma+l_{i},\\
\{s_{i}-1,\ s_{i}+x-1\},&\text{ if }x<\gamma+l_{i}.\end{cases}

Assume that
\gamma=0
. Then for
y=l_{i+1}
we have
\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\Gamma=\{s_{i}\}
. Thus we see that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
by any ordering starting as
(s_{i},\ s_{i}-1,\ \ldots)
, since after removing these two elements all the sets in
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
contain either one or zero elements.

Assume now that
1\leq\gamma\leq m_{i}-1
. Then for
x=l_{i}
we have
\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\Gamma=\{s_{i}-1,s_{i}+l_{i}-1\}
. Further, we have seen that
s_{i}+l_{i}-1=s_{i}+l-l_{i+1}
. Hence
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
by any ordering starting as
(s_{i}-1,\ s_{i}+l_{i}-1,\ s_{i},\ \ldots)
, since after removing these elements every non-empty set in
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1})
has only one element.
∎

Example 5.19
.

Consider the following summand-maximal
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
of
\Lambda(23,4)
with a full admissible configuration
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{3}})
of type (VII) with
\Xi
support to rigid. Then
\mathcal{I}=[7,11]
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(9,9))

\displaystyle=\{1<4<5<8<9\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(15,15))

\displaystyle=\{7<10<11<14<15\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(15,16))

\displaystyle=\{8<10<12<14<16\}.

In
\mathcal{I}
we find three subintervals of length
3
:
\Gamma_{1}=[7,9]
,
\Gamma_{2}=[8,10]
and
\Gamma_{3}=[9,11]
, which can be seen to have respective reducing sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}(M_{2}\oplus M_{3})

\displaystyle=\{\{8,9\},\{7\},\{8\}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{2}}(M_{2}\oplus M_{3})

\displaystyle=\{\{8,9\},\{10\},\{8,10\}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{3}}(M_{2}\oplus M_{3})

\displaystyle=\{\{9\},\{10,11\},\{10\}\}.

We are left with showing that also for the last configuration we obtain a reducing set of
\Xi(i,i+2)\setminus(R\cup B)
. The idea of the proof is quite similar to that of Lemma
5.18
, however the slight complication of working with three diagonals at once leads to a bit more juggling of indexes. It might be beneficial to revisit Definition
3.16
, and Example
3.19
before moving on.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and let
(M_{i},M_{i+1},M_{i+2})
be a full admissible configuration of type (VIII). Any subinterval
\Gamma
of

\mathcal{I}=[s_{i+1}-l_{i+2}+1,s_{i}-n_{i}+(l-1)]

with length
l-1
has
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1}\oplus M_{i+2})
as reducing set.

Proof.

We begin by noting that by assumption
d=2
, and that by Remark
3.9
also
l>3
. Now, we find it beneficial for the proof to rewrite
\mathcal{I}
as

[s_{i+1}-(l-l_{i+1}),s_{i+1}+n_{i+1}+2].

Let
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{I}
be a subinterval of length
l-1
. For
0\leq k\leq 2
, we denote by
\Gamma_{i+k}
the subset of
\Gamma
consisting of integers in
\Gamma
appearing in
\mathcal{P}(N)
for indecomposable summands
N
of
M_{i+k}
. Further, letting
X
,
Y
and
Z
be indecomposable summands of
M_{i}
,
M_{i+1}
and
M_{i+2}
respectively, we get by (
2.4
) that

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=M(s_{i+1}-x-1,s_{i+1}-2),

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=M(s_{i+1},s_{i+1}+y-1),

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=M(s_{i+1}+(l-z)+1,s_{i+1}+l),

where
1\leq x\leq n_{i}
,
l_{i+1}\leq y\leq n_{i+1}
and
l_{i+2}\leq z\leq l-1
.

With the help of Lemma
5.5
we calculate the following sets:

\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\mathcal{I}=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}-2\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}=1\text{ and }x=l-n_{i+1}-1=n_{i},\\
\{s_{i+1}-x-2\ <\ s_{i+1}-2\},&\text{ else,}\end{cases}

\mathcal{P}(Z)\cap\mathcal{I}=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}=1\text{ and }z=l-l_{i+1}+1=l_{i+2},\\
\{s_{i+1}\ <\ s_{i+1}+l-z\},&\text{ else,}\end{cases}

and

\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\mathcal{I}=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}=1,\\
\{s_{i+1}-1\ <\ s_{i+1}+l_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}>1\text{ and }y=l_{i+1},\\
\{s_{i+1}+n_{i+1}-l-1\ <\ s_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}>1\text{ and }y=n_{i+1},\\
\{s_{i+1}+y-l-1\ <\ s_{i+1}-1\ <\ s_{i+1}+y-1\},&\text{ if }m_{i+1}>1\text{ and }l_{i+1}<y<n_{i+1}.\end{cases}

From this it is easily verified that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{i}\cup\Gamma_{i+1}\cup\Gamma_{i+2}
. We now have two different cases to consider, either
m_{i+1}=1
or
m_{i+1}>1
. The first of these is significantly easier than the other.

Case
m_{i+1}=1
.
In particular
\Gamma=\mathcal{I}
. Further, we see that
\{\Gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+2}\}
gives a partition of
\Gamma
, and by Lemma
5.11
we see that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{i+k}}(M_{i+k})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma_{i+k}
for
0\leq k\leq 2
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1}\oplus M_{i+2})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
.

Case
m_{i+1}>1
.
For some
\gamma\in[0,m_{i}-1]
, we have

\Gamma=[s_{i+1}-(l-l_{i+1})+\gamma\ ,\ s_{i+1}+l_{i+1}+\gamma-2],

and

\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\Gamma=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}-1\ <\ s_{i+1}+y-1\},&\text{ if }y<l_{i+1}+\gamma,\\
\{s_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }y=l_{i+1}+\gamma,\\
\{s_{i+1}+y-l-1\ <\ s_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }y>l_{i+1}+\gamma.\end{cases}

It follows by Lemma
5.11
that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma_{i+1}
. We can now observe that

\mathcal{P}(X)\cap\mathcal{P}(Y)=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}+n_{i+1}-l-1\},&\text{ if }y=n_{i+1}\text{ and }x=n_{i},\\
\varnothing,&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Either
s_{i+1}+n_{i+1}-l-1
is in
\Gamma_{i}
, or
\Gamma_{i}
has
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i})
as reducing set by Lemma
5.11
. Similarly, we observe that

\mathcal{P}(Y)\cap\mathcal{P}(Z)=\begin{cases}\{s_{i+1}+l_{i+1}-1\},&\text{ if }y=l_{i+1}\text{ and }z=l_{i+2},\\
\varnothing,&\text{ else,}\end{cases}

and thus either
s_{i+1}+n_{i+1}-l-1
is in
\Gamma_{i+2}
or
\Gamma_{i+2}
has
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+2})
as reducing set.

Hence, letting
\Gamma^{1}=\Gamma_{i+1}
,
\Gamma^{2}=\Gamma\setminus\Gamma^{1}
,
\Psi^{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i+1})
and
\Psi^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+2})
, it follows by Lemma
5.10
that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(M_{i}\oplus M_{i+1}\oplus M_{i+2})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
.
∎

Example 5.21
.

We consider a summand-maximal
\tau_{2}
-rigid pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P})
of
\Lambda(23,6)
with a full admissible configuration
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{4}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{5}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M_{6}})
of type (VIII). Then
\mathcal{I}=[13,19]

We calculate the truncated projective resolutions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(15,15))

\displaystyle=\{9<14<15\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(17,18))

\displaystyle=\{12<16<18\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(17,19))

\displaystyle=\{13<16<19\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(17,20))

\displaystyle=\{14<16<20\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(M(19,23))

\displaystyle=\{17<18<23\}

There are three possible subintervals of
\mathcal{I}
of length
5
:
\Gamma_{1}=[13,17]
,
\Gamma_{2}=[14,18]
and
\Gamma_{3}=[15,19]
. These have reduction sets given respectively by:

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}(M_{4}\oplus M_{5}\oplus M_{6})&=\{\{14,\ 15\},\ \{16\},\ \{13,\ 16\},\ \{14,\ 16\},\ \{17\}\}\\
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{2}}(M_{4}\oplus M_{5}\oplus M_{6})&=\{\{14,\ 15\},\ \{16,\ 18\},\ \{16\},\ \{14,\ 16\},\ \{17,\ 18\}\}\\
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{3}}(M_{4}\oplus M_{5}\oplus M_{6})&=\{\{15\},\ \{16,\ 18\},\ \{16,\ 19\},\ \{16\},\ \{17,\ 18\}\}\\
\end{split}

Having done all the grunt work we are now ready to sew it all together into our main theorem of the section: Theorem
C
.

Theorem 5.22
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(m,l)
be as in Theorem
2.6
, and let
(M,P)
be a
\mathcal{C}
-pair. Then
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair if and only if
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}
is a
(d+1)
-silting complex in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}_{(M,P)}
is a
(d+1)
-silting complex in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
then
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair by Corollary
5.4
and Theorem
3.33
. For the opposite direction, assume that
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. By Lemma
5.3
we have that
P[d]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
is a
(d+1)
-presilting complex. Let
\Gamma=[1,n]\setminus(R\cup B)
. By Lemma
5.12
it remains to show that
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}(\bigoplus_{i=2}^{p}M_{i})
is a reducing set of
\Gamma
. Since
(M,P)
is well-configured by Theorem
3.33
, this follows by Lemma
5.13
(b) together with Lemma
5.14
, Lemma
5.16
, Lemma
5.18
and Lemma
5.20
.
∎

6.
Summary and concluding remarks

The aim of this section is to compare the theory we have developed in sections
3
,
4
and
5
with the classical case
d=1
. Throughout this section we fix a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
.

6.1.
Comparison to classical
\tau
-tilting theory

We start by recalling the definition of support
\tau
-tilting pairs.

Lemma 6.1

(
[
2
, Corollary 2.13]
)
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair over
\Lambda
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

If
(M\oplus N,P)
is a
\tau
-rigid pair for some
N\in\m\Lambda
, then
N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
.

(b)

If
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau(N)\right)=0
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,\tau(M)\right)=0
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,N\right)=0
for some
N\in\m\Lambda
, then
N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
.

(c)

\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

In particular if
(M,P)
satisfies any of the above conditions, then it is called a
support
\tau
-tilting pair
.

The equivalence of the above statements shows that if
(M,P)
is a
\tau
-rigid pair over
\Lambda
, then
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert\leq\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. Based on this fact and these definitions, we have the following generalizations to the case
d>1
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
.

(a)

[
39
, Def 2.4(3)]
We say that
(M,P)
is a
support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair
if it is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and moreover it satisfies the following:

(i)

if
(M\oplus N,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair for some
N\in\mathcal{C}
, then
N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
, and

(ii)

if
Q\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
, then

Q\in\mathrm{add}\left(P\right)\iff\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(Q,M\right)=0.

(b)

[
30
, Def 0.7]
[
39
, Def 2.4(4)]
We say that
(M,P)
is a
maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
if it satisfies the following:

(i)

if
N\in\mathcal{C}
, then

N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)\iff\begin{cases}\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,N\right)=0,\end{cases}

and

(ii)

if
Q\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
, then

Q\in\mathrm{add}\left(P\right)\iff\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(Q,M\right)=0.

(c)

We say that
(M,P)
is a
summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
if it is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair and for any other
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
we have
\lvert M^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert P^{\prime}\rvert\leq\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert
.

It is easy to see that we have implications

(M,P)
is a maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
\Downarrow
(M,P)
is a support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair
\Downarrow
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
\implies
(M,P)
is a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
\Downarrow
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair such that
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert\geq\lvert\Lambda\rvert

We are interested in studying when these implications can be reversed. It is clear that the bottom right implication can not be reversed. For the upper right implication we consider the following special case.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. Assume that for every indecomposable module
X\in\mathcal{C}
we have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(X,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(X)\right)=0
. Then a pair
(M,P)
with
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda
is a support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair if and only if it is a maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair.

Proof.

Assume that
(M,P)
is a support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair and we show that it is a maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair; the other implication always holds. Let
N\in\mathcal{C}
and we need to show that

N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)\iff\begin{cases}\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0,\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,N\right)=0.\end{cases}

Clearly we may assume that
N
is indecomposable. Since
(M,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, we have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0
and that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,M\right)=0
. Hence if
N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
, then we obtain that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(M,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0
, that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(M)\right)=0
and that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P,N\right)=0
as required. Assume now that these three equalities hold. By assumption we also have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(N,\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}(N)\right)=0
. Altogether we conclude that
(M\oplus N,P)
is a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair, and so
N\in\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
since
(M,P)
is a support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pair.
∎

Notice that the condition of Lemma
6.3
is satisfied for all representation-directed algebras. Since a homogeneous linear Nakayama algebra
\Lambda(n,l)
is representation-directed, in this article we provide no examples of support
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pairs which are not maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs.

On the other hand, Theorem
3.33
gives a way to construct maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs which are not summand-maximal. Indeed, we may pick a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair which is not well-configured and cannot be enlarged to a well-configured pair. For example, by their definition, well-configured pairs are never supported in three consecutive diagonals when
d>2
. But if
d>2
,
p\geq 4
and
l\geq 4
, then we can always find
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs which are supported in three consecutive diagonals. By enlarging such a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair as much as possible, we obtain a maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair which is not summand-maximal. We note that not all maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs are of this form either (i.e. supported in three or more consecutive diagonals), for an example of a different kind see Example
3.29
(a).

Remark 6.4
.

We remark that for an arbitrary finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
it is unknown whether a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
with
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
is also maximal, let alone summand-maximal. Moreover, as we have seen, a maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
does not necessarily satisfy
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
, in contrast to the case
d=1
. It is also unknown whether for a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
the condition
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
is always satisfied.

Both of these remarks are closely tied to the question of when a presilting object in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
is
partial silting
, i.e. it can be completed to a silting object. Observe that in this case we obtain from Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.3
that a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
is summand-maximal if and only if
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. A class of algebas where presilting is known to imply partial silting is silting-discrete algebras
[
5
, Thm. 2.15]
. In particular, by
[
3
, Prop. 3.1]
and
[
23
, Prop. 2.1]
, one obtains that in the algebras
\Lambda(n,2)
and
\Lambda(n,n-1)
(which always admit
d
-cluster tilting subcategories) a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
is summand-maximal if and only if
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
. However, most of the algebras on the form
\Lambda(n,l)
are not silting-discrete, as seen in Table 1 of
[
23
]
, hence Theorem
A
is needed to establish the property for
\Lambda(n,l)
in general.

6.2.
Maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs,
d
-torsion classes and
(d+1)
-term silting complexes

We start with recalling some useful notions. Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory for some
d\geq 1
. Recall that if
d=1
then
\mathcal{C}=\m\Lambda
. Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a
d
-torsion class. An object
X\in\mathcal{U}
is called
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective in
\mathcal{U}
if
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}_{\Lambda}(X,\mathcal{U})=0
. If moreover
\mathrm{add}\left(X\right)
contains all
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective objects in
\mathcal{U}
, then
X
is called an
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective generator of
\mathcal{U}
. It is shown in
[
9
]
that an
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective generator of
\mathcal{U}
always exists. It then follows by the definition of
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective generators that there exists a unique up to isomorphism basic
\mathrm{Ext}^{d}
-projective generator of
\mathcal{U}
, which we denote by
M^{\mathcal{U}}
. We further set
P^{\mathcal{U}}
to be a maximal basic projective
\Lambda
-module satisfying
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(P^{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{U}\right)=0
.

For a
2
-silting complex
P_{1}\overset{f}{\to}P_{0}
we write
f=\begin{pmatrix}f^{\prime}&0\end{pmatrix}:P_{1}^{\prime}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime\prime}\to P_{0}
where
f^{\prime}
is right minimal. For
d=1
we have the following result.

Theorem 6.5

(
[
2
, Theorem 2.7, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

There are bijections

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{functorially finite}\\
\text{torsion classes in $\m\Lambda$}\end{array}\right\}\mathrel{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\hphantom{$\scriptstyle\mspace{8mu}{\phi}\mspace{8mu}$}\cr\rightarrowfill\cr\vrule height=0.0pt,width=20.44434pt\cr\leftarrowfill\cr\hphantom{$\scriptstyle\mspace{8mu}{\phi^{-1}}\mspace{8mu}$}\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr}}}\limits^{\phi}_{\phi^{-1}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic support $\tau$-tilting}\\
\text{pairs in $\m\Lambda$}\end{array}\right\}\mathrel{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\hphantom{$\scriptstyle\mspace{8mu}{\psi}\mspace{8mu}$}\cr\rightarrowfill\cr\vrule height=0.0pt,width=20.44434pt\cr\leftarrowfill\cr\hphantom{$\scriptstyle\mspace{8mu}{\psi^{-1}}\mspace{8mu}$}\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr}}}\limits^{\psi}_{\psi^{-1}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic $2$-silting}\\
\text{complexes in $\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)$}\end{array}\right\}.

Explicitly, for a functorially finite torsion class
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\m\Lambda
, a basic support
\tau
-tilting pair
(M,P)
in
\m\Lambda
and a basic
2
-silting complex
P=P_{1}\overset{f}{\to}P_{0}
in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
we have

•

\phi(\mathcal{U})=(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})
and
\phi^{-1}(M,P)=\mathrm{Fac}(M)
, and

•

\psi(M,P)=P[1]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-1}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
and
\psi^{-1}(P)=(coker(f),P_{1}^{\prime\prime})
.

When
d>1
, we have the following generalization of Theorem
6.5
from
[
9
]
.

Theorem 6.6
.

[
9
]

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. There exist maps

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{functorially finite}\\
\text{$d$-torsion classes in $\mathcal{C}$}\end{array}\right\}\overset{\phi_{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic maximal $\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}$-rigid}\\
\text{pairs $(M,P)$ in $\mathcal{C}$ with}\\
\text{$\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert$}\end{array}\right\}\supseteq\mathrm{im}(\phi_{d})\overset{\psi_{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic $(d+1)$-silting}\\
\text{complexes in $\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)$}\end{array}\right\}.

Explicitly, for a functorially finite
d
-torsion class
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\m\Lambda
and a basic maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
in
\mathcal{C}
with
\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
we have

•

\phi_{d}
is injective, is in general not surjective when
d>1
, is given by
\phi_{d}(\mathcal{U})=(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})
, and it has a partial inverse given by
(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})\mapsto\mathrm{Fac}(M^{\mathcal{U}})\cap\mathcal{C}
, and

•

\psi_{d}
is defined on
\mathrm{im}(\phi_{d})
, and is given by
\psi_{d}(M,P)=P[d]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-d}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M)
.

Let
M
be any module in a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
. Let
\operatorname{d-tors}(M)
be the smallest
d
-torsion class in
\mathcal{C}
which contains
M
; this is well-defined since
d
-torsion classes in
\mathcal{C}
form a complete lattice with meet given by intersection by
[
10
, Theorem 4.3]
. It follows that we may define a map

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{functorially finite}\\
\text{$d$-torsion classes in $\mathcal{C}$}\end{array}\right\}\overset{\tilde{\phi}_{d}}{\longleftarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic maximal $\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}$-rigid}\\
\text{pairs $(M,P)$ in $\mathcal{C}$ with}\\
\text{$\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert$}\end{array}\right\}

by
\tilde{\phi}_{d}(M,P)=\operatorname{d-tors}(M)
. Since
\mathrm{Fac}(M)\cap\mathcal{C}\subseteq\operatorname{d-tors}(M)
, we conclude that if
(M,P)
is in the image of
\phi_{d}
then

\operatorname{d-tors}(M)=\mathrm{Fac}(M)\cap\mathcal{C},

and so
\tilde{\phi}_{d}
is surjective but not injective.

As far as the map
\psi_{d}
is concerned, we have the following result in our case.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
be a homogeneous Nakayama algebra admitting a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
. Then the map
\psi_{d}
in Theorem
6.6
can be extended to an injective map

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic maximal $\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}$-rigid}\\
\text{pairs $(M,P)$ in $\mathcal{C}$ with}\\
\text{$\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert$}\end{array}\right\}\overset{\psi_{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{basic $(d+1)$-silting}\\
\text{complexes in $\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)$}\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

Follows by Theorem
5.22
.
∎

As we have already mentioned, the bijection
\psi
in Theorem
6.5
can be seen as a motivation for the definition of MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting modules in
[
33
]
, see Definition
5.2
. Based on our results, we have the following description of MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting modules lying in
d
-cluster tilting subcategories in the case
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
.

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
be a homogeneous Nakayama algebra admitting a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
. Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
be a basic module. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

M
is MM-
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting.

(b)

(M,0)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid and
\lvert M\rvert=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
.

Proof.

If (a) holds, then (b) holds for any finite-dimensional algebra by
[
33
, Theorem 3.4]
and
[
33
, Theorem 5.7]
. If (b) holds then (a) follows by Theorem
5.22
.
∎

We illustrate Theorem
6.6
and Theorem
6.7
with an example.

Example 6.9
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda(37,4)
and
d=4
so that
\Lambda
admits a
4
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the
4
-torsion class defined as the additive closure of the encircled modules in the following picture:

Then
\phi_{4}(\mathcal{U})=(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})
where
M^{\mathcal{U}}
is the additive closure of the modules encircled in red while
P^{\mathcal{U}}
is the additive closure of the modules encircled in blue:

We see that
(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})
satisfies
\lvert M^{\mathcal{U}}\rvert+\lvert P^{\mathcal{U}}\rvert=37
. Also we have that
\tilde{\phi}_{4}(M^{\mathcal{U}},P^{\mathcal{U}})=\operatorname{d-tors}(M^{\mathcal{U}})=\mathcal{U}
, as expected. Now consider the summand-maximal
\tau_{4}
-rigid pair
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
where
M^{\prime}
is the additive closure of the modules encircled in red while
P^{\prime}
is the additive closure of the modules encircled in blue:

Then we have that
\lvert M^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert P^{\prime}\rvert=37
again, and also that
\tilde{\phi}_{4}(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})=\operatorname{d-tors}(M^{\prime})=\mathcal{U}
. Hence
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})\not\in\mathrm{im}(\phi_{d})
and we cannot apply Theorem
6.6
. However Theorem
5.22
gives that both

P^{\mathcal{U}}[4]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-4}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M^{\mathcal{U}})\text{ and }P^{\prime}[4]\oplus\sigma_{\geq-4}\mathbf{P}^{\bullet}(M^{\prime})

are
5
-silting complexes in
\mathrm{K}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,\Lambda)
.

6.3.
Mutation of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs

Finally we share some observations on mutations of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. These observations are not on the level of a full description. However, since mutation as a concept has been a driving factor for development within several branches of representation theory, e.g. the generalization from tilting to support
\tau
-tilting, we feel justified in sharing some of them here.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
be a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory. We say that two summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs
(M,P)
and
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
are
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutations of each other
if there exists a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(N,Q)
which is a direct summand of both
(M,P)
and
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
, and
\lvert N\rvert+\lvert Q\rvert+1=\lvert M\rvert+\lvert P\rvert=\lvert M^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert P^{\prime}\rvert
. We also define the
mutation graph of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs
(abbreviated
sm
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph
) of
\Lambda
as the graph having the summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs as nodes, and edges between two nodes if the pairs are
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutations of each other.

When
d=1
the definition of
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation coincides with the definition of mutation for support
\tau
-tilting pairs. In that case we have that when the algebra is
\tau
-tilting finite, the mutation graph of support
\tau
-tilting modules is connected
[
2
, Corollary 2.38]
. We may show the same for
\Lambda(n,l)
and
d>1
as well.

Proposition 6.10
.

Assume that
\Lambda(n,l)
admits a
d
-cluster tilting module
C
for some
d\geq 1
. Then the mutation graph of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs of
\Lambda(n,l)
is connected.

Proof.

We only give a sketch of the proof. Let
(M,P)
be a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair. Equivalently,
(M,P)
is well-configured. One can show that if
(M_{i})
is a full admissible configuration of type (III), then we can mutate the modules in the diagonal
\mathcal{D}_{i}
to the interval
\Xi_{i}
. In other words,
(M,P)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation equivalent to a well configured pair
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
with
M_{j}=M_{j}^{\prime}
for
j\neq i
and
M_{i}^{\prime}=0
. Similarly, if
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
is a full admissible configuration of type different than (III), then we can show that
(M,P)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation equivalent to a well configured pair
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
which is the same as
(M,P)
except for the fact that we have replaced
(M_{i},\ldots,M_{i+k})
with a full admissible configuration of type (III) and adjusted
\Xi(i,i+k)
accordingly.

By repeatedly applying these two steps we show that
(M,P)
is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation equivalent to a summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(P(R^{\prime}),P(B^{\prime}))
for some
R^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
. Necessarily we have that
R^{\prime}=[1,x]
and
B^{\prime}=[x+1,n]
for some
x\in[0,n+1]
. Such a pair can be easily seen to be
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation equivalent to
(0,\Lambda)
. Hence every summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair is
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-mutation equivalent to
(0,\Lambda)
which proves connectedness of the sm
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph.
∎

On the other hand, the case
d>1
has also some important differences as well when compared to the case
d=1
. Recall that if
d=1
then the mutation graph of support
\tau
-tilting modules is not necessarily finite. Currently every known example of a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\m\Lambda
has an additive generator
C
and it is an open question if that is always the case. In particular, there is no known example of an infinite sm
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph for
d>1
.

Another crucial difference concerns the regularity of the sm
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph. Bongartz’ (co)completion guarantees that a support
\tau
-tilting pair can be mutated in
\lvert\Lambda\rvert=n
different ways and so the mutation graphs for
d=1
are
n
-regular. However, for summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-tilting pairs this is not always the case as we can see in the following example.

Example 6.11
.

Consider the algebra
\Lambda(9,3)
which admits a
2
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathcal{C}
with
4
diagonals. From the formula at the end of Remark
3.34
one can calculate that there are
160
summand-maximal
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs in
\mathcal{C}
. These pairs give rise to the sm
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph drawn in Figure
1
1
1

1

This figure can also be found at
https://endresr.github.io/Higher_Tau_Nakayama/resources/CompleteGraph.pdf
in higher resolution
. Observe that in the neighbourhood of
(\Lambda,0)
and
(0,\Lambda)
we get planar subgraphs with nodes of degree either
3
or
4
, which both connects to two subgraphs (A) and (B) which are not planar and have nodes of degrees
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
.

Both (A) and (B) are characterized by having nodes where
(R,B)
is not uniquely determined by the diagonal component. We also note that the nodes of maximal degree are all in (A) and have diagonal partition
\{3\}
of admissible type (III). Equivalently, the nodes of maximal degree are summand-maximal
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs such that
\tau_{2}^{-2}\Lambda
lies in
\mathrm{add}\left(M\right)
. This may suggest that for a general algebra
\Lambda
admitting a
d
-cluster tilting subcategory a strategy for finding summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs with the maximal amount of possible mutations might be to restrict to those having
\tau_{d}^{-r}\Lambda
as rigid summands for some
r
.

Figure 1.
The sm
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph of
\Lambda(9,3)
.

Despite the complexity of the previous example we see that when
\Lambda=\Lambda(n,l)
is
d
-representation finite, that is when
d=\mathrm{gl.dim.}(\Lambda)
, the situation is greatly simplified.

Proposition 6.12
.

Assume that
\Lambda(n,l)
is
d
-representation-finite for some
d>1
. Then the sm
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph of
\Lambda(n,l)
is an extended Dynkin diagram of type
\tilde{A}
with
2n+l-1
vertices. In particular, it is a
2
-regular graph.

Proof.

By
[
38
, Theorem 3]
we have that
\Lambda(n,l)
is
d
-representation-finite if and only if
p=2
. By Remark
3.34
we have that there exist
2n+l-1
summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs. The claim about the shape of the graph follows easily using this and the description of summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs as well-configured
\mathcal{C}
-pairs in Theorem
3.33
; Example
6.13
below is indicative of the general picture.
∎

Example 6.13
.

The sm
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph of the
2
-representation-finite algebra
\Lambda(4,3)
is

Note that
\Lambda(4,3)
in Example
6.13
has
l=3
,
d=2
and
p=2
and
\Lambda(9,3)
in Figure
1
has
l=3
,
d=2
and
p=4
. In other words
\Lambda(9,3)
is the simplest example of
\Lambda(n,l)
where
l>2
and where
\Lambda(n,l)
is not
2
-representation-finite. There is a perhaps surprising jump in complexity between the two graphs. One might therefore ask if
d
-representation finite algebras can always be expected to posses such nice behaviour. With regard to regularity the answer is no, a counterexample is the Auslander algebra of type
\mathbb{A}_{3}
which is
2
-representation finite, but which has nodes of degree
3
and
4
in it’s sm
\tau_{2}
-rigid graph.

We finish this section with a remark about the lattice of
d
-torsion cases when
\Lambda
is
d
-representation-finite.

Remark 6.14
.

Let
\Lambda(n,l)
be
d
-representation-finite. By Remark
3.34
and Remark
4.23
we conclude that there are

2n+l-1-(n+l+1)=n-2

more summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs than
d
-torsion classes. Continuing from Example
6.13
, the lattice of
2
-torsion classes of
\Lambda(4,3)
is

As expected, it has
2
vertices less than the sm
\tau_{2}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid graph. The above picture is indicative of the general situation, where the lattice of
d
-torsion classes can be obtained by removing all summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pairs of the form
(P(R),P(B))
where
R=[1,x]
and
B=[x+1,n]
for some
x\in[2,n-1]
, and replacing each of the other summand-maximal
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
with
\operatorname{d-tors}(M)
.

Appendix A
An illustration for the notation

One aspect of this paper is the reliance on a wide variety of notations, which can be challenging but is necessary for clarity and precision. This is particularly evident in Sections
2.4
and
3
. In an effort to reduce the cognitive load imposed by this fact, we include an illustration which encodes the most widely used notation in the article. Our hope is that it can be used as a place to quickly (re)familiarise oneself with the meaning of the different symbols.

The illustration above should be thought of as 4 consecutive diagonals in the
d
-cluster tilting subcategory of an algebra
\Lambda(n,l)
for
n
and
l
large enough, and for suitable
d
. In the diagonals
\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
we have denoted some modules, to be thought of as direct summands of a
\tau_{d}^{\phantom{-}}
-rigid pair
(M,P)
. These in turn define all the intervals which we have drawn at the top. For a quick way to find the formal definitions of all of this notation we refer to the index at the end of this paper.

As a specific example for verifying some values, we note that if
l=15
,
p=6
,
d=4
(thus
n=121
) and
i=4
, then we obtain the following values:

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&i-2&i-1&i&i+1\\
\hline\cr m_{j}&0&2&6&3\\
n_{j}&0&12&10&4\\
l_{j}&15&11&5&2\\
s_{j}&31&48&78&95\\
\Xi_{j}&\varnothing&[28,58]&[47,78]&[65,98]\end{array}

Notice that increasing
p
(and thus also
n
) has no effect on the numbers in the above table.
